#227. These are the animals people most want to see photographed

Editor’s Note:  Call to Earth is a CNN editorial series committed to reporting on the environmental challenges facing our planet, together with the solutions. Rolex’s Perpetual Planet initiative has partnered with CNN to drive awareness and education around key sustainability issues and to inspire positive action.

Originally a term used by trophy hunters in Africa, the “Big Five” described the animals most challenging to shoot and kill: the lion, elephant, leopard, rhino, and buffalo. Nowadays, it is more loosely used to refer to some of Africa’s largest and most iconic animals.

However, British photographer Graeme Green has reclaimed the narrative, creating a global “New Big Five” for wildlife photography. In 2021, 50,000 people around the world voted for the five animals they would most like to photograph, or see photographed, in the wild. Five animals were crowned the winners: the elephant, polar bear, lion, gorilla and tiger.

This week, sees the publication of “The New Big 5” photography book, which features images of those animals and other at-risk wildlife, from photography legends such as Ami Vitale, Steve McCurry and Paul Nicklen, and essays from famous conservationists and activists including like Jane Goodall and Paula Kahumbu.

Green says that the book celebrates wildlife and is a global call to action on issues impacting wildlife, including habitat loss, poaching, pollution and climate change.

Green was on assignment in Botswana at least a decade ago when he came up with the idea for a project to encourage people to “shoot with a camera, not a gun,” he says.

“I thought this would be a way to get people really focusing on wildlife, thinking about the wildlife they love, thinking about the animals that are at risk.”

Ten photographs that made the world wake up to climate change

In total, the book includes the work of 144 globally renowned wildlife photographers from Ecuador to India. Curating the images took nearly two years of work, says Green.

“I think these are some of the most beautiful and creative images that I’ve seen put together in one book,” says Green. “These are the species that we are at risk of losing.”

According to the UN, nature is declining at an unprecedented rate, with around one million of the planet’s animal and plant species facing extinction. The “New Big 5,” all of which are threatened, act as ambassadors for what is happening in the natural world, says Green.

As well as being a powerful reminder of what we stand to lose, the book also points people towards potential solutions. Featured essays explore the benefits of rewilding and the importance of indigenous communities in conservation.

A chapter on endangered species from bees to blue whales illustrates the alarming threat climate change poses to animals outside of the “New Big 5.” “That’s only the tip of the iceberg – I could have included thousands of pictures because that’s how serious the situation is,” says Green.

Why a wildlife photographer took 40,000 photos to capture these extraordinary birds

Jane Goodall, a leading conservationist who also wrote the afterword to the book, said in a press release that “we have a window of time during which we can start to heal some of the harm we have inflicted on the natural world, but only if we get together and take action now.

“I hope the photos will lead people into the wonderful worlds of these iconic species. Then, perhaps, other people will become involved in helping to create a world where wildlife can flourish for future generations to enjoy,” she said.

“The New Big 5: A Global Photography Project For Endangered Wildlife,” by Graeme Green, published by Earth Aware Editions, is on sale from April 4, 2023.

This story has been updated to reflect the range of issues covered by the book.

#226. Waymo confirms it’s shaking up its Uber relationship in January 2028

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Waymo will end its exclusivity with Uber in the Austin and Atlanta markets starting in January 2028, co-CEO Tekedra Mawakana told Bloomberg News in a report published Wednesday.

The robotaxi company will offer rides directly through its own smartphone app, like it does in other cities. Uber confirmed to TechCrunch Wednesday that it still plans to host Waymo robotaxis on its app for as long as the two companies agree. Waymo did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Mawakana’s comments come after reporting earlier this year by the Financial Times that Waymo was looking to get out of its deal with Uber, which was set to expire in May 2028. Mawakana told Bloomberg that Waymo initially partnered with Uber because the ride-hail giant offered scale. But the experience for riders has been “a little bit of a crapshoot,” she said, especially because riders never know for sure if they’re going to hail a robotaxi or a human-driven car.

“It’s important for us to have that relationship with the rider. We’ve gotten that feedback from the riders in those markets,” Mawakana said.

Uber — which has many robotaxi and other autonomous vehicle partners — told Bloomberg that it was Waymo’s idea not to add an option in the ride-hail giant’s app to book a robotaxi directly.

This story has been updated with new information from Uber, and to correct that Mawakana’s comments were about the exclusivity.

#225. Fox News suffers ratings slump on bleak election night for Republicans

In early, preliminary figures, the news channel trailed several other networks in prime-time viewership

Send us your questions about the midterm results

On a midterm election night that was a huge disappointment for Republicans, with Democrats taking control of the US House and Senate, the country’s most prominent conservative television network felt the brunt of viewer disillusionment with Donald Trump and his party, according to preliminary audience numbers.

The Fox News Channel, which often leads in viewership on election night, appears to have come out far behind the competition. According to preliminary “Fast Nationals” ratings dating from the Nielsen firm, the network averaged 3.882 million total viewers between 8 and 11pm, far behind broadcast networks NBC (6.519 million viewers) and ABC (4.906 million viewers), and even trailing fellow cable networks CNN (3.927 million viewers) and MS Now (3.922 million viewers). Fox News did, however, defeat CBS News, which attracted just 2.629 million viewers.

Because the numbers are preliminary, they are likely to be revised upwards when final audience data is released on Thursday.

Overall, the big six television networks averaged 25.784 million total viewers during prime-time election coverage, up from 22.215 million total viewers in 2022. During those 2022 elections, Fox News averaged 7.422 million viewers.

With Trump reaching historic levels of unpopularity, Fox News has seen viewers tune out in recent months. Last month, the network’s viewership was down 27% compared with the same month last year in total day viewers, though it still handily defeated CNN and MS Now. Lachlan Murdoch, the chief executive of parent company Fox Corp, acknowledged “viewer fatigue” in an earnings call on Monday.

Trump, who criticized Fox throughout election night and blamed the network for his party’s losses, poked fun at the network’s October figures in a Truth Social post, chalking up the decline to the channel’s industry-standard practice of booking Democratic candidates for interviews.

“FoxNews Ratings are way down (27%!) because they’re spending too much time covering the Trump Deranged Dumocrats!” he wrote on Tuesday evening. “All of those people who used to watch Fox, and no longer do, get it. It’s them, not me – My numbers are better than they have ever been.”

#223. DuckDuckGo pitches itself to schools amid backlash over Google’s AI policy

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

DuckDuckGo is pitching itself to schools after Google created an opening for the alternative search engine. On Tuesday, Google told schools they could turn off Gemini, but it said shutting off its other AI features would cost schools Google Search, as well.

On X, DuckDuckGo’s founder and CEO, Gabriel Weinberg, pointed to the company’s online documentation, explaining how schools can switch their default search engine to DuckDuckGo’s service instead, adding, “get in touch for help.”

Meanwhile, DuckDuckGo’s chief public affairs officer, Kamyl Bazbaz, issued a stronger statement, writing, “Forcing AI on school children: how a monopolist behaves when they think people are powerless. Prove Google wrong.”

Forcing AI on school children: how a monopolist behaves when they think people are powerless.Prove Google wrong. https://t.co/Ta6ibmFdb9

In an email to TechCrunch, DuckDuckGo chastised Google for its decision, adding that Google could easily turn off Search’s AI features for schools if it wanted to.

The company’s comments come as the American public is weighing how much AI they want to allow into their lives. As Tuesday night’s elections indicate, they don’t want AI data centers built in their towns. But using AI at work and school has become a common practice and requires a more nuanced discussion.

Knowing that educators have struggled with AI, Google developed technology called “Guided Learning” that is meant to help kids learn by walking through problem-solving steps rather than just providing them with an answer. But educators are still asking for tools that let them remove AI from the learning process altogether.

After the backlash on Tuesday, Google defended its decision, saying it was just responding to schools’ feedback. It said that Gemini, Gemini Notebook, and Search would be off by default, and schools could choose to enable them individually.

We want to clarify what we announced today about our education services. Today’s announcements give schools more choice, not less. Here’s what we announced:– Gemini, Gemini Notebook, and Search will be OFF 🔴 by default. This is based on K-12 schools asking for more control…

The problem, of course, is that Google Search now heavily uses AI, through AI Overviews and a conversational AI Mode that answers users’ queries.

DuckDuckGo isn’t anti-AI, to be clear — it offers its own set of AI features, including a chat interface called Duck.ai and an AI search assistant.

However, unlike Google, it provides options to disable these features in both its search and browser settings, the company says. It also offers a dedicated website, noai.duckduckgo.com, where AI-assisted answers, AI-generated images, and AI feature suggestions are all turned off.

That flexibility may be driving demand for its services, as the company reported earlier this year that its app installs were up 30% as users rejected being force-fed Google’s AI Search.

#222. Giving Marjorie Taylor Greene a platform isn't good for America

Editor’s Note:  Dean Obeidallah, a former attorney, is the host of SiriusXM radio’s daily program “The Dean Obeidallah Show.” Follow him @DeanObeidallah@masto.ai. The opinions expressed in this commentary are his own. View more opinion on CNN.

Last year, GOP Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene of Georgia spoke at a white nationalist event organized by Holocaust denier Nick Fuentes that caused Republican leaders to denounce her.

Last week, Greene’s Twitter account was temporarily suspended by the Elon Musk-headed platform over a tweet with a graphic referring to a “Trans Day of Vengeance,” as she denounced a planned transgender rights rally.

And come Tuesday, Greene has announced plans to protest in New York City when former President Donald Trump is expected to be arraigned on an indictment of more than 30 counts, calling the proceedings against him an “unconstitutional WITCH HUNT!”

But on Sunday, Greene was featured on CBS’ “60 Minutes” in an interview the long-running show promoted on Twitter with the tease: “Republican Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene, nicknamed MTG, isn’t afraid to share her opinions, no matter how intense and in-your-face they are. She sits down with Lesley Stahl this Sunday on 60 Minutes.” The images attached to this tweet by “60 Minutes” include Greene and Stahl walking through the US Capitol, taking a stroll outside and Greene showing Stahl something on her phone.

It's time for Marjorie Taylor Greene to go

In the segment that aired Sunday night, Stahl noted the congresswoman had moved from the fringe to the GOP’s front row in two years despite a “sharp tongue” and “some pretty radical views” as well as “over the top” comments such as “the Democrats are a party of pedophiles.” Stahl also referred to video of Greene chasing a Parkland, Florida, school shooting survivor, still maintaining that the 2020 election was stolen and failing to criticize Trump over spending. (The interview was conducted before news of his indictment.)

But Stahl didn’t mention Greene spoke at a white nationalist event a year ago while a member of Congress or her extreme anti-Muslim views and her defense of January 6 rioters.

Criticism of CBS for amplifying Greene has been swift and well-deserved even before the program aired. Former GOP Rep. Adam Kinzinger of Illinois tweeted beforehand: “Wow. Insane that 60 min would do this.” (Kinzinger is a CNN senior political commentator.)

Journalist Molly Jong-Fast also slammed “60 Minutes” with the tweet: “Attention is currency and 60 minutes is spending its currency on the Jewish space lasers woman.” (Jong-Fast was apparently referring to Greene’s past claim that a massive California wildfire was started by “a laser” beamed from space controlled by a prominent Jewish banking family.)

David Hogg, who survived the 2018 horrific school shooting at Marjory Stoneman Douglas High School in Parkland and has since become an activist against gun violence, responded, “I look forward to your questions about why she thinks school shootings are fake and why she’s supported QAnon.”

Greene amplified the conspiracy theory — two years before being elected to Congress — that the Parkland shooting that took 17 lives was staged. And in 2019, Greene appeared on video confronting Hogg outside the Capitol when he was championing laws to save lives from gun violence, screaming that the then-teenager was a “coward.” She also called Hogg “#littleHitler” on social media.

Greene’s own tweet promoting Sunday’s segment was not filled with her typical smears of “fake news” when she doesn’t like the coverage. Rather, Greene urged people to tune in as she praised “60 Minutes” host Stahl (and misspelled her first name): “It was an honor to spend a few days with the legendary icon Leslie Stahl and talented crew @60Minutes.” Greene added, “Leslie is a trailblazer for women in journalism. And while we may disagree on some issues, I respect her greatly.”

Now, it is true that “60 Minutes” over its 50-plus-year history has featured what the show has dubbed “controversial” guests. These include a 2000 interview with Oklahoma City bomber Timothy McVeigh, whose 1995 terrorist attack left 168 dead, including 19 children. And the show did a 1979 interview with Iran’s Ayatollah Ruhollah Khomeini.

Perhaps “60 Minutes” views Greene — who has repeatedly downplayed and even defended the January 6, 2021, attack plus called for the GOP to become the party of “Christian nationalism” — in the vein of its long list of controversial guests. (CBS had not responded to a request from CNN for comment at time of publishing.)

However, the choice of Greene as a guest instantly recalls the comments of then-CBS CEO Les Moonves during Trump’s 2016 presidential campaign when he admitted that Trump’s candidacy “may not be good for America, but it’s damn good for CBS.” Moonves added, “The money’s rolling in and this is fun. … (T)his is going to be a very good year for us,” concluding, “Sorry. It’s a terrible thing to say. But bring it on, Donald. Keep going.”

Moonves may be gone from CBS, but it appears his mindset continues at “60 Minutes.” And that may be good for “60 Minutes,” but it’s definitely not good for America.

#221. Trump’s wealth dented after Democrats trounce Republicans in midterms

Trump Media stock and WLFI coin saw declines but he is still $2.7bn wealthier than when he won election in 2024

Send us your questions about the midterm results

Donald Trump’s wealth is falling along with his political fortunes.

Tradable investment assets that helped make the president more than $2bn during his first year in office are collapsing in value the day after the Democrats trounced the Republicans in the midterm elections. Trump Media and Technology Group stock, which trades on the Nasdaq exchange, was priced at just over $5 on Wednesday morning – its lowest mark ever, and down from an all-time high of $101. It lost 11% overnight, dropping from $5.90 once the election returns started coming in. Trump has reported he owns 114m shares of DJT.

Trump owns over 40% of the stock through a trust. He has still grown his wealth dramatically during his presidency; Forbes estimated in a September analysis that he is $2.7bn wealthier than when he won the White House in 2024.

Another major asset for Trump and his family are World Liberty Financial digital tokens. The digital coins are trading at less than 5 cents after the election. At their height, in 2025, they were valued at six times that, at 31 cents. The WLFI coin, the governance token of World Liberty financial, began sliding in late October as the election drew near, and as a hack of Binance rattled crypto markets. It experienced a steep decline around 7pm ET on Tuesday as polls in some states closed.

Trump already made more than $500m in profit selling crypto assets last year, according to his personal financial disclosures, though he still owns 15bn tokens.

World Liberty is a venture created by the Trump family and the family of Steven Witkoff. Trump appointed Witkoff, a personal friend and real estate developer, to negotiate diplomatic arrangements about Gaza, Iran and Ukraine.

The value of both WLIF and DJT has always been pegged to the way Trump is perceived, said Jim Angel, an associate professor at Georgetown University’s McDonough School of Business.

“People think they’re investing in Trump. They really aren’t. They’re really betting on his popularity. That’s what they’re betting on.

“Right now his popularity is at record lows,” Angel added, “and so we see the price of DJT and World Liberty at record lows.”

Separate digital novelty coins called $Trump, which made the president $636m in sales in 2025, have also collapsed in value. They are priced at about $1.40 and seem to have lost about 10% of their value overnight as election results came in. They launched shortly before his inauguration and briefly traded as high as $45 in January 2025.

World Liberty Financial, the Trump and Witkoff crypto company, also issues a so-called stablecoin, USDI1, and the Trump administration recently gave the company a banking license. The stablecoins are pegged to the US dollar and do not fluctuate dramatically.

#220. Hungary opens communist-era secret police files identifying informants

The archives name thousands of former informants who spied on citizens for the state security services during decades of one-party rule. The prime minister said "many families will now face difficult information."

Hungary released thousands of communist-era secret police records to the public on Wednesday as part of the country's long-delayed effort to come to terms with its repressive past.

The archives, which are now part of a searchable online database, include the names of thousands of former informants who worked for the state security apparatus during four decades of one-party rule.

Although communism ended in Hungary in 1989, attempts to open the files had been repeatedly stalled. Some critics blamed the delay on successive governments' reluctance to implicate public figures.

Center-right Prime Minister Peter Magyar, who unseated longtime populist leader Viktor Orban in April, had pledged to grant the public unrestricted access to the records.

"After 36 years, we are finally fulfilling what was once one of the key promises of the 1990 regime change — a promise that was later denied and lied about," Magyar told a media briefing at the state security archives on Wednesday.

He said ​some 60,000 ‌names had been made public in the new online database. A second batch of files is expected to be made available by February.

"I ask ‌everyone ​to treat this long overdue publication of documents with dignity and humanity ... as many families will now face difficult information often related to a deceased member of their family," Magyar said. "​It is impossible to process a trauma without discussion and asking questions."

Hungary was under one-party communist rule from 1949 to 1989.

As was the case in a number of Eastern European countries during the Cold War, secret police in Hungary relied on a vast network of informants to monitor citizens. These informants were often coerced into reporting on colleagues, friends and even relatives.

According to estimates from the state security archives, authorities recruited some 150,000 informants, although records for about two-thirds of them no longer exist.

Many files were destroyed in 1956 during Hungary's anti-Soviet uprising, or hidden during the transition to democracy. Some are believed to have been taken to Moscow.

Historian Laszlo Eorsi from the 1956 Institute Foundation told the AFP news agency that the move to make the files public was "better late than never."

"We are getting access to much more information than before about who the authorities have recruited and how the security apparatus worked back then," he said.

He predicted the release would trigger public and private revelations, saying it "could cause some hysteria in the coming months."

Ahead of the publication, rumors had circulated that Former Prime Minister Orban was an informant, but his name did not appear in the digital database on Wednesday.

Orban said on Tuesday that communist secret services had tried to recruit him while he was doing his military service, but he refused.

#216. Trump boasts about ‘magnificent’ ballroom day after Republican rout

US president rhapsodized about ballroom features for more than 20 minutes, with no mention of Democrats’ ‘blue wave’

Send us your questions about the midterm results

Less than 24 hours after suffering historic party losses in the US midterm elections, Donald Trump gathered reporters and network TV cameras at the White House to congratulate his team.

“The whole thing is a miracle, and it’s all because of you,” the US president said into a microphone on Wednesday afternoon.

He was not referring to the blue wave that swept the country as Democrats retook control of the US Congress, but his controversial White House ballroom.

Standing on the construction site on Wednesday, the US president boasted about the ballroom’s “magnificent” columns carved out of limestone, 50ft-high ceilings, ability to hold more than 300 guests, heavy-set beams, “drone-proof” roof, thick marble floors and military-grade bunker.

He went into great detail about the construction of the ballroom, seemingly teasing the fact that it would open soon. He pivoted to discuss his love for construction projects, and his other controversial efforts to beautify Washington DC.

For more than 20 minutes, there was no mention of the elephant in the room: the fact that dozens of Trump-endorsed candidates in competitive races for the Senate and House of Representatives nationwide lost their elections to Democrats on Tuesday.

When Doug Burgum, the secretary of the interior, took the microphone, he declared Wednesday to be “a day of celebration and a day of gratitude”.

Only when the US president finally took questions from reporters did he address his party’s dismal performance in the midterms, seemingly unfazed that Democrats outperformed expectations.

“Look, I’ll get along probably with the Democrats, unless they want to be destructive, but I have the power of the veto,” Trump said. He admitted it was “largely true” that the war with Iran played a role in helping Democrats, because it drove up fuel prices, but insisted the conflict was worth it.

Democrats took back the House and the Senate and won in several gubernatorial races. Senate candidates in historically red states, such as South Carolina, Texas, Kansas and North Carolina, defeated Republicans.

Even in districts gerrymandered over the last year to assist Republicans, at the behest of Trump, Democrats managed to retain control.

Trump, who spent months urging his supporters to pretend he was on the ballot, swiftly pointed out that he was not actually on the ballot as the scale of Tuesday’s Republican losses became clear. But this election was widely seen as a referendum on the president, and his agenda.

His decision to appear on Wednesday at the site of his planned ballroom mirrored an extraordinary decision to deliver his final address of the campaign at Washington’s Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool, describing – in detail – changes to its pipes, the grass surrounding the area, and granite.

For months, Trump has pulled focus to his various construction projects across the capital, including the ballroom, the refurbished Reflecting Pool, a planned triumphal arch, the cleaning of statues and monuments and a new White House helipad.

Meanwhile, Americans have grown increasingly frustrated with Trump’s performance as president – blaming his economic policies and war with Iran for increasing the cost of living.

But Trump continued to claim on Wednesday that voters were not rejecting him by voting for Democrats. “Look, if I were on the ballot we would’ve won,” Trump asserted to reporters.

#215. Hackers stole DreamHost customer files and inboxes during months-long attack

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Web host DreamHost has confirmed that hackers stole customer emails and credentials during a months-long cyberattack on its systems. DreamHost disclosed the breach last Thursday after it sparked an outage that spanned several days, during which it reset some customer passwords.

In a data breach notice filed with California’s attorney general, the web host said the attack began in August and allowed hackers to obtain session tokens, which keep users logged in after entering their passwords. If stolen, hackers can break into a system as if they were that user. The company said hackers exploited an unspecified vulnerability in DreamHost’s file manager, which customers use to store their files.

When reached by TechCrunch, a DreamHost spokesperson declined to say why the company did not detect the cyberattack until last week, citing an ongoing investigation. The spokesperson did not say what the vulnerability is. The company said it was unrelated to the vulnerabilities recently discovered in the PHP web software.

DreamHost’s spokesperson said that the number of affected customers is “well under 10%” but would not provide a more accurate figure. The web host touts some 400,000 paying customers across its network.

#214. What OPEC's surprise oil cut means for gas prices

OPEC and its allies’ surprise move to slash oil production will soon be felt at US gas pumps.

The group known as OPEC+ announced Sunday it would cut oil production by more than 1.6 million barrels a day starting in May, running through the end of the year. The news sent both Brent crude futures, the global oil benchmark, and WTI, the US benchmark, up about 6% in trading Monday.

The production cut announcement also had an immediate impact on gasoline futures, which will be passed onto US drivers much more quickly than the spike in oil prices. RBOB, the most closely watched wholesale gasoline price, was up about 8 cents a gallon, or about 3%, in morning trading.

“I think OPEC is reawakening the inflation monster,” said Tom Kloza, global head of energy analysis for OPIS, which tracks gas prices for AAA. “The White House has to be shocked and major-time pissed. It certainly alters the calculus for a while.”

The national average for US gas prices stood at $3.51, on Monday, according to AAA. Kloza said he could see it getting up to $3.80 to $3.90 in relatively short order thanks to the move by OPEC.

“We’re not going to get back to $5 a gallon. I don’t think we’re even going as high as $4,” he said. But he said by the end of the summer US drivers could be back above year-earlier prices, especially if there is a hurricane or other storms affecting production along the Gulf Coast.

The average US regular gas price a year ago stood at $4.19 a gallon in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and the disruption that caused to world’s energy markets. Prices eventually reached a record $5.02 a gallon on June 14, before starting a slow but steady decline over the course of more than three months during which the average price fell every day. The decline was partly driven by the release of oil from the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve, and partly by concerns that there could be a US or global recession that reduced the demand for gasoline.

Oil prices surge after OPEC+ producers announce surprise cuts

Even at $3.51, US gas prices were just below the $3.53 average on Feb. 23, 2022, the day before Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

Kloza said one thing keeping prices from getting anywhere near the record levels of 2022 is that the US plans additional releases from the SPR, and US oil production and refining capacity are both up. But a cut of 1 million barrels a day of oil by OPEC+ will not be easy to make up.

“They have ability to cut production and they seem motivated to do so,” he said.

#212. Trump says 'largely true' Iran war led to Republicans midterm loss

After Democrats won both the House and Senate in a blowout for Republicans, President Trump admitted fuel prices brought on by the Iran war impacted the outcome, but stuck by his decision.

Follow the latest developments from the US midterm elections:

President Donald Trump's Republican Party lost races even in states it had held for decades.

For Democrats, the results surpassed even the most optimistic expectations. They now have control of Congress.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Relief in Europe, but also a warning not to celebrate too soon. Donald Trump has lost both chambers of Congress, yet he remains in charge of US foreign policy and retains significant power over trade.

DW News looks at the reaction from Europe and the markets, and ask whether a weakened Trump might become more predictable, or more confrontational.

Donald Trump spoke about the outcome of the midterm elections to reporters at the construction site of the White House ballroom.

He hailed the ballroom, which is being built after the East Wing of the White House was demolished, as a "structure the likes of which has never been seen anywhere."

During the remarks, Trump was asked about Vice President JD Vance's post which blamed the Iran war for the midterm losses to the Democrats.

"I think it's largely true because it drove the gasoline up a little bit short term," Trump said. "But at the same time, if I had to do it again, I'd have to do it again. Because long term, it's the only answer. You can't let Iran have a nuclear weapon."

The president also ruled out firing Pentagon chief Pete Hegseth, who has guided efforts on the Iran war, saying "I think he's doing a very good job."

In regard to the Democrats, Trump said he was willing to work with the opposition. However, he warned the Democrats against impeachment.

"I think it would destroy the Democrat Party if they did that," the president said, while arguing there is "no reason" for him to be impeached.

Trump was impeached by the House two times during his first term, but was never convicted and removed by the Senate.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz said he believes Trump will continue to bypass the US House and Senate in his last two years by issuing executive orders.

"Trump has, for the past two years, largely governed without consulting Congress. Why should things be any different now?" Merz said during an economic forum of the center-left Social Democratic Party (SPD). Merz's center-right Christian Democrats are in a governing coalition with the SPD.

The German chancellor said he wasn't so "excited" about the Democratic victory in the midterms.

"What will change is budget planning — and of course planning for the armed forces as well," Merz continued.

The US Constitution grants the Congress the power of the purse. Congress can also influence military factors such as troop levels in Europe.

US Vice President JD Vance, in a long post on X, said many voters who backed Trump in the 2024 presidential election showed up to the polls and cast ballots for the Democrats out of anger.

"The reason we lost is the war," Vance said, referring to the US-Israel war on Iran.

"If you'd like to argue it was worth it, that losing the Senate for the next 6 years was worth it, then do so," Vance said. "Argue it was a worthwhile cost to pay. Agree or disagree, that's at least honest."

"But everyone knows why we lost. And any honest conversation about where we go from here must start here," the vice president said, while saying he remains hopeful "about our country."

Mike Johnson, a representative from Louisiana who currently serves as US Speaker of the House, is unlikely to seek the position of House minority leader when Democrats take over in January, AP and Reuters reported.

Johnson, a close Trump ally, has served as speaker since October 2023.

Jake Sherman, the founder of US congressional outlet Punchbowl News, reported that Jim Jordan, a Republican congressman from Ohio, is expected to announce his candidacy for House minority leader later on Wednesday. Jordan just won reelection in Ohio's 4th Congressional district.

Shares of SpaceX and Tesla, companies run by tech billionaire and world's richest man Elon Musk, were both down about 5%, as US markets opened on Wednesday.

Musk is a Republican megadonor and had spent nearly $500 million to back Republican candidates in the midterms, including efforts to keep Republicans in power in Texas, a state home to both the automaker Tesla and rocket-to-AI company SpaceX, as well as being a hub to AI data centers.

But with Democrats seizing both houses of Congress and Gina Hinojosa defeating Greg Abbott in the Texas governor's race, Musk and his companies could face increasing scrutiny by Congress as well as Texas state authorities.

Observers expect Democrats to tighten regulation of data-center ​projects and push for ‌construction moratoriums as local opposition grows over surging electricity costs.

EU High Representative for Foreign Affairs and Security Policy Kaja Kallas said she hopes the new Democratic-majority Congress will continue support for Ukraine and pressure Russia. The next US House and Senate will be sworn in on January 3 next year.

"US voters have made their choice clear," Kallas said.

Previous Democratic President Joe Biden was a keen supporter of Ukraine when the the Russian invasion began in February 2022. US President Donald Trump, a Republican, has since pressured Ukraine into giving up land to Russia since his term began last year and has threatened to stop selling arms to the Ukrainians.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The Democratic House and Senate will likely put a check on Trump's foreign policy toward Ukraine and Russia, with stronger congressional oversight expected. However, Trump is unlikely to make a major U-turn on foreign issues during his last two years in office and leads the US military as commander-in-chief.

After sitting out the counting and results phase through the night, the White House press office made its first post-election foray on social media with what it termed a public service announcement.

"PSA: Trump is still your president," said a caption in block capitals beneath a photo of Trump staring intently at the camera shared online.

The post was captioned with what tends to be the 47th president's catchphrase signoff on his personal account on his own social media site Truth Social: "Thank you for your attention to this matter."

Trump and senior members of his Cabinet were yet to wade back into the public debate as the clocks ticked past 9:30 a.m. on the East Coast.

Stock markets around the world were mostly in the green on Wednesday as traders digested the midterm election results.

As of around 3 p.m. on the trading floor in Frankfurt, the DAX had gained just under 2% since the start of trade.

This was broadly in line with the major indices in London, with the FTSE 100 up 1.94%, and Paris, with the CAC 40 up 2.19%. Spain's IBEX 35 was even more buoyant, up by more than 3%.

The only major Asian market to take a hit was Japan's Nikkei, closing down 1.42% on the start of trade. Hong Kong's Hang Seng rose by 1.67% and Shanghai's main index climbed by more than 2%. India's Sensex logged more modest gains of around 0.5%.

Even the US markets were expected to open in a bullish mood on Wednesday morning, despite some isolated stocks like Elon Musk's Tesla and SpaceX and a host of AI companies taking a moderate hit on election evening.

Many people in the US, and particularly beyond its borders, might be asking what the Democrats controlling Congress might mean for US foreign policy, not least the long-running Russian invasion of Ukraine and Trump's much more recent war on Iran.

Richard Walker from DW Geopolitics breaks down some of the battle lines, if you'll pardon the pun, between the Trump White House, the Democratic Party and the major foreign players when it comes to these issues.

If you're the read-only, low-data type who'd rather not click play on even the shortest of videos, the super-abridged version goes something like this: On most matters of foreign policy, it's the White House and not Congress that holds sway.

"These conflicts are stuck for a reason," as Richard puts it, and there's every chance that will remain the case at least in the short term. Not least because powerful actors beyond the US' borders remain a core part of both equations.

UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham told journalists that the midterm results in the US show there needs to be an international focus on the cost of living crisis.

Burnham, a member of the center-left Labour Party, has been in office since July and has vowed to tackle rising costs.

"President Trump has accepted the results, and I feel the world has an opportunity to unite around an issue that is affecting us all, and that's the cost of living," Burnham said.

Burnham was asked how the US midterm result relates to recent losses for right-wing parties in Brazil, Serbia and Hungary.

"I think people want to see the world coming together and focusing on the things that matter to people," Burnham said.

The UK leader said that the US war with Iran needs to deescalate. He said that without a settlement to resolve the Iran conflict, "I can't get people's cost of living pressures down."

German Foreign Minister Johann Wadephul believes the outcome of the US midterm elections could create new momentum for supporting Ukraine in its fight against Russia's military aggression.

"As far as Ukraine is concerned, I am somewhat confident that there will now be more discussions in Congress — including between both parties — about what can be done together to support Ukraine," Wadephul said in Berlin.

"I believe that people there held back for a certain amount of time during the election campaign, and I know that there are strong supporters of Ukraine in both houses and in both parties."

Wadephul stressed that foreign policy played a key role in the elections.

On US-Germany relations, he noted that bilateral cooperation could become broader and "perhaps more interesting in certain areas."

Finance Minister and Vice Chancellor Lars Klingbeil also congratulated Democrats for what he called a "landslide victory."

"That certainly inspires confidence," he said.

For a long time, he noted, the trend had been toward right-wing populism, but now the moderate center has prevailed.

While most foreign policy decisions ultimately rest with the executive branch, the outcome of the US midterms will have major repercussions for adversaries such as Iran, as well as countries like Ukraine, which rely on support from the United States.

#211. California progressive Randy Villegas ousts longtime Republican in redrawn House district

Community college professor unseats incumbent Republican David Valadao in seat redrawn under Prop 50

Send us your questions about the midterm results

Democrat Randy Villegas, a community college professor with notable progressive endorsements, has won the race for California’s 22nd congressional district in an contest that shaped up to be one of the toughest political battles in the state.

He unseated the six-term, incumbent Republican congressman, David Valadao, in a district that was considered the only true toss-up across the state of California after being redrawn under Proposition 50, a voter-approved ballot initiative that remade the state’s voting maps last year.

Villegas called it a victory for “hundreds of volunteers, tens of thousands of grassroots donors, and working families who believed we could build something better together”.

“With today’s election results, the Central Valley proved what’s possible when working people come together and refuse to let billionaires, Washington, and special interests decide our future,” Villegas said in a statement on Tuesday night. “The people of the Valley made their voices heard loud and clear: the Central Valley is not for sale.”

The Associated Press called the race of Villegas on Wednesday morning.

Valadao and Villegas are both Central valley natives from immigrant families with working class or agricultural ties, roots that reflect many of the constituents in California’s 22nd district, a majority-Latino district that stretches from Bakersfield to just west of Fresno.

But similarities between the two seemingly end there. They took starkly different approaches to winning in what is one of the country’s poorest congressional districts, and was, this year, home to the state’s most competitive election.

Residents said healthcare and rising costs were top of mind. The district has the second-highest rate and number of Medicaid enrollees of any congressional district, per KFF. The impact constituents are feeling from cuts to the federal health insurance program, passed last year via the One Big Beautiful Bill Act, was one of Villegas’s main talking points against Valadao, who cast one of the bill’s decisive votes.

Corporate financing was another big factor in this election. Valadao ended with $1.7m in cash on hand, per federal finance reports. OpenSecrets reported he has received over twice as much money from Pacs than the average House candidate.

Villegas, on the other hand, vowed not to take corporate campaign donations, running a platform that repeatedly emphasized the power of the working class. That earned him the endorsement of leading progressive figures including Bernie Sanders, Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez and Dolores Huerta.

His victory comes amid a historic night of wins for Democrats across the US, including in longtime Republican strongholds. Democrats took control of both the House and Senate as a larger than expected blue wave resulted in significant losses for Donald Trump and his party. Democrats also flipped several congressional seats in California with help from Proposition 50.

In a statement after the race was officially called, Villegas said: “They say it takes a village, we know it takes a Valley.”

#210. Meta acquires AI coding startup Conductor

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Meta has acquired Conductor, an AI coding startup that allows users to spin up software projects by deploying multiple agents into parallel workspaces.

The news was originally reported by Axios, and Conductor shared news of its acquisition on its blog.

“I have big news: Conductor is joining Meta,” wrote the startup’s founder and CEO, Charlie Holtz, on Wednesday. “We have some big, crazy plans for the next interface,” he added, teasing that the company would have “something new” for customers to use by “early next year.”

On X, Holtz also noted that he was looking forward to working with Alexandr Wang, Meta’s chief AI officer, and Nat Friedman, head of product at the company’s Superintelligence Labs. “They’ve been on an absolute tear, and I’m convinced MSL has the highest talent density of all the labs,” he added.

Meta confirmed the acquisition to TechCrunch but wouldn’t comment further or provide additional details about the deal.

Conductor, founded in 2024, only launched its core product just last summer. The company’s agentic teams, which can be deployed in the cloud, locally, or via a user’s phone, are designed to help software engineers work on separate, compartmentalized parts of a project in concert. The agents that the platform deploys aren’t developed in-house but come from major labs like Anthropic and OpenAI.

Holtz said that, until early next year, Conductor’s core features would continue to work as they previously have. The exception is Conductor’s cloud service, which will shut down on November 20, after which the company will begin deleting user cloud data.

Meta, which previously lagged behind in AI services, has spent the last several months trying to catch up. In August, the company launched Muse Code, its own coding agent. Meanwhile, the company’s recently launched Muse agents are seeing significant adoption, with the company recently reporting that the service had surpassed 10 million users. The company has also made a number of AI-related acquisitions over the past year, including Moltbook, the viral agent platform that made headlines.

#209. Scientists film deepest ever fish on seabed off Japan

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Cruising at a depth of 8,336 meters (over 27,000 feet) just above the seabed, a young snailfish has become the deepest fish ever filmed by scientists during a probe into the abyss of the northern Pacific Ocean.

Scientists from University of Western Australia and Tokyo University of Marine Science and Technology released footage of the snailfish on Sunday filmed last September by sea robots in deep trenches off Japan.

Along with the filming the deepest snailfish, the scientists physically caught two other specimens at 8,022 meters and set another record for the deepest catch.

Previously, the deepest snailfish ever spotted was at 7,703 meters in 2008, while scientists had never been able to collect fish from anywhere below 8,000 meters.

“What is significant is that it shows how far a particular type of fish will descend in the ocean,” said marine biologist Alan Jamieson, founder of the Minderoo-UWA Deep Sea Research Centre, who led the expedition.

Scientists are filming in the trenches off Japan as part of a 10-year study into the deepest fish populations in the world. Snailfish are members of Liparidae family, and while most snailfish live in shallow water, others survive at some of the greatest depths ever recorded, Jamieson said.

During the two-month survey last year, three “landers” – automatic sea robots fitted with high-resolution cameras – were dropped into three trenches – the Japan, Izu-Ogasawara and Ryukyu trenches – at varying depths.

In the Izu-Ogasawara trench, footage showed the deepest snailfish hovering calmly alongside other crustaceans on the seabed.

Jamieson classified the fish as a juvenile and said younger deep sea snailfish often stay as deep as possible to avoid being eaten by bigger predators that swim at shallower depths.

Another clip shot at between 7,500 and 8,200 meters in the same trench showed a colony of fish and crustaceans munching at bait tied to an undersea robot.

Images of the two captured snailfish – identified as Pseudoliparis belyaevi – provide a rare glimpse of the unique features that help the deep sea species survive the extreme environment.

They have tiny eyes, a translucent body, and their lack of swim bladder, which helps other fish float, works to their advantage, Jamieson said.

The professor said the Pacific Ocean is particularly conducive to vibrant activity due to its warm southern current, which encourages sea creatures to go deeper, while its abundant marine life provides a good source of food for bottom feeders.

Scientists would like to know more about creatures living at extreme depths, but cost is the constraint, Jamieson said, adding that each lander alone costs them $200,000 to assemble and operate.

“The challenges are that technology has been expensive and scientists don’t have a lot of money,” he said.

#207. OpenAI is already trying to shape the 2028 election

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

OpenAI’s chief global affairs officer Chris Lehane thinks the 2028 presidential election could be a defining moment for AI. And he wants politicians to start developing policies that address Americans’ concerns about the technology, rather than simply paying lip service to anti-AI sentiment and the backlash against data centers.

In a new essay, Lehane compares AI’s potential to reshape the economy to President Franklin D. Roosevelt’s New Deal, the 1930s set of federal programs and reforms enacted after industrialization and the Great Depression shattered the old economic order. He observes that Americans today feel similarly anxious about losing control over their economic futures and urges politicians to find a “compelling third path” that allows people to share in AI’s benefits. He lays out five policy priorities, spanning worker protections and education to AI safety and data center development.

The essay comes on the heels of a stunning Democratic victory in the midterm elections, in which Democrats flipped control of Congress amid widespread voter frustration over the cost of living. Kate Brennan, who leads AI and corporate power policy at the AI Now Institute, told TechCrunch that Lehane’s rhetoric marks a notable shift from OpenAI’s policy agenda last year, which focused on accelerating AI development, reducing regulatory barriers, and expanding infrastructure.

Unsurprisingly, many of Lehane’s proposals would encourage wider AI adoption and support the infrastructure expansion companies like OpenAI need to grow. The original New Deal established protections for workers and placed constraints on corporate power. It’s less clear how Lehane’s vision would hold AI companies accountable for the economic disruption their technology could create.

“Invoking a New Deal metaphor is to invoke state capacity to actually benefit the public,” Brennan said. “This is written by a private company that is trying to advocate for policies for its own benefit. It is ahistorical at best and harmful at worst.”

While AI wasn’t a dominant issue in the midterms, opposition to data centers emerged as a major bipartisan concern in several races in the midterms. And Lehane may be right that AI will become a bigger part of the conversation over the next two years.

Affordability is already shaping up as a key issue for the 2028 election as Americans grapple with inflation, tariffs, and the rising cost of living. AI could become an increasingly large part of that conversation as voters stress about data centers hiking up electricity bills, AI replacing their jobs, and technology’s economic gains flowing only to a handful of wealthy companies.

Lehane acknowledges many of these concerns, but he offers few concrete proposals to prevent AI’s gains from becoming concentrated. Instead, he largely pitches AI as a path to greater prosperity for Americans willing to embrace it.

Take his proposals for the tax code. He suggests incentives for workers to learn AI skills, easier capital access for entrepreneurs, and new approaches to employee ownership and profit-sharing. Those are not the same as binding requirements that would ensure workers share in AI-generated wealth or that companies pay the costs of displacing workers.

“I was struck by how much of this policy is for a future…that is already predicated on mass AI adoption and AI benefiting society, which we have yet to see,” Brennan said. “All of it is predicated on benefiting from AI’s upside.”

Brennan said it would make more sense to see things like a revised corporate tax rate or tax incentives for hiring human beings rather than automating jobs, as well as ways to keep companies from amassing outsize market power.

Lehane’s emphasis on expanding AI’s economic opportunities, rather than limiting potential harms, extends to data centers. He notes concerns about data centers while emphasizing the construction jobs and local tax revenue they could generate. He then warns that politicians who support a data center moratorium could jeopardize America’s lead over China, going so far as to compare that stance to the 2002 vote to authorize the Iraq war.

Lehane also makes child safety and education central to his proposed agenda, stressing the importance of age-appropriate protections and safeguards around AI companions for minors. He also warns against keeping AI out of classrooms, arguing that doing so could lead to inequality between wealthy students and “children in rural communities.” But the argument takes for granted that AI improves learning, a claim that remains unproven and one critics say deserves more examination than more funding or individualized attention from human teachers.

Lehane also calls for enforceable AI safety standards, independent evaluations, public oversight, and the ability to restrict AI systems or use a kill switch to shut them down — though recent studies show that OpenAI and other AI labs haven’t published comprehensive plans for what they would do if a model escaped human control.

Brennan called these stances “disingenuous,” pointing to OpenAI’s history of opposing legislation designed to impose such measures. In 2024, for example, OpenAI opposed California’s AI safety bill, SB 1047, which would have mandated catastrophic risk evaluations and shutdown capabilities. Last year, OpenAI also sought to limit state regulation, arguing that states could undermine American innovation. The company has more recently shifted some of those positions, backing several state safety laws and saying it would be open to independent evaluators embedded in the company.

Lehane also argues that people should maintain control over consequential decisions made using AI, rather than handing off judgment entirely to machines. But that raises the question of what human control really looks like in a society where humans are increasingly being required to use AI in education, work, healthcare, and government.

Throughout his essay, Lehane frames AI as a technology that could give Americans back their sense of agency and economic opportunity. But he ties average Americans’ “middle class” success to participation in an economy increasingly shaped by companies like OpenAI. Whether his policies will meaningfully redistribute power or subtly entrench it is less clear.

TechCrunch reached out to OpenAI for more specifics on Lehane’s proposals and the company’s stances, but it did not respond by the time of publication.

#206. US midterm elections 2026 live: Democrats pick up more wins as vote counting in tight races continues and Republican blame game begins

Florida governor Ron DeSantis called Democratic wins in the midterms ‘close to an extinction-level event for Republicans’

Tell us: how do you feel about the midterm results?

Florida’s governor, Ron DeSantis, called Democratic wins in the midterms “close to an extinction-level event for Republicans” in remarks today.

He added that Republican losses in Texas “could reverberate for the next decade or more” and that if “the Republican party doesn’t have Texas as an anchor, then we’re not going to be viable nationally going forward”.

DeSantis added that he thought Republicans failed to target their ad spending on issues that would motivate voters, saying that ads on transgender Americans didn’t drive voters to the polls.

“Morale is as low as it can get” inside the White House, a senior official told the Associated Press today. The official added that senior aides were bracing for staff departures, Cabinet changes and Democratic investigations.

“It absolutely sucks to be here right ‌now,” the unnamed official said.

Despite internal polling six weeks ago that warned Republicans could expect losses in both chambers of Congress, the official said, Donald Trump refused to change his policy on Iran.

“Ultimately, he just didn’t care,” the official said. “He refused to listen to anyone.”

Mississippi is facing a shockingly close race for its open Senate seat – with no law on the books for initiating a recount.

Currently, Democrat Scott Colom is leading Republican incumbent Cindy Hyde-Smith by just 65 votes, with 95% of votes counted.

In many states, that close of a race would automatically trigger a recount, but Mississippi has no recount law. Derek Muller, an election law expert at the University of Notre Dame, tells the New York Times that a losing candidate may be able to challenge the inclusion of certain ballots.

The Republican senator Thom Tillis blamed Republican losses last night on the party’s “well intentioned, poorly executed policies” and certain members of the Trump administration, including Kristi Noem and Pete Hegseth.

In an interview with CNN, Tillis said: “We’ve got to regroup and be ready for 2028, and we won’t be ready for 2028 if we try and re-execute what I think were well intentioned, poorly executed policies over the last 18 months.”

He added criticism of former homeland security secretary Noem, saying: “What, a $220m ad campaign with her riding on a horse, people dead in Minneapolis because she put untrained people in a horrible situation. Give me a break. That’s the sort of tone deafness that will cause ’28 to look like ’08.”

Tillis also said the defense secretary, Pete Hegseth, is “just not the right guy for the job”, particularly in leading the war in Iran. “Nothing personal. The guy’s just demonstrated, like Noem, that they’re not up to the task of running these large, complex organizations,” he said.

The Ukrainian president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, said he hopes Democratic wins in the United States generate more support for Ukraine.

“I am also grateful to the Republicans, but the Democrats speak very clearly about supporting Ukraine and providing assistance ⁠to Ukraine,” Zelenskiy said in comments provided in German by German television station ZDF.

“They speak about renewing the connection between the United States and Europe … ⁠Therefore, there is hope that a balance of power will emerge and that we will receive ​more support.“

Zelenskyy has navigated a turbulent relationship with Donald Trump, particularly in recent weeks as the Ukrainian president said he felt personally betrayed by the United States pausing its sharing of military intelligence and waiving some sanctions on Russia to buy its diesel.

Florida’s governor, Ron DeSantis, called Democratic wins in the midterms “close to an extinction-level event for Republicans” in remarks today.

He added that Republican losses in Texas “could reverberate for the next decade or more” and that if “the Republican party doesn’t have Texas as an anchor, then we’re not going to be viable nationally going forward”.

DeSantis added that he thought Republicans failed to target their ad spending on issues that would motivate voters, saying that ads on transgender Americans didn’t drive voters to the polls.

Donald Trump said there are “many things, many more things than people understand” that he will consider working on with Democrats, during an interview with CNBC today.

During a phone call with CNBC’s Eamon Javers, Trump said he would work on financial items – though Javers described the president as “a little bit cagey here about exactly what those ideas are, at least at this early stage.”

Javers added, “But this is a president who is signaling to markets and signaling to Washington that he’s ready to deal”.

Democrats in Texas took control of the state house after historic wins for governor and US Senate, the Associated Press has called, amid a series of sweeping gains nationwide in Tuesday’s elections that have shifted the balance of power across several battleground and Republican-leaning states.

It will be the first time that Democrats control the house in Texas since Republicans took over after the 2002 election. Republicans are keeping control of the senate in the state.

It comes as Texas elected Democrat James Talarico to the US Senate, breaking a 38-year drought for his party in the Republican stronghold and delivering a stunning blow to Donald Trump, and Gina Hinojosa as its first Latina governor in a stunning upset. She defeated the incumbent Greg Abbott, a titan of Texas politics who was running for a record fourth term.

Democratic congressman Hakeem Jeffries, the current minority leader in the US House, has officially announced his candidacy for speaker.

As of now, Democrats have secured 237 seats in the House, completely routing Republicans by flipping several districts and overcoming a mid-decade GOP redistricting push.

With an overwhelming majority in the lower chamber, Jeffries is poised to become the first Black speaker of the House of Representatives in US history. In January, Once the new Congress is sworn in, it will vote on Jeffries’ candidacy, and on those of any other Democratic members who run for the position.

“Against all odds, House Democrats were consistently successful in the minority. We must now deliver the dramatic change the American people are demanding in the majority,” he said in a statement, while outlining the caucus’s plans in the new year.

Donald Trump’s wealth is falling along with his political fortunes.

Tradeable investment assets that helped make the president more than $2bn during his first year in office are collapsing in value the day after the Democrats trounced the Republicans in the midterm elections. Trump Media and Technology Group stock, which trades on the Nasdaq exchange, was priced at just over $5 this morning – its lowest mark ever, and down from an all-time high of $101. It lost 11% overnight, dropping from $5.90 once the election returns started coming in. Trump has reported he owns 114m shares of DJT.

Trump owns over 40% of the stock through a trust. He has still grown his wealth dramatically during his presidency; Forbes estimated in a September analysis that he is $2.7bn wealthier than when he won the White House in 2024.

Another major asset for Trump and his family are World Liberty Financial digital tokens. The digital coins are trading at less than 5 cents after the election. At their height, in 2025, they were valued at six times that, at 31 cents. The WLFI coin, the governance token of World Liberty financial, began sliding in late October as the election drew near, and as a hack of Binance rattled crypto markets. It experienced a steep decline around 7pm ET on Tuesday as polls in some states closed.

Trump already made more than $500m in profit selling crypto assets last year, according to his personal financial disclosures, though he still owns 15bn tokens.

World Liberty is a venture created by the Trump family and the family of Steven Witkoff. Trump appointed Witkoff, a personal friend and real estate developer, to negotiate diplomatic arrangements about Gaza, Iran and Ukraine.

The value of both WLIF and DJT has always been pegged to the way Trump is perceived, said Jim Angel, an associate professor at Georgetown University’s McDonough School of Business. He added:

double quotation markPeople think they’re investing in Trump. They really aren’t. They’re really betting on his popularity. That’s what they’re betting on.

Right now his popularity is at record lows, and so we see the price of DJT and World Liberty at record lows.

Separate digital novelty coins called Trump$, which made the president $636m in sales in 2025, have also collapsed in value. They are priced at about $1.40 and seem to have lost about 10% of their value overnight as election results came in. They launched shortly before his inauguration and briefly traded as high as $45 in January 2025.

My colleagues compiled a visual analysis of key Republican losses this cycle, mapping called-race returns to show how Democratic victory margins in GOP strongholds compare to past elections.

The political demise of Susan Collins – a fixture of Maine and Washington politics for three decades – recalls the famous line in Ernest Hemingway’s The Sun Also Rises about how one of the novel’s characters experienced bankruptcy: gradually, then suddenly.

In the run up to Tuesday’s stunning Democrat “blue wave” election triumph, it seemed obvious that Collins’ 30-year tenure as Maine’s Republican senator was vulnerable to the challenge of Troy Jackson, the Democrats’ explicitly working-class candidate, as voters prepared to unleash pent-up anger with Donald Trump’s presidency.

Rather like the Berlin Wall in the days before it finally fell in 1989, however, no one seemed to believe it could really happen until it did.

Collins, after all, had survived seemingly credible electoral challenges before and appeared primed – despite opinion polls showing a close race – to win a sixth Senate term, having once pledged to serve only two. She had become something of a state institution, with her assertions that Washington connections brought federal funds and contracts to Maine carrying weight with many constituents.

Jackson, meanwhile, seemed a less-than-polished prospect for a state that had previously sent such august figures as Ed Muskie, George Mitchell and Bill Cohen to the US Senate before they went on to become international statesmen.

The realization that this elemental political force was about to sweep away Collins came late in the evening, when – with about 60% of the ballots counted – returns showed Jackson with a convincing lead. Within minutes, MS Now reported that Collins had called to concede; wild cheers and chants of “Troy, Troy” filled the ballroom at Portland’s Holiday Inn.

Amid the euphoria, it was left to Jackson to deliver a message of humble, down-to-earth populism that emphasized his trade union background and underlined that his unlikely triumph had been driven by grassroots discontent.

“You understood what it would mean to send one of your own to the United States Senate,” he told the crowd. “Donald Trump and the billionaires need to understand one thing very clear – something about your new senator. I don’t owe them a damn thing.

“When they threaten your healthcare, your livelihood, or your freedoms, I am going to stand up to them. You know where I’ll stand. You know how I’ll vote. And when you’ve got a question about it, you’ll be able to ask me yourself. I’m not acting like I’m the best thing that ever happened to the US Senate or anything like that.”

It was a promise of accessibility that critics say Collins never provided – despite her claims of independent-mindedness and protestations of frequently voting against Trump, who failed to carry Maine in any of his three presidential campaigns.

Democrat Leticia Gutierrez has won the race for Texas’ ninth congressional district, a seat that was gerrymandered in 2025 to benefit Republicans. It’s another example of how the GOP’s mid-decade redistricting push ultimately backfired this cycle.

Gutierrez beat her Republican challenger Alex Mealer for control of the district, which includes the greater Houston area.

On a midterm election night that was a huge disappointment for Republicans, with Democrats taking control of the US House and Senate, the country’s most prominent conservative television network felt the brunt of viewer disillusionment with Donald Trump and his party, according to preliminary audience numbers.

The Fox News Channel, which often leads in viewership on election night, appears to have come out far behind the competition. According to preliminary “Fast Nationals” ratings dating from the Nielsen firm, the network averaged 3.882 million total viewers between 8 and 11pm, far behind broadcast networks NBC (6.519 million viewers) and ABC (4.906 million viewers), and even trailing fellow cable networks CNN (3.927 million viewers) and MS Now (3.922 million viewers). Fox News did, however, defeat CBS News, which attracted just 2.629 million viewers.

The numbers are preliminary, and are likely to be revised upwards when final audience data is released tomorrow.

Overall, the big six television networks averaged 25.784 million total viewers during prime-time election coverage, up from 22.215 million total viewers in 2022. During those 2022 elections, Fox News averaged 7.422 million viewers.

With Trump reaching historic levels of unpopularity, Fox News has seen viewers tune out in recent months. Last month, the network’s viewership was down 27% compared with the same month last year in total day viewers, though it still handily defeated CNN and MS Now. Lachlan Murdoch, the chief executive of parent company Fox Corp, acknowledged “viewer fatigue” in an earnings call on Monday.

Trump, who criticized Fox throughout election night and blamed the network for his party’s losses, poked fun at the network’s October figures in a Truth Social post, chalking up the decline to the channel’s industry-standard practice of booking Democratic candidates for interviews.

“FoxNews Ratings are way down (27%!) because they’re spending too much time covering the Trump Deranged Dumocrats!” he wrote on Tuesday evening. “All of those people who used to watch Fox, and no longer do, get it. It’s them, not me – My numbers are better than they have ever been.”

The historic Democratic victories in the 2026 midterm elections were more than just a rebuke of Donald Trump and his Maga movement – they are a five-alarm fire for the Republican party itself, which may be on the verge of collapse. The sweeping wins in GOP strongholds like Texas, Iowa and Kansas reveal an electorate that is fed up and eager for change. But that anger could turn on Democrats – quick. Now that they run Congress, what can the fractured Democratic coalition achieve? And will they do enough to keep voters on their side in 2028?

#205. Indonesia deploys troops to Jakarta after wiretapping allegations

Indonesian troops are being stationed in Jakarta amid wiretapping allegations implicating the president's chief of staff. Suspects are being accused of illegally tapping the president's phones.

Indonesia's military deployed more than 20,000 troops and personnel to the capital city Jakarta after authorities launched an investigation into an alleged wiretapping operation targeting the President Prabowo Subianto.

Some 20,000 troops have been deployed in Jakarta amid the scandal with the president's chief of staff is currently being investigated.

So far, three senior police generals have been detained. They are being accused of illegally tapping the phones of the president, journalists, politicians and business people.

Among those detained is Listyo Sigit Prabowo — not related to the president — who was the national police chief before becoming the president's chief of staff last month.

The presidential palace claims the alleged wiretapping targeting President Subianto could be regarded as an act "tending towards treason."

The authorities did not officially confirm that the deployment is connected to the scandal.

The number of troops and personnel deployed to the capital since Monday surpassed 20,000 as of Wednesday, according to the navy operations assistant, Vice Admiral Yayan Sofiyan.

Indonesian armed forces spokesperson Major General Muhammad Nas claimed that the troop movement was a "standart procedure" for maintaining national security.

According to Indonesia's senior minister for legal affairs, the government has yet to determine the motive for the wiretapping.

The suspects, who have not responded publicly to the allegations, could face unlawful surveillance related charges, which can lead up to 10 years in prison.

"There is a strong suspicion that the wiretapping was carried out not in the interests of the state or the National Police, but for the interests of other parties,” Adrianus Meliala, a criminologist at the University of Indonesia and a former commissioner of the National Police Commission said.

"No one expected Listyo Sigit Prabowo to be named a suspect,” said Meliala, "But if the allegations are proven, the case could be understood in the context of political maneuvering. Anyone who engages in politics must also be prepared to face the political consequences," Meliala said.
