#202. What pet cemeteries reveal about being human

Editor’s Note:  Eric Tourigny is a lecturer in historical archaeology at Newcastle University, UK. His research interprets osteological and material culture remains alongside historic texts to examine changing human-animal relationships in Europe and North America over the past 500 years. The views expressed in this commentary are his own. Read more opinion on CNN.

Why do we bury our departed loved ones in a cemetery? A primary purpose is to provide survivors with an opportunity to grieve and gain a sense of closure. The cemetery is a place for the living as much as it is a place for the dead.

But what about when the dearly departed are not human – but our pets?

The Spanish city of Barcelona recently announced that it will be investing in the establishment of the country’s first public pet cemetery. Set to open next year, it will offer both burials and cremations – with an estimated 7,000 carried out each year.

For me, as someone who has spent years researching the development of pet cemeteries elsewhere in the world, this news came as a shock. Barcelona is a densely populated city with limited privately owned land – one where 50% of families own a pet.

How did a city that is home to 180,000 dogs not already have a public pet cemetery? Until now, the service was only provided by the private sector, according to Barcelona’s Councilor for Climate Emergency and Ecological Transition, Eloi Badia. He added that the municipal-funded initiative was sparked by “constant public demand.”

After all, public pet cemeteries have been around in Europe and the Americas since the late 19th century. Britain’s first public pet cemetery appeared in London’s Hyde Park in 1881. New York’s Hartsdale pet cemetery was founded in 1896, followed a few years later by Paris’ ornate Cimetière des Chiens in 1899.

I became interested in the history of modern pet burial practices while investigating the archaeological record of a centuries-old house in Toronto. I came across a (very) large dog buried in the back garden which, according to the historical record, was occupied between 1840 and 1870.

This dog survived to an elderly age but, sadly, suffered from degenerative joint disease and severe infections during his last months. His ailments progressed to such a state that suggest he received some level of care in his final weeks. He then was buried in a personal plot behind the family home.

This elderly dog led me to think about the different ways people interact with the bodies of their pets after death. Could this behavior be reflective of the relationships they held with their animals in life? In this instance, why take the time to carefully bury a dog in its own space when other, arguably easier options existed?

This, after all, was an era when people often disposed of their dead pets in the river, or might have sold their bodies for meat and skin.

Good hygiene is an obvious reason to choose a burial – no one wants decomposing animal bodies in the street or their garden – but that wouldn’t immediately warrant a personalized, dedicated burial and gravestone.

The most straight forward option would be to dispose of a deceased animal with the household waste. But such treatment would evidently feel less ceremonious and would not offer appropriate emotional closure to what was likely an important relationship.

Like the burial of people, the burial of pets is an intimate cultural practice, one that changes over time and is reflective of the changing relationships that a society has with its cherished creatures.

My study of historic gravestones and epitaphs in Britain from the Victorian period to today shows this changing human-animal relationship. In the 19th century, gravestones were often dedicated to a “loving friend” or “devoted companion,” suggesting pets were mostly considered important friends.

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

By the early 20th century, pets had become members of the family – evidenced by the appearance of family surnames on the gravestones, and loving epitaphs written by “Mummy and Daddy.”

Society’s changing attitude towards the role of animals in the afterlife can also be found. Fast-forward a few decades later, and gravestones were more likely to reference a reunion then earlier ones. For example, the owners of Denny, the “brave little cat” buried in an east London cemetery in 1952, wrote on his epitaph “God bless until we meet again.”

I wonder what the epitaphs in Barcelona’s new cemetery will reveal about modern Catalan relationships with animals.

Over time, our ways of treating our animal dead appear to reflect an even closer relationship in life. Once strictly forbidden by law, the last decade saw many jurisdictions, like the state of New York, allowing the co-burial of cremated animals and people, which will undoubtedly lead to changing funerary and commemoration practices for both humans and animals.

For me, the most remarkable similarity between modern and historic pet cemeteries is the striking evidence for the heartbreak and taboos around grieving for animals.

The connection that someone has with their pet can be just as strong and just as significant as their relationships with other humans. Yet today, as in over 100 years ago, individuals continue to struggle to find the appropriate outlet to express their pain, hiding heartache for fear of the social repercussions that might come with public acknowledgment of the existence of such a bond.

The RSPCA reassures the public on its website that they should not feel shame for their grief. In the UK, charities like the Blue Cross and Rainbow Bridge Pet Loss Grief Center offer counseling to bereaved humans.

Close relationships between people and animals have existed for millennia, but in western European cultures, there were few acceptable ways to mourn that relationship. As society becomes more accepting of the importance of human-animal relationships to our collective wellbeing, it is not surprising to see us follow rituals resembling those we use to mourn the loss of our closest human relationships.

At an estimated cost of €200 ($217) per service at the new Barcelona cemetery, it is important to acknowledge that this opportunity to grieve will not be financially available to everyone in the city.

This will not be a space for all of the city’s pets. Pet owners may opt to keep cremated remains within their home or spread out ashes in a meaningful location instead. Online forums and digital pet cemeteries also provide other opportunities to commemorate the relationship and express grief.

Whether one chooses a pet cemetery or not, there are many acceptable ways to express your grief – and to remember your relationship with the important animals in your life.

#201. Almost no one uses AI agents to book their Uber, company says

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Very few people are using personal AI agents to order Ubers, apparently. On the transportation giant’s third-quarter earnings call on Wednesday, the company said that only four rides were ordered through an AI agent during its busiest week in October: the week of Halloween.

While more people used an AI agent to order food through its delivery service, Uber Eats, the volume was still fairly small compared to the overall number of trips.

Uber’s chief financial officer, Balaji Krishnamurthy, told Wall Street analysts on the call that the company handled around 320 million trips and orders across Uber and Uber Eats during the week of Halloween, but only 500 Uber Eats orders were placed by an AI agent.

“It is very, very early and very limited traction that we are seeing so far,” said CEO Dara Khosrowshahi, referring to the low number of orders placed by AI agents. “Listen, we hope that there will be more traction.”

The surprisingly low numbers of AI-initiated transactions come amid growing hype for a new wave of AI agents that don’t just answer questions but also handle tasks on users’ behalf. There’s been an explosion of personal AI startups, including one of the buzziest, Instinct, which was founded last year and raised its most recent round of funding at a $10 billion valuation. Meta’s Muse also became the No. 1 app in Apple’s U.S. App Store last month, topping 10 million users, per Meta’s own reporting. The company said many were using it to shop and transact with businesses.

OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, and SpaceXAI (Grok) are also now offering their own AI agents, as are numerous smaller startups.

Still, early numbers from public companies indicate the agentic web still has a long way to go. DoorDash, for instance, said last week on its earnings call that it has only received a few hundred orders from AI agents so far, as most customers are still placing orders through the DoorDash app.

Uber is seeing the same behavior, it seems. Khosrowshahi pointed out that the average time to book a ride is only 50 seconds in the company’s app, and re-ordering a favorite food only takes 90 seconds. That suggests an AI agent isn’t necessarily saving anyone time.

Despite the low number of AI-driven transactions, Khosrowshahi seemed bullish on the idea of agents in general. “We think that is another opportunity to reach our platform. We think it will be a net positive in terms of demand on the ecosystem,” he said.

Right now, there are around eight major AI assistants on the market, Khosrowshahi noted, and he expects that number to grow to more than 10 in time.

“Every single AI system company is talking to us,” he added.

#200. Cambridge asks Post Office inquiry barrister to lead review into death of Jason Arday

Jason Beer KC will examine university’s handling of claims against Arday and protection offered to academics in spotlight

The lead barrister for the Post Office Horizon inquiry has been appointed to head an independent review into the death of the Cambridge academic Jason Arday.

Jason Beer KC will examine the handling of allegations of research and other misconduct made against Arday before his resignation as well as the university’s recruitment and support for him as its youngest ever black professor at the age of 37.

Beer said he would consider whether wider institutional changes were needed at Cambridge after looking into the protection it offered to academics facing significant public and media scrutiny and the procedures around the appointment of Arday.

The professor of the sociology of education died at his home in south London while facing widely publicised allegations of plagiarism and inconsistencies in statements about his career.

Arday denied the accusations of plagiarism but Cambridge said on 5 August it would open an investigation into his academic qualifications. He resigned hours after this was announced and was found dead nine days later.

Beer said Cambridge had asked him to conduct an independent, thorough and searching examination of its institutional arrangements and the decisions it made over Arday.

He said: “I will look not simply at what the university’s policies and procedures required but also at whether they were themselves reasonable; how they operated in practice; who was responsible for making decisions; what information was reasonably available to them; whether relevant concerns were recognised and properly addressed; and whether the university’s various structures and functions worked together effectively – all in the case of Prof Arday, and more broadly.

“I hope that those who have relevant knowledge or experience will feel able to contribute to the review. Their perspectives may be important in understanding not only what happened in Prof Arday’s case but whether there are wider institutional changes to be made.

“I will take seriously my responsibility to treat participants with sensitivity, to respect confidentiality and to ensure fairness to anyone whose actions may be examined.”

Get the day’s headlines and highlights emailed direct to you every morning

Beer’s review is the second of two independent reviews of Cambridge’s involvement with Arday. The first, announced by the university in September, asked Nazir Afzal, a former chief crown prosecutor for north-west England, to focus on the support given to Arday in the immediate period before his death.

A veteran of public inquiries, including working for NHS England in the Covid-19 inquiry, Beer is best known for his role as lead counsel to the Post Office Horizon IT statutory inquiry that investigated the wrongful criminal prosecution of nearly 1,000 post office operators.

Prof Deborah Prentice, Cambridge’s vice-chancellor, said: “Jason Beer is an eminent legal expert who will bring vast experience of leading complex reviews and inquiries. We are equally thankful to the distinguished panel of independent experts he has convened to assist him in this important task and look forward to receiving their findings and recommendations in due course.”

#198. Germany news: Commerce chief calls for stable US trade relations after midterms, warning that uncertainty over Trump's tariff policies remains

German business is pinning its hopes on greater stability in US trade relations after a midterm blow for Donald Trump. Meanwhile, Germany's automakers appear to be feeling less gloomy than in recent years. DW has more.

Germany's main Chamber of Commerce says it's hoping for more predictable US trade policy after Donald Trump's Republicans lost control of both chambers of Congress

Germany's auto industry confidence has jumped to its highest level since mid-2024, according to a survey

Despite the improvement, experts warn that uncertainty remains high and German carmakers still face major challenges

Housing giant Vonovia has criticized Berlin's proposed takeover of large private housing portfolios

Here is a roundup of top stories from and about Germany on Wednesday, November 4, 2026:

Bavarian politician Hubert Aiwanger was found dead in a hotel in Berlin on Wednesday, the German capital's public prosecutor's office said.

Aiwanger was Bavaria's deputy premier, economy minister and the chairman of the right-wing populist Free Voters party. He was 55 years old.

The circumstances surrounding Aiwanger's death are still unclear. Police and prosecutors in the German capital have launched an investigation.

On Wednesday evening, investigators said the goal is to "determine the specific cause of death and the circumstances surrounding it, as well as to rule out any possible foul play."

Read our full report about Aiwanger's sudden death.

The German Cabinet has decided to slash the number of workers it admits into the country every year as part of the Western Balkans Regulation.

The program provides a simplified pathway for nationals of six Western Balkan nations to migrate to Germany for work, including Albania, Bosnia and Herzegovina, Kosovo, Montenegro, North Macedonia, and Serbia.

The quota currently allows 50,000 people from these countries to move to Germany each year. But Chancellor Friedrich Merz’s government has now slashed that figure to 25,000 per year starting January 1, 2027.

The government says its aim is to ensure that unemployed individuals already in the country are given priority for vacant positions.

The measure needs approval from the Bundesrat, or upper house of parliament, which represents Germany's states.

Numerous business associations have opposed the decision and warned of a loss of workers.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The number of new asylum-seekers in Germany fell last month to the lowest October figure since 2011, the tabloid Bild reported citing figures from the Interior Ministry.

According to the report, the number of initial asylum applications and new arrivals in the asylum process in October stood at 5,707, marking a 40% decline compared to the same period last year and a nearly 70% drop compared to 2024.

"We are successfully curbing illegal migration," the newspaper quoted Interior Minister Alexander Dobrindt as saying.

Chancellor Friedrich Merz’s government had initiated what it said was a "migration turnaround" at the start of its term in May 2025.

As part of it, Dobrindt ordered stricter border controls with neighboring countries and the return of migrants. The number of unauthorized entries has since fallen significantly, and asylum applications have also declined.

In the first ten months of this year, the Federal Police recorded 40,193 unauthorized entries at Germany’s land, air, and sea borders — about 24% fewer than in the same period last year.

German Interior Minister Alexander Dobrindt reiterated his statements about the groups responsible for the riots that followed nationwide protests led by young people on October 31.

"There were violent left-wing extremists, and there were troublemakers from migrant communities," he said on Wednesday during a special debate in parliament.

Opposition parties like the Greens and The Left criticized the minister's comments.

Irene Mihalic, the parliamentary secretary of the Green Party, even accused Dobrindt of using "racist remarks" to divert attention from the legitimate demands of young protesters.

Some members of the Social Democratic Party (SPD) defended their coalition partner.

"We can only solve the problem if we name it," said Sebastian Fiedler, the SPD parliamentary group’s spokesperson on domestic policy.

His party colleague Helge Lindh, however, cautioned against "stigmatization."

Meanwhile, Bernd Baumann, a member of parliament from the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD), said Germany is standing on the "brink of an abyss," accusing the far left of allying itself with those he described as "radical migrants who reject Western civilization."

Of the more than 8,000 people who took part in the mostly peaceful protests in Frankfurt, authorities estimated there were 1,000 to 1,500 violent troublemakers, "predominantly from a migrant background."

Lamya Kaddor of the Greens argued that such categorizations must take into consideration the demographic makeup of Frankfurt.

"If more than one in two people in Frankfurt have a migrant background, then it is hardly surprising that many of the suspects also have a migrant background," she said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Finance Minister and Vice Chancellor Lars Klingbeil said more private investment was needed to boost the German economy and put it on a self-sustaining recovery path.

Government spending alone would not be enough, he said, adding that 2027 should become the year of private investment.

To help achieve that goal, he pointed to improved depreciation rules for businesses and a gradual reduction in the corporate income tax rate starting in 2028.

The minister, from the center-left Social Democratic Party (SPD), also rejected plans by Berlin's possible incoming post-communist Left Party-led administration to expropriate apartments to tackle the city's housing crisis.

Klingbeil argued that such a move would deter investors and damage Germany’s reputation as a business location.

The federal government will introduce an investment protection law to prevent the plans in Berlin, he said.

The minister also called on the Left Party to expel antisemitic elements and individuals with ties to criminal clans from the party.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The German Association of the Automotive Industry (VDA) has called for changes to the European Union's rules governing hybrid cars.

"It is utterly incomprehensible that the EU is sticking to its plan to tighten the so-called utility factor for plug-in hybrids this month," VDA President Hildegard Müller said on Wednesday.

"In doing so, Brussels is putting around 50,000 jobs at risk in Germany alone," she added.

Plug-in hybrids are cars that have both a combustion engine and an electric motor with a rechargeable battery. The utility factor indicates how effectively plug-in hybrids contribute to manufacturers' climate and fleet targets.

As usage data has shown that these vehicles run more frequently on the internal combustion engine than in electric mode, the EU plans to amend the utility factor from November 29.

And this change is set to apply more widely from 2027.

Plug-in hybrids will then, for calculation purposes, offset the impact of internal combustion engines to a lesser extent.

"We are in danger of losing a technology in Europe that is in demand worldwide. Brussels must give plug-in technology a future," said Müller.

BMW has reported a nearly 15% drop in quarterly net profit as weaker sales in China outweighed gains in Europe and the US.

The head of Germany's main Chamber of Commerce has called for more predictable US trade policy after Republicans lost control of both chambers of Congress.

"We are counting first and foremost on stability in trade relations with the US," Helena Melnikov, chief executive of the German Chamber of Commerce and Industry (DIHK), told the broadcaster ARD television. "No more zigzagging."

Trump's repeated tariff announcements, reversals and changes to implementation deadlines have made it harder for German exporters to plan.

Melnikov said reliability in Germany's biggest export market was "extremely important," but cautioned against predicting President Donald Trump's next economic moves.

She urged Germany to expand trading partnerships beyond the US and implement promised domestic economic reforms.

Achim Wambach, president of Germany's ZEW economic research institute, warned that losing his congressional majorities could push Trump to pursue more of his agenda through trade and foreign policy, where he retains extensive powers.

"Uncertainty about the future direction of US trade policy remains high," he said, also warning of potential deadlock over the US budget.

Wambach said Democratic control would probably not bring a fundamental return to free trade, pointing to broad bipartisan agreement on protecting US industry and trade policy toward China.

Confidence in Germany's auto industry has risen sharply, reaching its highest level since mid-2024, according to the Munich-based Ifo institute.

The survey was conducted in October, so it does not reflect the US midterm election results or their potential impact on trade policy.

Ifo's business climate index climbed from minus 33.2 to minus 4.1 points. Companies' assessment of their current situation turned positive for the first time since mid-2024, while expectations improved but remained negative overall.

"There can be no talk of an all-clear for the automotive industry," Ifo industry expert Anita Wolfl said, pointing to sharp monthly fluctuations in business confidence as evidence of considerable uncertainty.

Wolfl said a Franco-German agreement on a joint automotive strategy may have helped lift sentiment, but offered only temporary relief.

"It does not change the industry's central challenge of catching up with the leaders in technological competition," she said.

Germany's housing group Vonovia has criticized plans by Berlin's prospective governing coalition to bring large private housing portfolios into public ownership.

The socialist Left Party, Greens and center-left Social Democrats (SPD) have agreed during coalition negotiations to target companies owning more than 10,000 apartments. Their plan envisages compensation of no more than €16 billion ($18 billion) for 160,000 homes — an average of €100,000 each.

"The plans to bring private companies' apartments into public ownership naturally concern us," Vonovia CEO Luka Mucic said while presenting quarterly results.

He called the proposal "simply the wrong approach" and legally questionable, arguing that it would create no additional housing and undermine confidence in investment.

The Financial Times estimated Vonovia's Berlin properties were worth €20.4 billion, compared with potential compensation of €12 billion.

But Maximilian Fuhrmann, a housing researcher at the German Tenants' Association in North Rhine-Westphalia, disputed that assessment. Using Vonovia's stock market valuation and debt, he calculated an average value of just over €100,000 per apartment across its portfolio — broadly in line with the proposed compensation.

#197. CEOs are tired of being held responsible for gun regulation

Americans have grown used to corporate executives treading the well-worn paths of the Northeast corridor to convene alongside elected officials in Washington, DC, and discuss geopolitics, policy and all that’s in-between.

In 2017, major CEOs from across the country came together to oppose North Carolina’s transgender bathroom law. In 2019, they called abortion bans “bad for business.”

After the deadly attack on the US Capitol on January 6, 2021, many of corporate America’s biggest names denounced the rioters and pledged to halt their political giving.

Recently, more than 1,000 companies promised to voluntarily curtail their operations in Russia in protest of Moscow’s war on Ukraine.

Dick’s Sporting Goods stopped selling semi-automatic, assault-style rifles at stores and Citigroup put new restrictions on gun sales by business customers after the mass shooting at a high school in Parkland, Florida, in 2018.

A year later, after mass shootings at a Walmart in El Paso, Texas, and a nightclub in Dayton, Ohio, Walmart ended handgun ammunition sales.

Corporate leadership has long been vocal on the issue of gun control – in 2019 and again this past summer nearly 150 major companies – including Lululemon, Lyft, Bain Capital, Bloomberg LP, Permanente Medical Group and Unilever – called gun violence a “public health crisis” and demanded that the US Senate pass legislation to address it.

That’s why corporate America’s silence in the wake of the latest mass shooting at a school in Nashville is so jarring. The United States has come to rely on the increasing power of large corporations as political advocates.

But Yale professor Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, a vocal advocate of corporate social responsibility who has a direct line to major CEOs around the globe, said that top executives are forlorn. Their previous efforts haven’t done much to push the needle on gun control legislation and without more backing, they don’t know what else they can do at the moment, he said.

Before the Bell spoke with Sonnenfeld, who runs Yale School of Management’s Chief Executive Leadership Institute, a nonprofit educational and research institute focused on CEO leadership and corporate governance.

This interview has been edited for clarity and length.

Before the Bell: CEOs have been quiet about gun reform since the latest mass school shooting in Nashville, have you heard anything about plans to speak out?

Jeffrey Sonnenfeld: Where is everybody else? Where is all of civil society? CEOs are just one group of people and it’s like we’re turning to them to be our saviors on every topic. They’ve joined causes with valor and nobility but they can’t just be taking cause after cause as if there’s nobody else in society. The social change that happened in the 1960s wasn’t being led primarily by CEOs. Social changes really happened when we saw the interfaith activity of clergy locking arms and canvassing legislators. We saw campuses alive and aroused. Where’s all the student activism?

The CEOs are still the most active even if they’re less active than they were six months ago. They’re not there as hired hands of shareholders to fill the role of politicians and civic leaders. They’re there to join that chorus, but they don’t want to be the only one singing.

So is this what you’re hearing from top CEOs? Have they gotten tired of advocating?

I just got off of a CEO call on voting rights and this morning we had a forum on sustainability – CEOs are still the most active on these fronts. It’s the same thing on immigration reform. If a CEO was working an 18 hour day on a 12 day week, they still couldn’t address all of the issues that need addressing.

The nation’s CEOs are waiting for everybody else to join them. They don’t need to restate something they’ve already stated. They’ve jumped in the pool, where’s everybody else?

So what do you think has led to this complacency amongst Americans and the growing reliance on CEOs to advocate on our behalf?

They’ve taken a very strong stance and they’ve gone out further than the general public. They are where the general public is on surveys, but they’re not where the general public is on action in the streets. So we’re ready for others to now do something. Enough already on saying ‘what are the CEOs doing?’ Social capital is as valuable as financial capital. CEOs understand that in their soul, they want there to be social capital. They want there to be public trust, but they need the rest of civil society to join them. And that’s their frustration.

But don’t these CEOs hold the purse strings in terms of donating to powerful politicians?

You would think that, but since the 2020 elections much less of campaign contributions have come from big business. Since the 2021 run on the Capitol, a lot of businesses either had an official moratorium or they’ve given mere pennies to politicians. The common impression on the street that CEOs are controlling campaign purses strings is 100% wrong.

Tesla reported. a modest 4% rise in sales in the first quarter compared to the final three months of last year, despite a series of price cuts on its lower priced vehicles and talk by CEO Elon Musk about strong demand at those lower prices.

The first quarter also marked the fourth straight quarter that Tesla has produced more vehicles than it has delivered to customers. Some of that may be due to the ramp up in production at two new factories, one in Texas, the other in Germany, which opened last spring, and a lag between that increased production and sales.

Tesla said there was an increase in the number of its more expensive models, the Model S and Model X, in transit to Europe, the Middle East and Africa, as well as to the Asia Pacific region.

But it does mean that over the last 12 months Tesla has produced 78,000 more cars than it has sold, suggesting that talk of strong demand by Tesla executives may not be backed up by the numbers.

“Early this year, we had a price adjustment. After that, we actually generated a huge demand, more than we can produce, really,” said Tom Zhu, Tesla’s executive in charge of global production and sales. “And as Elon said, as long as you offer a product with value at affordable price, you don’t have to worry about demand.”

#195. AI PACs spent millions trying to shape Congress — here’s how they fared

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Political groups both supporting and opposing the AI industry spent millions of dollars trying to influence Tuesday’s midterm elections and shape the future of AI regulation. That spending produced mixed results, with some of the most heavily backed candidates losing their races.

On the industry side, two AI-focused political action committees (PACs) that raised and spent money to influence the elections — American Mission and Building the Future — collectively spent $4.8 million to back South Carolina Senate candidate Darline Graham, who lost. American Mission is the Republican spending arm of Leading the Future (LTF), a broader political group backed by OpenAI president Greg Brockman and the VC firm Andreessen Horowitz.

American Mission also spent more than $500,000 apiece supporting Republican House candidates Aaron Flint in Montana and Laurie Buckhout in North Carolina, both of whom lost. LTF’s Democratic spending arm, Think Big, had better luck backing Florida Rep. Jared Moskowitz, who won reelection.

Groups advocating for stronger AI safeguards also saw mixed results. Public First, which is backed by Anthropic, supported several Republicans who lost through its affiliated PAC but had more success backing Democratic candidates. It also contributed $3 million to a PAC supporting California state Sen. Scott Wiener, who was leading his race at the time of publication.

Meanwhile, Guardrails Alliance, a group that has sought to make taking money from AI companies a political liability, spent more than $1 million apiece opposing two candidates who lost: California Rep. Jimmy Gomez and Montana’s Aaron Flint. It also spent $300,000 supporting Angela Gonzales-Torres, who defeated Gomez.

It’s difficult to separate the impact of AI-related spending from the broader Democratic wave that swept Tuesday’s elections. Elon Musk, who reportedly poured half a billion dollars into supporting Republicans in the midterms, also saw many of his candidates lose. Indeed, LTF also backed some candidates it believed were likely to win, only to see them lose to the Dems.

For LTF, though, the general election wasn’t the main focus. The group told TechCrunch ahead of Election Day that it thinks primaries (the contests that choose each party’s nominee) are a more effective use of its money, because spending there can build name recognition and help candidates set up AI policy agendas.

LTF spent $27.38 million in the primaries, including $8 million alone on opposing New York Assembly member Alex Bores, who lost his bid for Congress back in June. When deciding whether it’s worth jumping into a general election race, LTF assesses how competitive the race is, whether it thinks spending could make a difference, and whether it should keep its powder dry for future elections.

Charlie Blaettler, political director of Guardrails Alliance, had a different theory for why LTF spent comparatively little in the general election.

“They don’t want their money to become an issue in these elections,” he said. He pointed to the Michigan Senate primary election, in which a pro-Israel lobbying group called AIPAC spent close to $30 million backing Democratic candidate Haley Stevens against Abdul El-Sayed. Stevens conceded the primary to El-Sayed, who went on to win the Senate seat.

“AIPAC became the story in that race,” Blaettler said. “We know people hate AI billionaires. They hate the people running these companies. They’re very distrustful and disgusted by the political spending and lobbying that the tech industry does. If they spend in a competitive race, they would just become an enormous liability for their candidates.”

The main fight among AIPACs, whether they are pro-industry or pro-regulation, is whether Congress should establish a national AI framework that overrides state laws, or whether states will maintain their own ability to impose safeguards.

Neither side seems poised to back down. LTF, which favors a single national AI framework, told TechCrunch it plans to keep spending in the 2028 elections, while Guardrails Alliance says it wants to keep opposing candidates backed by the AI industry.

Blaettler said that while LTF’s ads rarely touch on AI, instead focusing on popular issues for each candidate, Guardrail Alliance’s ads are specifically about who is taking money from the AI industry.

“Our goal is to make AI money politically toxic,” Blaettler said.

#194. UK politics: Burnham says US midterm results have created ‘opportunity’ for world to focus on cost of living and ending Iran war – as it happened

‘I think people want to see the world coming together and focusing on the things that matter to people,’ PM says

Andy Burnham has said that he hopes the sweeping losses for Donald Trump’s Republican party in the US elections will give the world an “opportunity” to focus on ending the Iran war and easing the cost of living pressures.

In an interview at the Commonwealth summit in Antigua, asked if he was worried the results might make Trump more unpredictable, Burnham replied:

double quotation markPresident Trump has accepted the results, and I feel the world has an opportunity now to unite around the issue that is affecting us all, and that’s the cost of living.

That has been the issue that I have put front and centre in my first three months in Downing Street, and we’ve made moves.

But I can’t get people’s cost of living pressures down until the conflict in the Middle East is resolved. The conflict in Ukraine started by Russia.

These are the things we have to focus on now, and I hope the world has a moment to do that. And certainly, in my calls with leaders, that is what I will be asking everybody to do.

Some politicians and commentators have interpreted the results as evidence that rightwing populism is in retreat around the world. But when asked if he thought the elections were part of a global pattern, Burnham was reluctant to make that claim. Instead, he replied:

double quotation markI think people want to see the world coming together and focusing on the things that matter to people, and that’s what the voters, in my view, are saying in the United States.

Let’s get focused in a laser-like way on those cost of living issues. And I think around the world there’s a recognition that countries can’t do that on their own, and can’t do that when we have division between nations.

I’ve been working hard as prime minister to bring a new approach, a new approach in the Middle East, and the foreign secretary set that out [with the announcements about sanctions on illegal Israeli settlement in the West Bank].

A new approach to Europe, and we believe that will help us get growth back up and reduce those cost of living pressures. But a new approach with the Commonwealth too.

Asked if Britain would be pushing for de-escalation in the Iran war, Burnham said:

double quotation markI’ll be working for de-escalation. To make it very clear, we want to see a resolution, the Strait of Hormuz fully opened again. That’s what we will be working towards.

The government has also introduced into parliament the energy independence bill, which it says is “a down payment on longer-term reforms that will fix Britain’s broken energy system, putting affordability at its heart, while unlocking barriers to deliver clean power”.

The Press Association’s QuickCheck service has been looking at some of the facts cited by Louise Haigh, the first secretary of state, and Alex Burghart, the deputy Tory leader, at PMQs. Here are three figures that it suggests are potentially misleading.

215,000 jobs at risk from Labour tax rises?

Burghart claimed the OBR report said 215,000 jobs were at risk from the government’s tax rises.

PA says he seemed to be quoting a figure that applied to the impact of the tax threshold freezes that started when the Tories were in office.

The OBR said: “Following our standard approach, we have estimated that, taken together, the threshold freezes in income tax and NICs from 2022/23 to 2030/31 could decrease labour supply by 215,000 in average-hours equivalent (AHE) terms by 2030/31.”

Burghart asked Haigh how much the government was spending on working-age welfare and, when she refused to answer, he said the figure was £183bn.

PA says he seemed to be referencing a figure that covered spending for children and pensioners. It says:

double quotation markThe OBR forecasted total welfare spending to reach £352.7bn in the current fiscal year.

This figure falls to about £182.8bn when excluding certain spending on pensioners. This might be the figure Mr Burghart was referencing, however it includes £13.7bn of child benefit spending.

This figure also includes attendance allowance, which is a disability benefit paid to those of state pension age.

Separate data show the Department for Work and Pensions spending on benefits for working-age people is set to reach £136.1bn in 2026/27. This does not include welfare spending from other departments, such as HM Revenue and Customs.

Welfare spending rising £88bn under the Tories?

Haigh said welfare spending rose by £88bn in a decade under the Tories.

PA says this is close to what OBR and DWP figures say. But those are cash figures, it says. “In real terms, a measure which offsets for inflation, the DWP figure rose by a more modest £21.8bn,” PA says.

The UN refugee agency has said a government plan to make refugees wait up to 30 years before they get permission to settle in the UK “raises significant concerns”. Diane Taylor has the story.

Legislation aimed at ensuring more major sporting events are held in the UK is poised to become law after being approved by MPs this afternoon. As the Press Association reports, the sporting events bill is modelled on bespoke laws passed for the London 2012 Olympic and Birmingham 2022 Commonwealth Games and creates a framework for future events. It will support competitions such as the upcoming Uefa European football championship in 2028, in addition to future bids, such as the UK’s application to host the Fifa Women’s World Cup in 2035.

Lisa Nandy, the culture secretary, told MPs the bill would allow the government “to stand ready to host the biggest events in the world”, and added the UK had already bid to host the 2029 World Para Athletics Championships.

The bill has already gone through the House of Lords.

Andy Burnham tried his hand at playing the steel drum during his trip to the Caribbean, the Press Association reports. PA says:

double quotation markThe PM played Mary Had A Little Lamb while in Antigua for the Commonwealth heads of government meeting. Severin Carrell is the Guardian’s Scotland editor.

John Swinney, Scotland’s first minister, has rejected calls from MSPs for a review of laws which allow people with limited rights to live in the UK to stand for Holyrood.

Yesterday the standards, procedures and public appointments committee, chaired by the Scottish National party MSP Jenni Minto, said the reforms could undermine “fragile” confidence amongst voters in the country’s institutions.

Swinney told LBC he had no plans to review the provisions, which first came into force this year in the Scottish Elections (Representation and Reform) Act 2025. He said:

double quotation markWe don’t intend to do so, no. The parliament voted unanimously for the provision for foreign nationals to be able to stand for elections in certain circumstances.

The reforms came under intense scrutiny after the May election after the Conservatives and Reform UK protested about the election of Q Manivannan, an Indian citizen, for the Scottish Greens in Edinburgh and Lothians East region.

A post-graduate student at St Andrews, it emerged their student visa was due to expire at the end of 2026. They have since been granted a three-year graduate visa, given them right to remain until the end of 2029.

Swinney’s government is bidding to expand the provisions of the act by including Commonwealth citizens, who were excluded as a category last year. Swinney told LBC “the government will press ahead with that.” Manivannan only qualified because they were on a student visa, not because they were a Commonwealth citizen.

The act covers elections to Holyrood and Scottish councils; Westminster contests in Scotland are reserved to Westminster and excluded.

The Department for Work and Pensions’ report released today on the employment of disabled people (see 2.41pm) says that the financial year ending 2026 was the first for more than a decade where the number of disabled working-age people in the UK fell. It says it is too early to know if this was just temporary. It explains:

double quotation markThis (FYE [financial year ending] 2026) is the first full year since financial year ending (FYE) 2014 where the number of disabled people and disability prevalence have decreased, by 0.5% (around 50,000) and by 0.2 percentage points, respectively. It is too early to determine whether this is a temporary change, also as the latest quarterly data for April to June 2026 reports a year-on-year significant increase in the number of disabled people (250,000).

The Commons Treasury committee has started taking evidence from officials from the Office for Budget Responsibility about the budget. Harriett Baldwin, a Conservative member of the committee, asked about Shabana Mahmood’s announcement on Monday axing some of the restrictions to indefinite leave to remain (ILR) that had been planned by the Home Office.

The Tories claim this U-turn will cost the exchequer £10bn.

Asked what impact this announcement would have on the OBR’s fiscal assumptions, Tom Josephs, a member of the OBR’s budget responsibility committee, said the OBR would have to review this for its next forecast in the spring. He said he could not give an answer now.

Asked if he thought it really would cost £10bn, Josephs said “that is not an estimate that we produced”. He said that was a government estimate for the lifetime cost of some migrants.

Asked if the change on Monday would be "material enough” to require a change to the OBR’s forecast, Josephs just said the OBR would assess the policy as a whole. “If we think [the Monday announcement is] significant enough to adjust the forecast, then we will do so.”

Mahmood originally produced the £10bn figure to justify the plans she originally announced last year. She said making it harder for migrant workers to get ILR would save the government £10bn over time.

This claim backfired when Mahmood U-turned on a large part of her plan, leading the Tories to claim she was now in effect raising spending by almost £10bn (because the savings were forgone).

Some experts claim the £10bn figure for supposed savings was never realistic in the first place.

Just under half of young people not in work, education or training were classed as disabled in the year to March – a proportion which has more than doubled in just over a decade, the Press Association reports. PA says:

double quotation markThe government is currently working on reducing the number of 16 to 24-year-olds not in education, employment or training – known as Neets – with a review expected to be published in the coming weeks making recommendations for changes in the system.

The number of Neets topped a million earlier this year for the first time in more than a decade, fuelling calls for more help to get young people into work.

Government data published today showed 45.3% of Neets were classed as disabled in the year to March.

The Department for Work and Pensions said this was a rise of 24.2 percentage points since the proportion of 21.1% in the 12 months to March 2014.

The latest data showed disabled young people were more than three times as likely to be Neet compared to non-disabled young people – 30.2% compared with 8.6%.

Depression, bad nerves or anxiety was the most common main health condition reported by disabled Neets, accounting for almost a quarter (23.8%), followed by autism (22.7%).

Here is an initial take from Pippa Crerar, the Guardian’s political editor, on what the US election results might mean for the UK.

double quotation markWhile the public here in the UK - which overwhelmingly believes his presidency has been bad for Britain - may welcome the ‘blue tsunami’, ministers and officials will be watching nervously for how Trump reacts.

The US president acknowledged overnight the Iran conflict - which has pushed up the cost of living globally - had cost the Republicans votes and has previously suggested a deal could be struck with Tehran post-midterms.

But there are concerns here that a bruised Trump - whose domestic agenda is constrained after losing control of Congress but who still wields a veto and significant power over foreign policy - could now lash out like a caged animal.

There could be a backlash on the world stage as he tries to cement his legacy during his last two years, potentially including more military action in Iran, Ukraine and even Cuba; more trade tariffs; and more unhelpful interventions in UK and European politics.

But for now, there is also relief that the US has cast its verdict on Trump and his combative style of politics, and hope that if the Democrats get their act together (for this was a referendum on the US president rather than a ringing endorsement of his opponents), the 2028 election could transform the special relationship.

double quotation markI’ll be working for de-escalation. To make it very clear, we want to see a resolution, the Strait of Hormuz fully opened again. That’s what we will be working towards.

If you are deputising at PMQs, it may be nice to get a good write-up from the sketchwriters, and satisfying to craft a line that will play well on the TV or radio later, but, frankly, one task matters above all others: don’t mess up. And, in that respect, Louise Haigh and Alex Burghart both performed well – in exchanges likely to be quickly forgotten.

For Haigh, this was her first time, but it did not particularly sound like this. She sounded confident and authoritative, without being brash and hubristic. She did not have anything very new to say about welfare, and Labour does not have a firm answer yet to the question as to when it will cut the benefits bill, but Haigh swerved this quite adequately with attacks on the Tories’ record and by making the Labour argument about now wanting welfare spending on failure. She said:

double quotation markWe have been repeatedly clear that we cannot afford to pay for failure and must invest in success – and that is how we will bring the welfare bill down.

We will make support more conditional on taking up opportunities, ensuring young people get help they need earlier, tackling the long-term drivers of welfare spending, including high housing costs, and we will do all this in a Labour way.

She would not rule out tax rises to pay for more welfare spending (see 2.16pm), but that was the sort of question opposition parties ask when they can’t think of a proper, specific one and so instead have to resort to simplistic politics-by-slogan.

Burghart has done PMQs before, and he was a bit more polished. His pre-scripted riffs about “two 10-year plans in 10 months”, and John Healey’s Today programme welfare gaffe last week, were elegantly done, but not quite withering or funny enough to be truly damaging. He didn’t fail, but he didn’t score.

The most interesting question came from the Lib Dem deputy leader Daisy Cooper, who is probably the first MP to get a ‘Trump might be a rapist’ allegation into Hansard. (See 12.29pm.) Rather ingeniously, she tried to suggest there might be some sort of UK government angle. But there probably isn’t. This is one for Congress.

If anyone had a bad PMQs, it was probably Lindsay Hoyle. It was not clear who he was referring to when he delivered his ‘don’t shout a woman down’ bollocking to some Tory MP. (See 12.20pm.) But it sounded a bit chauvinist. Haigh is the second most senior person in government; she seemed perfectly capable of coping with a bit of heckling; and if shouting down a minister is unacceptable, it should not matter what sex they are.

Helen Maguire (Lib Dem) asks about conditions at Epsom hospital, where urgent repairs are needed.

Haigh says she is aware of the problems. The minister is happy to meet Maguire to discuss the situation, she says.

Munira Wilson (Lib Dem) says Haigh recently said the government would give local leaders more power to invest in things that matter. But it is cutting funding for Richmond council by more than £100m over the next four years, 60% of its budget? Will Haigh meet her to discuss this to find a solution.

Haigh says the government inherited an unfair council funding system. She offers Wilson a meeting with a minister.

Bobby Dean (Lib Dem) asks if the government will back legislation to make US tech companies pay fair compensation to victims of online scams. He says, given the US election results, the government should be tougher with the US administration.

Haigh says the Online Safety Act requires tech companies to take robust action on online scams.

#191. Leading ice hockey nations, sponsors oppose Russia's return

Ice hockey's governing body says the impact of a court decision forcing it to reinstate Russia presents "significant practical and sporting challenges." Several leading nations say they won't play against Russian teams.

The International Ice Hockey Federation (IIHF) said in a statement on Wednesday that the impact of last week's Court of Arbitration for Sport (CAS) decision requiring it to reinstate Russia to international play had been "significantly underestimated."

It added that it "maintains" its disagreement with the decision and that it remained "in dialogue with Member National Associations, organizing committees, host authorities and other key stakeholders to determine the best way forward."

Shortly after Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, the IIHF barred its teams from competing in international ice hockey tournaments, citing security concerns. In last Friday's ruling, the CAS said that the IIHF's concerns were "not supported by sufficient evidence."

Wednesday's statement comes after two days of talks by the IIHF Council aimed at charting a way forward following Friday's CAS decision, which appears to have caught most of the international ice hockey community off guard.

Two of the organizing committees the statement refers to are those of Canada, which is due to host the IIHF World Junior Championship starting next month, and Germany, which is to host the IIHF Men's World Championship next May. Both countries are strong supporters of Ukraine in its war against Russia, and both have expressed serious reservations about their upcoming tournaments in light of the CAS ruling.

On Tuesday, Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney ruled out allowing Russians to compete in the World Juniors in Alberta.

"While Russia and Belarus are supporting this illegal, unjustified ​war in Ukraine, as long as that's going on, there will be none of those athletes competing for their countries in Canada," he told reporters at a press conference in Antigua, where he was attending a Commonwealth meeting.

"So, ​if you're asking specifically about the World Juniors, the Russians will not be there."

Last weekend, Frank Lutz, chairman of the German Ice Hockey Federation (DEB) conceded in a television interview that following the CAS decision, the DEB would "also have to consider the option of handing back (hosting rights to) this championship."

Lutz was to discuss the matter with government officials in Berlin this week. A government spokesman said on the weekend that Berlin "takes a very critical view" of the ruling.

Meanwhile, the ice hockey association of Finland, the defending men's world champions, has been spearheading an effort to boycott upcoming IIHF tournaments should the Russians be allowed to compete.

"We are currently working to bring as many countries as possible into a united front to tell the IIHF that none of us will play against Russia under these circumstances," Finnish Ice Hockey Association boss Heikki Hietanen said in a statement released on the organization's website on Monday.

"Our stance is that we will not play against Russia as long as the war of aggression against Ukraine continues," added Hietanen, who is also a member of the IIHF Council.

Sweden, Norway, Denmark and Czechia have since made similar statements.

"The Three Crowns will not play Russia in coming world championship tournaments," Swedish Ice Hockey Federation boss Anders Larsson said.

This came shortly after Norway said it would not play Russia at the Men's World Championship and the Danish Ice Hockey Federation issued a similar statement on its website.

"Denmark will – based on the current situation and the information available to us today – not play against Russia at the World Championship in Germany in May 2027," it read. Denmark did not qualify for the World Juniors.

Czechia Ice Hockey Association President Alois Hadamczik confirmed on Tuesday that they too would refrain from playing Russian teams.

Latvia's federation said it “reaffirms its longstanding and firm position" not to play against Russia or its ally Belarus.

Alexander Helwig, chairman of the Austrian Ice Hockey Association told public broadcaster ORF that “as of now, we would certainly not play against Russia."

USA Hockey has yet to comment on the CAS ruling.

At the same time, sponsors of IIHF tournaments are also getting cold feet. Skoda, which has been the main sponsor of the Men's World Championship for more than three decades, told the Associated Press that it would "reassess" its position if Russia takes part.

"We do not support the Court of Arbitration for Sport's decision regarding the participation of Russian national teams in IIHF competitions. With Russia's aggression continuing, we believe the Russian national team's return to the world championship is incompatible with the values we have consistently upheld," the Czech carmaker said.

Skoda isn't alone either, Finnish tire manufacturer Nokian had said it would end its backing if Russia took part and Swedish furniture maker AJ Products, whose logo has been seen on the boards and officials' jerseys at World Championships since 1989, said Monday it would ask for its logo not to be shown.

On Wednesday afternoon, yet another sponsor joined the list, with German workwear company Strauss saying it would "forgo all logo exposure associated with the (men's) tournament" and was considering withdrawing its sponsporship entirely.

With the IIHF Council having failed to agree on how to proceed during its meetings on Monday and Tuesday, President Franz Reindl told Germany's SID news agency on Wednesday, that it would reconvene for further talks on Thursday.

#188. Husband who invited men to rape wife asked for an online review, court told

Nine men on trial in Manchester accused of either taking part in abuse or encouraging offending on internet

A husband who invited other men to rape and sexually assault his wife arranged for “more and more individuals” to visit his marital home as he resolved “to satisfy his own perversity”, a jury has been told.

The husband, who is in his 60s and from Stockport in Greater Manchester, has pleaded guilty to more than 60 offences against his wife. He cannot be named in order to protect the anonymity of his victim.

Nine men are on trial at Manchester Minshull Street crown court accused of either taking part in the abuse of the victim, or encouraging the offending online while “hiding behind the screens of their mobile telephones and computers”.

One of the “non-contact” alleged offenders, Daniel Rayner, 42, of Whitstable, Kent, repeatedly sought imagery of the abuse of the victim, the court heard. He told the husband: “Passed out ones are obviously better,” and: “Hope she’s being used tonight.”

The husband had shared imagery of other men, Graham Brougham and Sean Peers, assaulting his wife, and when Rayner asked: “So I guessing she had no idea he had stripped and fucked her,” the husband confirmed she did not.

Rayner then asked him: “What did you give her … I’ve always used GHB.”

Rayner denies conspiracy to rape, conspiracy to assault by penetration and conspiracy to administer a substance with intent.

Setting out a timeline of the allegations, the prosecutor Louise Oakley KC told the court that by the start of January 2025: “[The husband] doesn’t appear to have had any new year’s resolutions to stop his offending on his wife.”

“If anything, his resolve to satisfy his own perversity continued,” she said. “With arrangements being made for more and more individuals to attend.”

Two of the defendants, Philip Wild, 59, and Mohammed Sabir, 28, both from Stockport, had sexually abused the woman one after the other in a single night in early 2025, the court heard.

Oakley said Wild was “fully aware” that the woman was not conscious and not able to consent to any sexual activity. “He’d been there before so that was no surprise, and certainly was no deterrent the state he’d seen her in before,” she told the court.

Wild has pleaded not guilty to sexual assault, attempted rape, assault by penetration and conspiracy to rape.

Oakley told the court that Sabir also “knew before he went into the bedroom” that the victim was not conscious, and “at no point” did he attempt to communicate with her, or seek her consent.

The husband later asked him to write a review on a swingers website, the court heard, and the jury was shown the review, which read: “Really easy to set up a meet. It was my first [experience] fucking someone’s wife in front of them, so thanks for that. Hope to meet again soon.” Sabir denies rape and conspiracy to rape.

In May 2025 the husband made contact with another stranger on the site, the court heard. Jordan Wallace, 31, a building supplies employee from Wythenshawe in Manchester, said he was “pissed” and asked the husband to pick him up.

He wrote: “13 mins if [you] wanna pick me up [and] me [and] [you] will get perverted with your wife.” When the husband declined to collect him, he said he would ride his bicycle over to the address.

Wallace was “fully aware” the wife was unconscious, and “because of her state of consciousness she was not able to consent to any sexual activity”, Oakley told the court.

She said the husband “recalls that at one point Jordan Wallace ‘got rough’ with [his wife] and he had to actually push him away, saying, ‘What the fuck are you doing?’ telling him he was going to wake her up”.

Wallace later apologised but asked the husband to send the videos he had taken, which he did, the court heard. Wallace has pleaded not guilty to rape and attempted rape.

In June 2025, the husband subjected his wife “to a humiliating, degrading and sustained sexual assault” over the course of three hours, and again invited two men in succession into their home to assault her.

The second man, Alan Keelan, 42, an industrial cleaning firm worker also from Wythenshawe, had climbed on top of the unconscious woman wearing a pair of black women’s knickers and attempted to have sex with her, the court heard.

When he was unable to, he sexually assaulted her before climbing off the bed, the court heard. Keelan denies attempted rape and assault by penetration.

In July last year, the husband again posted on the swingers website, and made contact with the sheet-metal worker Richard Townsend, 38, the court heard. Townsend was aware the woman was not conscious when he proceeded to sexually assault her, assisted by her husband, the jury was told.

The woman’s leg moved, and “it was clear she was restless and stirring, so together they decided enough was enough for that night and he left”, the court heard. Townsend, of Heywood, Greater Manchester, denies assault by penetration.

Jonathan Kirk, 43, from Stockport, was described as “more cautious” than the other defendants, and first requested to meet the husband at his own house.

The husband visited Kirk at his house in August and showed him photographs of the woman passed out and being abused by him. He told Kirk he would consider drugging his wife, and Kirk, a paramedic, told him that “could be very dangerous”, the court heard.

Later, Kirk took a taxi to the husband’s house. “As a senior paramedic, he cannot have failed to notice she was not conscious. He is trained to spot that,” Oakley told the court. “The truth is, he knew and that’s how he wanted her to be.”

After having sexual intercourse with the woman, “Jonathan Kirk stood up and said, ‘fucking hell, wow’ and how great it had been before he left”, the court heard.

Two other men are also on trial. Robert Stewart, 70, of Stockport, denies rape, conspiracy to rape and conspiracy to administer a substance with intent.

David Graves, 60, of Ilkeston, Derbyshire, denies “non-contact” offences of conspiracy to rape, conspiracy to assault by penetration and conspiracy to administer a substance with intent.

Peers, 38, from Stockport; Brougham, 74, from Cheshire, and another man, Keith Fotheringham, 59, from Dundee, have pleaded guilty to offences against the same woman.

Karl Lindsay, 56, from Somerset has also pleaded guilty to “non-contact” offences. All will be sentenced alongside the husband, at a later date.

#187. HSBC's top execs face tense shareholders calling for a breakup

HSBC’s top brass defended their strategy Monday to frustrated shareholders in the lender’s largest market, as Europe’s biggest bank continued to face calls to be split up.

At an informal shareholder meeting in Hong Kong, Chairman Mark Tucker and CEO Noel Quinn took questions from investors on issues ranging from how the bank was approaching demands for an overhaul of its business to its purchase of Silicon Valley Bank’s UK arm.

In prepared remarks, Tucker and Quinn each reiterated the board’s recommendation that shareholders vote against a resolution on the docket for its annual general meeting in May that would force the bank to come up with a plan to spin off or reorganize its Asian business — the lender’s main source of profits.

HSBC's biggest shareholder reignites debate about breaking up the bank

Tucker said the board was unanimous in its opposition to the resolution, stating plainly: “It would not be in your interest to split the bank.”

He said the board had previously reviewed a range of options for restructuring the bank, and concluded that such alternatives would “materially destroy value for shareholders,” including dividends.

“Our strategy is working,” Tucker told the room of more than 1,000 shareholders. “Our current strategy is moving dividends up.”

HSBC has been facing calls to separate its Asian business from the rest of the bank over the past year.

Shareholders in Hong Kong — where HSBC is a mainstay of many retail investors’ portfolios — contend that the London-based lender’s performance has been dragged down by its businesses in other regions.

Quinn addressed those complaints head-on Monday, saying “our profits in Hong Kong and the UK are no longer being dragged down by underperformance elsewhere. The group is performing well as a whole.”

Pressed later by a shareholder on the issue, Quinn said a breakup of the bank would result in “significant revenue loss” because much of its business relied on cross-border transactions.

Investors have also been unhappy with HSBC scrapping its dividend in 2020, at the request of British regulators. They argue that if the lender cordoned off its activities in Asia, it would no longer have to expose Hong Kong shareholders to requests in other jurisdictions.

Christine Fong, a district council member in Hong Kong, said she represented about 500 small shareholders who had been affected by the dividend cancellation.

“Street hawkers, taxi drivers or teachers — they all relied on the dividend to pay for their regular expenses, like mortgage, insurance payments, school fees,” Fong told CNN.

“That’s why, three years ago, what HSBC did upset those small minority shareholders.”

Fong has now joined calls for shareholders to vote in favor of the proposal for the bank to spin off its Asian business, despite the lender bringing back its dividend in 2021, albeit at a lower level.

Ken Lui, an activist shareholder in Hong Kong who put the resolution together, doubled down on his call for support ahead of the meeting Monday.

The resolution will require 75% of votes to be passed in May, but “nothing is impossible,” he told reporters outside the meeting venue.

Lui, who said he personally held a stake worth 100 million Hong Kong dollars ($12.7 million), laid out plans for his team to focus on “targeted outreach to institutional shareholders to present our case and gain their support.”

His group will also canvass 18 districts of Hong Kong “to tell HSBC shareholders that they finally have a chance to speak for themselves and protect their rights through voting,” he added.

HSBC is also facing pressure from its largest shareholder.

Ping An (PNGAY), China’s biggest insurer, holds an 8% stake in HSBC and has backed calls for the bank to rethink its structure.

In a series of remarks made public by the Chinese firm last November, Huang Yong, chairman of Ping An’s asset management arm, said “we will support any initiatives including a spinoff that are conducive to improve HSBC’s performance and value.”

Since then, the insurance giant’s views haven’t changed, according to a person familiar with the matter.

The source told CNN that Ping An has been calling for HSBC to explore a reorganization, with an eye on boosting its valuation and simplifying its regulatory obligations around the globe.

The insurer has not recommended a specific path forward but will support any initiatives, including a spinoff of its Asian business, that could boost its stock performance or value, the person added. Ping An did not immediately respond to a request for comment on how it planned to vote at the upcoming general meeting.

HSBC’s leaders were also asked Monfday why the bank had scooped up the British unit of SVB following the stunning collapse of its parent in the United States. The purchase was made for £1 ($1.20) last month, just days after SVB had folded.

Critics have questioned HSBC’s ability to perform adequate due diligence on SVB UK’s customers because of how quickly the deal came together.

“Did HSBC look into the clients of SVB in detail? Say, the financial statement — whether they can pay back the loan?” said Fong.

HSBC buys SVB's UK business, ending 'nightmare' for British tech

Quinn and Tucker defended the acquisition, calling it a good business opportunity that allowed the bank to gain hundreds of innovative startups as customers. They pushed back on the notion that management hadn’t had time to carry out proper due diligence.

Tucker also weighed in on recent tumult in the banking industry, saying he did not expect an “immediate impact” on HSBC.

“After the collapse of a number of smaller regional banks and the takeover of Credit Suisse, the share prices of all banks have been suppressed,” he noted.

But he said he did not believe such developments represented “a systemic risk” to the sector. “I do expect a period of uncertainty” before nerves settle, he added.

Minister hints at restriction on selling technology ‘to certain users’ amid worries over surreptitious filming of women

The government is considering placing restrictions on smart glasses amid concerns the technology is being used to surreptitiously film women, a minister has said.

A weekly dive in to how technology is shaping our lives

Missing Chinese billionaire CEO assisting authorities in investigation, company says

In January, Wang Bin, former party chief and chairman of China Life Insurance, was charged by national-level prosecutors with taking bribes and hiding overseas savings.

#179. EU says Montenegro on track to become bloc's next member state by 2028

Brussels says Montenegro is likely to become the EU's 28th member state, followed by Albania and Moldova. It said Ukraine needed to speed up reforms and offered fresh hope to Serbia following elections last month.

The European Union could finish accession negotiations with Montenegro as early as the end of the year, paving the way for the Balkan country to become the bloc's next member in 2028, the European Commission said Wednesday.

"This ​is testament to ​Montenegro's sustained efforts to advance on ​the European ‌path and implement the reforms needed ​to turn ⁠EU membership into reality," the EU's executive said in its annual enlargement policy report.

The EU said Montenegro was the frontrunner among nine candidate countries currently seeking membership. The last country to join the bloc was Croatia in 2013.

"The significant progress achieved by some candidates over the last year allows us to turn the promise of enlargement into reality," European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said. "Step by step, we are building a more complete, stronger Union."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The commission said Montenegro, with a population of 630,000, must still implement a number of reforms, particularly in the areas of fundamental rights and financial services.

If negotiations are wrapped up this year, an accession treaty could then be signed in June 2027, the report said.

The treaty would need to be ratified by all 27 EU member states, potentially allowing membership to be finalized in 2028.

As part of accession negotiations, candidate countries must bring their national laws and standards into line with EU requirements in areas ranging from environmental protection to human rights.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The European Commission said in its report that Albania, Ukraine and Moldova were also making progress toward EU membership.

In its "roadmap" for the countries, it said Albania was roughly a year behind Montenegro and needed to continue reforming its justice system and tackling organized crime and corruption in order to finish accession negotiations by the end of 2027.

The commission said Moldova's aim of implementing the necessary reforms by early 2028 was also "ambitious but achievable" if the country continues to make changes at the current pace.

It praised Ukraine for making progress, despite the Russian invasion, but added that Kyiv needed to accelerate the pace of reforms to improve the rule of law and functioning of democratic institutions.

Progress has been slower in the case of candidate countries North Macedonia, Bosnia-Herzegovina and Serbia, the commission said.

But it also stressed that Serbia’s membership talks could get a boost if the student movement that beat longtime populist President Aleksandar Vucic last month forms a government willing to strengthen democracy.

"Now Serbia has the chance for a fresh start," Enlargement Commissioner Marta Kos told EU lawmakers on Wednesday.

"The new Serbian government has an opportunity to turn the page, strengthen democratic institutions and fundamental rights, and take the necessary steps to advance on Serbia's accession path," she said.

Kosovo hopes to join the bloc but has not been granted candidate status.

Meanwhile, Georgia and Turkey are considered furthest away from attaining membership due to democratic backsliding.
