#177. Debt relief and aid no replacement for slavery reparations, UK told

Leading figures in Caribbean reparatory justice movement speak out after UK PM announced infrastructure package

Offers of debt relief and development assistance from the UK cannot be a replacement for reparations for centuries of slavery, leading figures in the Caribbean reparatory justice movement have said.

Their comments follow British prime minister Andy Burnham’s announcement on Friday that he wanted to begin a structured dialogue on slavery and reparations and was open to Britain apologising, but ruled out reparations.

Instead, he proposed discussions on debt resets for smaller Commonwealth nations and announced £40m ($54m) for critical infrastructure in the Caribbean over the next three years.

As leaders of the 56-member Commonwealth bloc meet in a private retreat at their summit in Antigua, Ralph Gonsalves, a former prime minister of St Vincent and the Grenadines and a senior figure in the reparatory justice movement’s Repair Campaign, said that while countries in the region were likely to welcome Burnham’s offer, the Caribbean would resist any attempt to treat debt restructuring or development assistance as a form of reparation.

A 10-point plan drawn up by the Caribbean Community’s reparations commission, which outlines the region’s vision for reparations, specifically asks for “debt cancellation”– not debt restructuring – and “monetary compensation”, arguing that former colonies’ “existing debts are intergenerational and are ultimately and intimately tied to centuries of colonial extraction”.

“When I listened to Andy Burnham, and I listened to the king, and they’re talking about what is helpful is not reparations, but for them to work on specific things like debt relief, and education and health, and climate change infrastructure.

“Now, all those things are within the 10-point plan. But they are also within the sustainable development goals of 2015, which we all signed on to at the United Nations,” he told the Guardian.

He added: “There are things in the reparatory justice 10-point plan which deal with issues … the first one is the ecology, and then psychological repair. And for people like members of the Rastafari faith, or brothers and sisters who may wish repatriation, that’s something we have to work out with African countries, those who want to go back. But there are financial implications in that. So you’ll have to have a separate carve-out on the basis of the 10-point plan.”

Labour MP Bell Ribeiro-Addy, chair of the House of Commons’ all-party parliamentary group for Afrikan Reparations, said the question was fundamentally about “agency”.

Speaking at an event on Tuesday to launch a global reparations caravan, which will travel across countries to educate and engage people in the reparations movement, she said: “So when it comes now to reparative justice, and as we move forward in the movement, I have to be clear with everybody, it has to be about agency, not making deals here and there, because agency was the crime. When you think about what was taken from people of African descent when they were enslaved, it was their agency, their right to decide all of these different things about their lives.

“So when reparative justice arrangements are being made, it absolutely has to be made with those who were affected, because if it is done to us, it is not reparations. I don’t know what it is, but it is not reparative justice.”

Gonsalves said the danger of reducing reparatory justice to development assistance was that such assistance remained discretionary on the UK’s part. “We have to understand the specificities of reparatory justice as distinct from general sustainable development goals. They’re shaping the discussion only within the frame of sustainable development goals,” he said.

Prof Kingsley Abbott, a human rights lawyer and director of the Institute of Commonwealth Studies at the University of London, also stressed the importance of harmed communities having a decisive say in what reparations should entail.

Get the day’s headlines and highlights emailed direct to you every morning

Speaking at the global reparations caravan launch on Tuesday, he said: “It’s for you to say. It’s your story. It’s your lived experience. But it’s time to start listening to those who continue to suffer from those crimes even today. It’s time to listen to you and to listen and learn from you.

“I reflected on my work as a human rights lawyer in different parts of the world. And in that time, I’ve met with many different victims of serious human rights violations, atrocity crimes, and their families and their descendants in places like Guatemala, Colombia, Nepal, Myanmar. And here in Antigua tonight, I’m meeting with all of you.

“Wherever those victims of those terrible crimes and their families were from, and whatever their faith, whatever their ethnicity, whatever their beliefs, they all had one thing in common. When I met with them, they all said that they wanted justice. And they wanted not only justice, but they wanted to be made whole again.”

A spokesperson for the Foreign Office said: “The prime minister wants to build stronger relationships to renew the relevance of the Commonwealth in a new era. We will do that by facing our past together and focusing on a future where every person living within it has equality, security and hope.

“We are already committed to working with Commonwealth partners on issues facing the world, including the role of private finance in tackling global debt issues, addressing the climate crisis, and supporting economic development. We will continue to do so.”

#175. Myanmar’s Aung San Suu Kyi ‘sounds strong’ but is isolated, son says

Kim Aris tells the Guardian chance to speak with detained Nobel laureate was ‘public relations’ move from military leaders

Myanmar’s former democratic leader Aung San Suu Kyi appears to be in good physical and mental health but is living in near-complete isolation, her son has said after the first call with his 81-year-old mother in more than five years.

“She still sounds certainly all there mentally, and physically she sounds strong,” Kim Aris said. “I don’t think she has anybody outside of the military or the police around her, so essentially she’s been held in solitary confinement from what I gather.”

After being deposed in a 2021 military coup, the Nobel Peace prize laureate has been moved to an unspecified location in the capital. “I’m still not entirely sure where she is,” said Aris. “I think it’s a residence in Naypyidaw, which is fenced off and it has a scrubby bit of land around it.”

Aris, who has lived in the UK most of his life and whose late father was a British historian, was allowed to speak to his mother on Thursday at the Myanmar embassy in London. While he welcomed the chance to talk, he said the invitation was part of a “public relations” campaign by the country’s military leaders.

The former junta chief Min Aung Hlaing has sought to rebuild international diplomatic ties, and in April switched his military fatigues for civilian clothes after elections that were widely condemned as a sham.

Activists have long demanded “proof of life” for Aung San Suu Kyi, as well as scores of other political prisoners. In August, she was finally photographed during a Red Cross visit. Last week, a government spokesperson published images of her smiling in a wood-panelled room as officials delivered two gardening books given to her as gifts by Britain’s King Charles. A paper calendar on the wall behind her showed the date.

Aris said these photo-ops were well-known stunts that the regime had used before to pretend his mother, a national icon, was being treated fairly. The daughter of the assassinated national independence hero Gen Aung San has spent much of her life since the late 1980s in and out of house arrest for leading pro-democracy movements.

“She remains completely shut off from the rest of the world. I think she’s unaware of what’s happening outside of where she is because she’s not allowed any newspapers or radio or television,” Aris said. “She’s been locked up for five years on trumped-up, politically motivated charges and she should be free.”

Ruled for decades by generals and suffering from western sanctions, Myanmar experienced a brief democratic moment after Aung San Suu Kyi was allowed to run in a 2015 election that her party overwhelmingly won. She took up the role of “state counsellor” under a civilian-military power-sharing agreement.

Her international reputation was soon tarnished after the 2016-2017 mass killings and expulsions of Rohingya Muslims. Aung San Suu Kyi flew to the international court of justice in The Hague, where she said she acknowledged military units might have used “disproportionate force”, but defended the government against accusations of genocide.

Aris said that his mother was never “silent” on the Rohingya crisis and what she said on the issue “could fill volumes”. Under her leadership, Myanmar launched a domestic inquiry and appointed the former UN chief Kofi Annan to head a commission.

He added that as state counsellor, Aung San Suu Kyi was “always walking on a tightrope”, knowing the military could strip her of her fragile power at any moment.

“The very hard-won nascent democracy that she was trying to lead was holding on to things by a thread,” he said. “The military could have taken over at any point, and they were doing their best to make my mother’s situation a lot more difficult and to undermine her position. And I think in that respect they did a pretty good job.”

The 2021 power grab by the military triggered a civil war that has killed more than 100,000 people, according to conflict monitors.

Aris said the level of public global support for his mother had “dwindled incredibly” since the Rohingya crisis and that the whole country was now suffering from a lack of international attention. While many governments have called for Aung San Suu Kyi’s release, she is not treated as the beloved international figure that she once was.

“Never mind my mother, the fact that people ignore what’s happening in Burma shouldn’t be down to what was being done to the Rohingya,” he said, using the country’s former name. “And that seems to be the case sometimes, that people say: ‘Oh, well, look how the Rohingya were treated. We can ignore what’s happening in Burma.’”

#171. What Marco Rubio’s sidestepping of New Delhi says about the US’s stance on India

Rubio’s decision not to meet Narendra Modi last week was seen by some as a snub – but by focusing on business leaders and oil, the secretary of state made American priorities clear. Plus: the artist bringing a female perspective to men’s clothing

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

When the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, visited India for the second time this year, headlines concentrated on who he met and where he went.

The fact that Rubio bypassed New Delhi and did not meet the prime minister, Narendra Modi, during his trip last week, and instead traveled to the financial capital, Mumbai, where he met India’s foreign minister, Subrahmanyam Jaishankar (pictured top with Rubio), and business leaders, did not go unnoticed. Nor did his decision to travel to Jamnagar, the small Gujarati town that is home to Reliance Industries’ colossal oil refinery complex.

Bilateral ties between the two countries have come under strain under the second Trump administration, particularly over tariffs and India’s purchase of Russian oil. Yet, Rubio’s return to India just five months after his first visit as secretary of state signals that the country remains useful for president Donald Trump’s ambitions after all.

As director of the South Asia Program at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace, Milan Vaishnav has spent a decade tracking India’s place in the world, particularly its ties with the US. Vaishnav has described Trump 2.0 as “unbound” marking a new era in India-US relations.

Tracking foreign affairs, he told me, is a minefield right now. From the White House’s transactional approach to global alliances, ongoing conflicts in the Middle East and consequential elections around the world, politics is changing at a pace that means all predictions are off the table. “I think anyone who tells you they know exactly what is going on and how it fits into their neat theory of the world is lying to you,” says Vaishnav.

I asked him about the many confusing aspects of the US-India relationship, from the hostile political rhetoric to the US embrace of Pakistan, as well as Rubio’s latest visit.

Ahead of Rubio’s visit, several commentators noted the secretary of state’s “unusual” itinerary. Vaishnav thought the pre-visit chatter about Rubio skipping Delhi to visit Mumbai and Jamnagar was overblown. “Not every bilateral meeting has to take place in a national capital,” he says.

But that doesn’t mean it wasn’t noteworthy. “I do think the choice of venues was intentional. This administration and, particularly, this president, sees foreign policy through the lens of ‘deal-making’. And if you’re trying to drum up business deals, what better place to visit than Mumbai?” he says.

The Jamnagar visit, where Rubio met Mukesh Ambani, the chair of Reliance, also indicates some politicking. “At a time when petrol prices are extremely high in the United States and are likely to be a major issue in the 3 November midterm elections, the administration wants to show that it is doing everything it can to bring them down. India is a major consumer of oil, but it is also a major exporter of refined petroleum products,” says Vaishnav. “One of Rubio’s objectives was to encourage Reliance and, by extension, India to process more Venezuelan crude [oil],” he added.

While this could present an opportunity for India to advance relations, it’s not a given: the US president remains, after all – and to put it lightly – mercurial.

“The biggest plot twist for me has been the Trump administration’s embrace of Pakistan after the May 2025 conflict with India,” says Vaishnav. Pakistan’s standing in Washington had eroded over the years, especially under Joe Biden, who left office without ever having made a presidential phone call to his counterpart in Pakistan. “Many officials in Congress had grown exhausted with Pakistan’s double game, speaking the language of partnership on the one hand while undermining US interests on the other,” says Vaishnav.

But, after coming to power the second time, Trump thanked the country for its help in nabbing a suspect linked to a 2021 bombing in Afghanistan that killed 13 US service members. This reversal in Pakistan’s fortunes gathered pace after the May conflict with India, which Trump took credit for ending. In June, he welcomed Asim Munir, Pakistan’s army chief, to the White House, after Munir called for the president to be nominated for the Nobel peace prize.

“I chalk up the reversal to a few factors: Trump’s well-known strongman envy, Pakistan’s savvy diplomacy, and the prospect of energy and crypto deals involving members of Trump’s inner circle,” says Vaishnav. For him, it isyet another demonstration of how much weight Trump places on deals, transactions and short-term wins. “For New Delhi, this means Modi’s personal rapport with Trump and 25 years of investment in the bilateral relationship offer no guarantee against a 180-degree reversal in US policy,” he adds.

In a recent piece, Vaishnav’s colleague Sameer Lalwani pointed out that the US relationship with India remains more resilient than the political rhetoric may suggest with quiet, but nonetheless strategic, progress being made.

As Lalwani points out, bilateral trade reached a record high between India and the US in 2025; nearly $68bn worth of AI infrastructure investment in India has been pledged by US technology companies; and the two countries have carried out record number of joint military exercises this year.

Despite these wins, in terms of future bilateral relations, “there are at least three reasons for caution,” warns Vaishnav. First, he points to the onslaught of US actions that have dented Indian interests. Trump had imposed a punitive 50% tariff on India in 2025, which was reduced to 18% earlier this year, but without a comprehensive trade deal being reached. Second, on immigration, the White House’s H-1B visa and green card restrictions have disproportionately affected Indian workers in Silicon Valley.

“This has created a real trust deficit on the Indian side, and with good reason,” says Vaishnav. The frustration was on display earlier this month, when New Delhi branded remarks made by the US vice-president, JD Vance, about Indian professionals as “deeply offensive”. Furthermore, Vaishnav argues that while governments and private sector firms might be great at making announcements, they’re not always a guarantee of delivery.

His final caution is how political tensions can have a chilling effect. The trade negotiations offer a telling example: in January, the US commerce secretary, Howard Lutnick, blamed the lack of a trade deal on Modi’s failure to phone Trump, a characterisation that New Delhi has disputed. Vaishnav says: “Much of the progress we are seeing is the product of decisions made years ago. It is one thing to keep existing projects moving, but turbulence in the relationship could make new initiatives a heavier lift.”

If India is serious about making its ambition to be a global powerhouse a reality, Vaishnav thinks there’s a clearcut way to win. “I would argue that the most important investment is political – ensuring India remains a robust, inclusive, participatory democracy,” he says. “On the economic front, India has made massive new investments in physical and digital infrastructure. But the returns on those investments will be limited unless the government doubles down on reforms: more efficient dispute resolution, an impartial legal system, even-handed tax enforcementand regulatory certainty.”

Ultimately, neither side can afford to treat the other as expendable. The test now is whether strategic foundations can hold at a time of global political upheaval.

Student protests | Bikaner House, one of Delhi’s premier arts venues, asked visual artist Raihan Vadra to remove from his show more than 70 photographs that featured recent student protests. Vadra, the son of the Congress leader, Priyanka Gandhi, defended his work and also refused a request to remove images featuring the national flag. A recommendation from someone with great taste

Film-maker Ritu Sarin, director of the annual Dharamshala international film festival, which runs from 29 October to 1 November, suggests a film from this year’s lineup:

“Shot over three years on real locations, Anmol Sidhu’s sophomore feature, Flowers of Acacia, follows wedding orchestra dancers in rural Punjab with a gaze that allows all the large brutalities and smaller intimacies to shine through. Sidhu’s confident, consummate grasp of the medium is on full display in terrifying scenes that stretch to the edge of what is palatable. The camera refuses to look away.”

That’s the number of trees Mumbai’s municipal authorities authorised to be felled over five months this year, to make way for development works, according to the Indian Express. Of these, about 3,500 will be permanently removed, while the rest will be replanted elsewhere.

Prarthna Singh has consistently placed the female identity at the heart of her artistic practice, documenting Indian women’s aspirations and acts of rebellion in male-dominated spaces. When commissioned to photograph rkivecity’s menswear line, made from discarded clothes reworked into new garments, she turned that same gaze outward. The setting was the acclaimed 151-year-old Mayo College boys’ school in Ajmer, Rajasthan – Singh attended the linked girls’ school, and credits it with shaping her rebellious spirit.

“I was thinking about how I could bring a sense of intimacy and camaraderie of boarding-school life to the images, because that exists,” she told me. One image shows a pair of hands holding another boy’s jacket, the faces left out of the frame, so the garment stands in for the familiarity they share. Seeing this work against the backdrop of students taking to the streets to fight for their rights lends the series a lasting significance. “This was just my way of showing all of this is connected. These worlds are not existing separately,” said Singh.

#168. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#166. Tens of thousands protest against Narendra Modi regime in Delhi

Led by Congress party, India’s opposition alliance channels anger over removal of 130 million names from voter roll

Thousands of opposition supporters have gathered in Delhi as protests against the Narendra Modi government continue to gather momentum.

Led by the Congress party, India’s opposition alliance has brought together supporters from across the country, amid growing anger over a large-scale revision of the country’s voter roll that has seen 130 million names erased.

Opposition leaders have alleged the process gives Modi’s ruling Bharatiya Janata party (BJP) an electoral advantage, and have called for the resignation and impeachment of the chief election commissioner, Gyanesh Kumar. The BJP has denied the claims.

Addressing tens of thousands of party workers and supporters, the opposition leader Rahul Gandhi said Kumar had “carried out the biggest crime. He has tried to silence the voice of people, strangulate our country.”

Gandhi said the opposition, united under the acronym India (Indian National Developmental Inclusive Alliance), would not let “democracy be finished in the country” and warned Modi: “You can’t run anywhere.”

The protest was one of the largest shows of opposition strength in years, taking place amid a wider resurgence of anti-government dissent which has been systematically crushed over the 12 years of the Modi regime.

The biggest challenge to the regime has come from youth-led protests that erupted over the summer – led by a movement that began satirically calling itself the “Cockroaches” – and forced the resignation of one of Modi’s cabinet ministers. Cockroach-led protests hit the streets again last month, also in opposition to the revision of the electoral rolls known as special intensive revision (SIR).

The opposition, in particular the Congress party, has become emboldened in the wake of the youth mobilisation. However, a significant trust deficit remains between the establishment opposition parties and the Cockroach movement. On Sunday, while Cockroach leaders had extended their support to the opposition rally, they did not attend themselves.

Congress leaders had publicly predicted that a million people would attend the rally, but in the end the numbers were in the tens of thousands and empty seats were visible in the protest arena.

The somewhat lacklustre turnout demonstrated the challenge still faced by Congress and its opposition allies in being seen as a formidable and viable challenger to the Modi government.

The opposition, led by Gandhi, has lost the last three general elections to the BJP as the parties have widely failed to counter the BJP’s muscular Hindu nationalist rhetoric. The Modi government has systematically gone after political opponents, leaving little space for dissent.

The majority of those gathered at the protest site on Sunday were party workers and organisers who had been bussed in, and there was very little evidence of a mobilisation of the young people who have been participating in the Cockroach-led protests. According to the opposition, many of their buses were stopped from entering Delhi by police barricades.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Shalini, a restaurant owner from Goa, said she was there “not for the opposition but because I am against the BJP and how they are trying to remove any avenue of dissent in this country. I’m not particularly for any opposition party.”

She said many questions still remained about the viability and support of that opposition, but she sensed a “groundswell of dissatisfaction with the BJP and the opposition is cashing in on it – good for them”.

The issue of the revision of the electoral rolls first emerged last year, but recently erupted into a national scandal after an investigation by the Indian Express newspaper revealed irregularities and discord within the election commission over its implementation.

Millions of allegedly eligible voters have discovered their names have been left off the electoral rolls, with Muslims and minorities disproportionately targeted. This week, Human Rights Watch said the accusations raised “serious questions about the fairness of future Indian elections”.

Arwind Chaudhery, 48, said he too was not aligned with any party. “I am here because SIR has undermined our democracy and the situation has got so bad, it is beyond repair or rectification,” he said. “So it is natural that people will be driven out to protest and that is a ray of hope.”

The BJP have sought to dismiss the opposition protests as anarchy. The party’s president, JP Nadda, said Sunday’s rally would be the “nail in the coffin” for the opposition alliance. He said the protest was “not ​an act of respect for democracy. ‌It is a calculated conspiracy ​to plunge the nation into anarchy, ​all in the name of democracy.”

#164. What the Democrats' midterms win means for the US economy and Trump

From high oil prices and data centers to tariffs and mounting debt, economic concerns played a key role in the crushing defeat of President Donald Trump's Republicans. So can the Democrats now make a difference?

For the millions who cast their ballots in the US midterm elections, one issue ranked above all others: the economy.

So what does the Democrats' stunning capture of both the US Senate and House of Representatives mean for the economy and economic policy, given that President Donald Trump still has two years of his term to go?

Markets have rebounded after initially trending negatively. Global stocks rallied as investors appeared to take heart from the Democrats' victory.

With Democrats now holding comfortable majorities in both houses of Congress and Trump still in the White House, it could lead to gridlock in Washington. Trump has already said he would become "Mr Veto," meaning he would block any legislation the Democrats advance.

"The magnitude of the gains for the Democrats in the midterms does not change too much for the near-term policy outlook, given the party still lacks a filibuster-proof majority in the Senate and President Trump has veto power to block legislation," Stephen Brown, chief North America economist with Capital Economics, said in a note.

"I think we are looking at two years of complete 100% gridlock," Jacob Funk Kirkegaard, nonresident senior fellow with the Peterson Institute for International Economics, told DW after the results came in. "Nothing will get done of any kind."

Some investors appear worried that the Democrats could force through AI regulation, especially if they also win the White House in 2028. Rising opposition among large majorities of the US population to data centers — the key infrastructure behind AI — helped secure victories for many Democratic candidates.

Kirkegaard, who is also a senior fellow at Bruegel, a European economics think tank, said the vote showed the extent of that opposition.  Even if the Democrats can't force regulation, he thinks local opposition will make it harder for data centers to be built, which could hit the valuation of big tech firms that have benefitted from the AI boom.

"This election shows that the political underpinning of the rapid, aggressive AI growth story is shaky," he said. "It doesn't mean it's going to stop or be made illegal, but the dynamic of the growth story has shifted."

If the Democrats were to win the presidency in 2028, Brown said, they would be perfectly placed to make major policy changes. "That includes the potential for tax hikes on corporations and high-income individuals as well as regulation that could delay the AI rollout," he said.

According to polling conducted in early October by the Pew Research Center, 56% of Americans say Trump's tariff policies will have mostly negative impacts on the country, with only 20% saying mostly positive.

That sentiment clearly drove many Democrats to victory in the midterms — particularly in states where tariffs have had a particularly negative impact. That includes those with sectors reliant on exports, such as Texas, Michigan, Iowa and Illinois, where counter-tariffs have hit and those that have high trade volumes with Canada.

Yet, barring an unprecedented climbdown from Trump — who has been ideologically wedded to tariffs since he first became a public figure almost 50 years ago — it's not clear how the Democrats could force substantial change to the president's signature economic policy.

"Trump will not be moderated," Deborah Elms, head of trade policy at the Hinrich Foundation in Singapore, told DW earlier this year. "Expect him to continue his trade disruption and tariff policies for as long as he remains in the Oval Office."

Though the US Constitution gives Congress the power to collect and organize tariffs, various laws passed over the years have given presidents power to implement tariffs when they cite certain conditions, such as national security or economic emergencies.

Trump has already used such avenues for his tariffs and can continue to do so, despite some legal rebukes. However, while Congress can't necessarily force an end to Trump's existing tariffs, it can now take further legal action against them to prevent greater escalation.

Though Trump and the Democrats are clearly not going to make common cause, Kirkegaard said, the scale of the Republicans' defeat might force Trump to be less erratic in his policymaking, from escalating his trade war with Canada to his Iran policy.

Trump said in the aftermath of Tuesday's defeats that he would "work with" the Democrats and told the Financial Times in the run-up to the vote that he was unlikely to escalate the war in Iran.

Were a deal to be struck with Iran, soaring energy prices — a pivotal election issue — could finally fall, helping voters with their cost-of-living concerns.

"You've got to get a deal with Iran, and you've got to bring down gas prices," Kirkegaard said. "From a political point of view, that's the most important thing now."

George Saravelos, an analyst with Deutsche Bank FX Research, thinks the markets are beginning to become more optimistic in that area. "With oil flows through the Middle East having materially recovered, the market is pricing a large imminent risk premium of re-escalation and energy supply disruption," he said in a note.

Despite the scale of the Democrats' victory, the fact that Trump remains in the Oval Office will complicate all efforts to force a change to signature economic policies and issues.

Yet Congress can now exert considerable pressure. Another issue vital to voters was corruption and the belief that the Trump administration has engaged in corruption and personal enrichment while in office.

That raises the prospect of the new Democratic-controlled Congress launching legal investigations and potentially pursuing another impeachment of Trump, which could force the president to negotiate on some economic issues to secure his political survival.

"Can you find 10 or 12 Republican senators who, in the face of overwhelming corruption, would be willing to vote for articles of impeachment? I wouldn't rule it out," Kirkegaard said. "And that's a scenario in which Trump might suddenly be much more accommodating to, for instance, abandoning most of his tariffs."

Despite voters' clear rejection of Trump's economic policies, the Democratic Party's newly minted senators, congressmen and congresswomen face an uphill battle in trying to reverse them.

#162. UK ministers told not to engage formally with Indian mega-zoo after lobbying

Guardian and Mongabay reveal approaches to officials and MPs on behalf of Vantara zoo ahead of UK-chaired Cites meeting on endangered animal imports

British government ministers were told not to engage formally with Vantara, the controversial mega-zoo run by the Indian billionaire Anant Ambani, after lobbying efforts by the private animal sanctuary in the run-up to a UK-chaired meeting on the trade in endangered species, according to documents.

This week, the UK heads a 32-country committee meeting in Geneva of Cites, the United Nations body that regulates trade in endangered species. The meeting will examine how India has responded to compliance concerns about imports of endangered animals.

Documents obtained by the Guardian and the news outlet Mongabay under the UK’s Freedom of Information Act reveal Vantara’s efforts to lobby British officials and ministers before the meeting.

Ambani, 31, is the heir to the oil and gas fortune of one of India’s richest men, Mukesh Ambani. He set up a 1,400-hectare (3,500-acre) private zoo near his family’s oil-refining facilities in Gujarat, where more than 150,000 animals have reportedly been amassed since 2022. Vantara boasts high welfare standards for animals in its care, including facilities such as an elephant hydrotherapy pool, where more than 260 elephants can also receive oil therapies and Ayurvedic massage.

But conservation groups have repeatedly questioned how such a vast number of species, many of which are endangered, were gathered so quickly. Last year, India’s supreme court cleared the zoo’s operators of any wrongdoing.

A subsequent Cites fact-finding mission looking into the zoo raised concerns about India’s compliance with international rules on animal imports in 2025. It recommended temporary restrictions on India, although the recommendation was later withdrawn.

Its November 2025 report said: “There is a need for caution to ensure that imports of large numbers of animals by these facilities are not inadvertently creating a demand for illegally sourced animals.”

In May, officials from the UK’s Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra) met a representative from Vantara and the US lobbyist firm Checkmate Government Relations, founded by Ches McDowell, a hunting friend of Donald Trump Jr.

Before the meeting, internal briefing documents said Checkmate was “seeking to persuade the UK to take a more sympathetic view of India’s handling of imports to Vantara and of Checkmate’s argument that responsibility for verifying legality sits primarily with exporting or re-exporting parties rather than with India as importer”.

During the meeting, the Vantara representative invited UK officials to visit the zoo – an invitation that was subsequently declined to ensure the impartiality of the UK’s Cites chair role.

After the meeting, a representative from another lobbying firm, Atlas Strategy, working on behalf of Vantara, approached the then secretary of state, Emma Reynolds, and then nature minister, Mary Creagh, also raising the prospect of a visit to the zoo.

When a department official asked for advice, a note was issued recommending that ministers did not engage formally with Vantara.

The government documents also reveal concerns about an application by Vantara to import falcons and falcon hybrids, after an unnamed organisation made a £50,000 “donation” to a breeder in the UK, whose identity was also not disclosed. British Cites officials classified the transaction as commercial, according to the documents. The falcons were not exported to India.

A spokesperson for Vantara said it had not imported falcons from the UK, nor had the zoo hired or paid a lobbying firm. It is understood that Checkmate and Altas Strategy were working pro bono for Vantara and not required to register with the Office of the Registrar of Consultant Lobbyists.

After recent scrutiny, Vantara has sought to “reset” its activities by voluntarily suspending animal imports for a year and hiring John Scanlon, a former Cites chief, to oversee its operations. In an interview with the Sunday Times this year, Ambani said he only brought animals to Vantara legally to ensure their welfare, mentioning a group of hippos he had saved from a hunting lodge in South Africa and jackass penguins saved from a shopping centre in Qatar.

“I think since I was a baby I have been taking care of animals. It’s always been a passion,” he said.

Vantara was used to host Ambani’s lavish pre-wedding celebrations in 2024, which were attended by celebrity guests and politicians. In September, Newcastle University awarded the oil heir a professorship of practice for “his work in conservation, biodiversity protection, animal welfare and environmental stewardship”, sparking a backlash from conservationists.

A Defra spokesperson said: “The UK has strict rules in place to protect endangered species and ensuring international wildlife trade is legal and sustainable, with every application assessed individually against internationally agreed and domestic requirements.”

Find more age of extinction coverage here, and follow the biodiversity reporters Rebecca Ratcliffe, Phoebe Weston and Patrick Greenfield in the Guardian app for more nature coverage.

#160. Dogecoin jumps after Elon Musk replaces Twitter bird with Shiba Inu

Twitter’s traditional bird icon was booted and replaced with an image of a Shiba Inu, an apparent nod to dogecoin, the joke cryptocurrency that CEO Elon Musk is being sued over.

Musk addressed the change Monday afternoon, tweeting, “as promised” above an image of a year-old conversation in which another user suggested that Musk “just buy Twitter” and “change the bird logo to a doge.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

The doge logo appeared on the site two days after Musk asked a judge to throw out a $258 billion racketeering lawsuit accusing him of running a pyramid scheme to support the dogecoin, according to Reuters.

Lawyers for Musk and Tesla called the lawsuit by dogecoin investors a “fanciful work of fiction” over Musk’s “innocuous and often silly tweets.”

It wasn’t clear whether the logo change was permanent. Musk has been known to use Twitter to troll both his fans and his critics.

The price of dogecoin, which is typically volatile, was up more than 20% over the past 24 hours, to about 9 cents. It was trading just under 8 cents Monday morning.

Dogecoin was created December 6, 2013, by a pair of software engineers — as a joke. The name is a nod to the “doge” meme that became popular a decade ago. Its Shiba Inu mascot mimicks that meme: a dog surrounded by a bunch of Comic Sans text in broken English.

#157. The private creative world of Rabindranath Tagore, the man Yeats called ‘greater than any of us’

A previously unseen family archive offers a glimpse into the Indian Nobel prize winner’s artistic process and personal relationships in his final years

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

It’s easy to run out of words to describe Rabindranath Tagore. The first non-European to win the Nobel prize in literature, Tagore was a Bengali poet, musician, novelist, philosopher, songwriter, painter, humanist and educator.

Born in 1861, Tagore left an indelible mark on the subcontinent’s literature, music, education and art. Among his enduring legacies are the national anthems of India and Bangladesh.

As Nobel laureate economist Amartya Sen once wrote, Tagore was “a towering figure in the millennium-old literature of Bengal”, whose songs resonate across eastern India and Bangladesh, while his ideas on education, nationalism and freedom of the mind remain relevant today.

Yet his work also made a deep impression on the literary world far beyond the shores of India. WB Yeats is said to have described Tagore as “greater than any of us”. Gitanjali, the collection of poems that won Tagore the Nobel prize that same year, was praised by the committee for its “profoundly sensitive, fresh and beautiful verse”. Tagore’s English translation was published in London that March and reprinted 10 times by the time the prize was announced in November.

Tagore’s life and works have, of course, been well documented, but a collection of material preserved in a private family archive is now being brought into public view for the first time at an exhibition by Christie’s, opening in Mumbai this month. The archive draws from Tagore’s visits to the family home of his protege, Maitreyi Devi, in the bucolic village of Mungpoo in Darjeeling between 1938 and 1940. He died in 1941.

The collection on display – artworks, manuscripts, letters, photographs and personal effects – offers an intimate glimpse into Tagore’s creative process and personal world in his final years.

I spoke to the exhibition curator, Nishad Avari, head of south Asian modern and contemporary art at Christie’s, about the stories behind the archive.

One instructive document Avari points to is a manuscript from 1940. It appears to be a draft of Tagore’s memoir Chelebela or My Boyhood Days, in which he reflects on his life – from growing up in a large family in Calcutta (modern-day Kolkata) to the rapidly changing world ushered in by electricity and the motorcar.

For Avari, what stood out was how the ideas in the document echoed the educational philosophy Tagore put into practice in Santiniketan (or Abode of Peace), the experimental school he set up about 100 miles from the state capital.

“He speaks about how education is like being in prison and that it should be open and free … about the idea of wonder and even fear as inspiring creativity,” Avari told me.

“That’s a direct relationship to Santiniketan and the open model of the school where people sat under trees with their gurus and learned in the open. Education didn’t feel like a restriction, prison or imposition,” he added.

Avari points to another detail that makes this particular document valuable. Of the 10 complete manuscripts in the exhibition, it is the only one dictated to Devi, whose father was a close friend of Tagore’s. For Avari, this speaks to their close relationship and the trust he placed in her.

“If you look at the collection as a whole, she has copies of almost every single one of his publications signed to her by him,” he says. The collection includes hundreds of letters, too: first between Tagore and her father, and later between Tagore and Devi herself.

There are doodles among corrections, Avari explains, which show how his literary practice fed into his artistic practice. Alongside the manuscripts and correspondence are vintage photographs and personal effects, including a kurta and a lungi that Tagore left behind at the family home.

Among the artworks in the collection is a portrait of Devi, particularly notable because Tagore’s figure studies were not generally attributed to specific individuals in the way that portraits by his nephews, Abanindranath and Gaganendranath, often were, Avari said. There is also a landscape depicting the forests of Mungpoo, which Tagore embraced as a retreat.

On the international art market, Tagore’s paintings command significant prices. This year, Sotheby’s sold one of his watercolours for £1.02m (Rs 12.97 crore), nearly four times its low estimate.

Institutions such as Visva-Bharati in Santiniketan and the National Gallery of Modern Art hold much of Tagore’s archival materials, making the discovery of a private archive particularly rare.

He hopes that making these materials public will offer historians, scholars and admirers alike additional perspectives on Tagore. “We hope to add to the understanding of the artist, their career, and in this case, of course, our understanding of the man beyond his art as well.”

Rabindranath Tagore and Mungpoo runs between 10 November and 18 December at Christie’s space in south Mumbai.

Politics | India has invited Pakistan to attend the Shanghai Cooperation Organisation’s Civilisational Dialogue Forum in New Delhi later this month, the Indian Express reported. It is the first invitation since the two neighbouring states engaged in a military conflict last year.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Academia | Indian academic Mahmood Kooria, a lecturer at the University of Edinburgh, is among this year’s recipients of the Philip Leverhulme prize in Humanities. The £100,000 award recognises his work on the premodern Indian Ocean world.

A recommendation from someone with great taste

Freddy Birdy, a copywriter whose delightful words have earned him a devoted Instagram following, recommends writer and historian Malvika Singh’s cookbook, Cooking for My Firefly.

“This book isn’t about recipes, though it does include them. It is a voyage through another time and place, tracing the social fabric of India of the 60s, 70s and 80s, trickling down to today. Told in Malvika’s beautiful prose, it takes us to a time when people entertained in homes, not in restaurants; when dishes were cooked in-house and there were no caterers or party planners. An era long before Instagram posts, WhatsApp invitations, celebrity chefs and food delivery apps, when the point of having a party was simply to have fun. It is an archive of photographs, food, culture, gossip, movie stars, politicians, royalty, set against the spectacular backdrop of Malvika’s dining tables through the decades. I like to dip into it every now and then, much as one might do standing in a dark kitchen with the light of the refrigerator enticing you to midnight goodies.”

Indians lost Rs 22,495 crore (£1.77bn) to cyberfraud in 2025, Times of India reported citing data from the Home Ministry. While the figure is marginally down from the previous year, losses have risen sharply since 2023.

At a time when public anger has spilled into the streets, artist Aamir Aziz’s new track, Dopeher Blues (Afternoon Blues), captures the frustration and angst of a generation that has lived through many crises.

Aziz describes the song as an expression of a period of deep suffocation. Issues such as farmer suicides, unemployment and conflicts in Palestine and Kashmir, he told me, are things he has grown up with even as public attention shifts with the news cycle. It is also a warning, in part, against internet culture, where injustices risk becoming normalised.

Aziz, 35, works in acting, music and writing, and became an internet sensation with his defiant poem Sab Yaad Rakha Jayega (Everything Will Be Remembered) during the citizenship protests. At the heart of his new track is a note of defiance and hope: “We’ll find a way to sing,” he says, as he calls on everyone “to keep speaking up even in a climate of fear, misinformation and oppression.”

#156. Harry Potter films turns 25 ahead of new TV series' release

In 2001, "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone" hit cinemas and took the world by storm. Today, anticipation for the new series is high, but its creator, J.K. Rowling, remains a controversial figure.

On November 4, 2001, London's Leicester Square became a world of witches and wizards. The occasion was the world premiere of "Harry Potter and the Philosopher's Stone" (released in the US and India as "Harry Potter and the Sorcerer's Stone").

"Thousands of excited fans, some dressed up in wizard hats and cloaks, gathered to catch a glimpse of arriving celebrities," the BBC reported at the time.

Daniel Radcliffe, who played Harry Potter, was just 12 years old and clearly impressed by the spectacle. The BBC quoted him as saying, "It is so cool. I have never heard my name shouted so much in an hour."

The crowds showed just how far Harry Potter fever had spread in only four years.

The first Harry Potter book was published by Britain's Bloomsbury in June 1997, with an initial print run of just 500 hardcover copies.

About 5,000 paperback copies were also released around the same time.

Today, first editions are prized collectors' items, with hardcover copies commanding high prices at auction. In mid-October, one sold for $400,000 through US auction house Heritage Auctions.

At first, the book's popularity grew largely by word of mouth among schoolchildren. Media attention followed when it won the prestigious Nestle Smarties Book Prize later that year.

With an estimated 120 million-plus copies sold worldwide, it is the best-selling book in the series and ranks among the best-selling novels of all time.

For years, J.K. Rowling's public image was defined by her extraordinary rise from a single mother on welfare to a billionaire.

But in recent years, debate over her views on gender and transgender identity has increasingly overshadowed that success. It's an issue Rowling herself frequently weighs in on.

In an essay published on her website on October 21, 2026, Rowling reiterated her opposition to what she calls the "current, dominant strain of trans activism." She argues that this movement "holds that a man who declares himself a woman is, literally, a woman, and is therefore entitled to be treated as a woman in all contexts." Rowling argues that allowing trans women into single-sex spaces poses a risk. She describes biological sex as an indisputable biological fact.

Such statements have led critics to accuse Rowling of transphobia, while others regard her stance as a defense of women's rights and safety.

The controversy has also divided her fans. Some stand by Rowling, while others try to draw a distinction between the books and their author.

Others have left Harry Potter behind altogether. One former fan is German YouTuber Coldmirror, known for her Harry Potter parodies. The media artist from the Bremen area, whose real name is Kathrin Fricke, told the Süddeutsche Zeitung newspaper: "I don't have any love left for Harry. I'm still somewhat interested, but I can no longer watch it in good conscience." She cited Rowling's campaigning against transgender people as the reason.

The "Harry Potter" film cast is divided as well. The three main stars — Daniel Radcliffe, Emma Watson and Rupert Grint — have publicly spoken out in support of transgender people. Meanwhile, Ralph Fiennes, who played Lord Voldemort, and Helena Bonham Carter, who portrayed Bellatrix Lestrange, have defended Rowling's right to express her views.

Despite the controversy surrounding Rowling, Warner Bros., which holds the Harry Potter film rights, is bringing the books back to the screen — this time as a series for HBO. The premiere is scheduled for December 25.

The eight-episode first season is an an adaption of the first book. Even though the episode lengths haven't been announced, the scope of a TV series gives each book much more room than the original films did. That means more time to explore subplots, character development and everyday life at Hogwarts. While many fans are looking forward to a more detailed adaptation, others remain skeptical. They find it hard to imagine the familiar characters being played by anyone else.

The new trio consists of Dominic McLaughlin as Harry Potter, Arabella Stanton as Hermione Granger and Alastair Stout as Ron Weasley.

J.K. Rowling is an executive producer on the show. HBO says, however, that her views on gender identity won't be reflected in the storyline. Several people involved in the new production have publicly distanced themselves from her views. Paapa Essiedu, who has been cast as Severus Snape, signed an open letter supporting transgender people. Rowling subsequently said she had neither the power nor the desire to have him removed from the show because of it. John Lithgow, who plays Dumbledore, and Nick Frost, who portrays Hagrid, have also spoken out against Rowling's views.

Meanwhile, the Harry Potter merchandising machine is still going strong. On November 4, Britain's largest Harry Potter store to date is set to open in London. According to Warner Bros., the two-story shop covers more than 1,950 square meters.

This article was originally written in German.

#154. US strikes Iran’s Qeshm Island as Vance voices optimism about a deal

Strikes on eve of US midterms reportedly target surface-to-air missiles being used against US assets

The ⁠US launched limited strikes on ​Iran’s Qeshm Island late on Monday, hours after the vice-president, JD Vance, said he was optimistic that diplomatic efforts to end the war were moving in the right direction.

The strikes, disclosed by Iranians on social media and confirmed by US Central Command (Centcom), came on the eve of crucial midterm elections in which Donald Trump’s Republican party is expected to be punished by voters. Diplomatic mediators believe it will be clear within two days whether the basis for continued indirect talks between the US and Iran exists.

The US tried to downplay the significance of the attacks by stressing they were limited and not the precursor of a much-anticipated major assault on Iran that Trump has promised after the midterms if the US adjudges Iran’s nuclear program continues as a threat to the west.

The US described the strikes as defensive in nature, aimed at missile batteries from which Iranian attacks on US planes had been mounted the day before.

Inside Iran, blame was initially placed on Israel, but later it was conceded that the US was responsible. Israel itself insisted it had no role in the attacks. Similar military exchanges have happened at least three times since the cessation of major hostilities, as the US and Iran jostle for control of the strait.

Iran’s deputy foreign minister, Kazem Gharibabadi, referring to the overnight US strikes, said: “We are still in a state of war. The aggression against Iran has not ended in our view, and until the military aggression is completely and permanently terminated Iran has the right to legitimate defence.”

He added Iran was not waiting for the results from the midterms to adjust its negotiating position, saying: “We do not tie our foreign policies to the elections of other countries.”

Iran would like to see Trump weakened in those elections, but recognises the two-month interlude between the elections and the start of the newly elected Congress represents the point of maximum tension.

Chief negotiator Mohammad Ghalibaf made no direct mention of the attacks during an address to parliament, but said any miscalculation by the US would lead to an unpredictable Iranian response. In common with other Iranian leaders, he suggested the attacks would extend to US bases in Europe. Maps of Nato bases in Europe have appeared on the front page of the more conservative newspapers, implying they will be targets in any war.

The greater concern in Tehran appears to be the apparent inability of Qatari mediators to bridge the gap between Iran and the US over the upfront commitments Iran must make to end its nuclear program irreversibly, a phrase used by JD Vance.

It was Vance, in a letter to Mohsen Rezaei, the secretary of the supreme national security council, who proposed the restart of talks, but so far those talks have not led to any direct contact between the US and Iranian sides.

On Tuesday Rezaei appeared to reject the US response as inadequate, saying on X: “America’s response to Iran’s rights and demands was not convincing, and they must fully and explicitly address Iran’s seven conditions and act upon them; only in this case will the Strait of Hormuz be reopened.” The seven conditions include the unfreezing of Iran assets abroad, and the lifting of sanctions on oil exports.

Iran has offered to lift the blockade on the strait of Hormuz within two days of the US lifting economic constraints on Iran. Once those two days elapse, Iran would also enter into talks about its nuclear program, but Tehran has refused to give upfront concessions either about the return of UN inspectors to visit Iran’s bombed nuclear sites or to agree to ship the 400kg of highly enriched uranium to a third country. The most likely compromise is a down-blending of the stockpile inside Iran under international supervision, and with an external guarantor, such as China.

Esmail Baghaei, the foreign ministry spokesman, struck a pessimistic note saying the US attitude to Iran’s nuclear program was “the biggest and most costly lie of the century”, and was fundamentally based on having a problem with Iran as an idea and historical entity.

Qatar said its mediation was continuing, and there was a very high level of coordination by countries in the region. Most of these Gulf states know they will be attacked by Iran if the US mounts an assault, and are desperate to avoid further damage to their fraying economies. Majed al-Ansari, the spokesman for the Qatar foreign ministry, insisted both sides remained committed to the process.

Speaking to Fox News hours before the polls opened in the US on Tuesday, Trump continued to play down the significance of the conflict and its effect on the US economy.

“There’s no war raging. It could happen if they don’t make a deal, but they want to make a deal very badly and there’s a very good chance we make a deal.”

However, he said the US might still “have to go after Pickaxe Mountain” – referring to a fortified underground site in central Iran that is suspected to be a nuclear facility – but said it would not be a “big deal” and could be done in “one hour”.

Speaking from Washington in his closing message of the midterms campaign, Trump hailed a sharp drop in oil prices, as his administration touted renewed diplomatic efforts being made to end the war. Rising petrol prices caused by the war have become a signature issue of the midterm elections.

#153. Africa's shifting role in the global drug trade

Recent discoveries of meth labs suggest parts of Africa are moving beyond drug transit and becoming sites of synthetic drug production.

For years, Africa was primarily known as a transit point for international drug trafficking networks.

But recent drug seizures and laboratory discoveries show that illicit drugs are no longer only passing through African countries — they are increasingly being manufactured there.

Recent discoveries of methamphetamine laboratories in countries such as Nigeria, South Africa, Kenya and Mozambique have fueled concerns that Africa is becoming a significant production hub for synthetic drugs.

According to the UN Office on Drugs and Crime (UNODC), synthetic drug production increasingly involves transnational criminal networks and clandestine laboratories capable of supplying both local and international markets.

The debate gained new urgency this year after Nigeria's National Drug Law Enforcement Agency (NDLEA) dismantled what the agency described as the largest clandestine methamphetamine laboratory ever uncovered in the country.

The operation in Nigeria's southwestern Ogun State led to the arrest of 10 suspects, including three Mexican nationals whom the NDLEA said had been brought into the country to help manufacture methamphetamine.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

NDLEA's chairman, Mohamed Buba Marwa, said officers seized more than 2.4 metric tons of methamphetamine, precursor chemicals and other materials with an estimated international market value of about $363 million (€312 million).

The scale of the operation attracted widespread attention in Nigeria.

"It is no longer something you only see in movies," said DW correspondent Olisa Chukwumah. "The discovery raised serious questions about the level of local consumption if production is now happening in Nigeria."

Chukwumah said Nigerian authorities have uncovered meth laboratories before, but the size and sophistication of recent operations suggest that drug manufacturing networks are becoming more deeply established.

Dokun Adedeji, co-founder of the Compassionate Care Recovery Initiative, said Nigeria's drug landscape has evolved significantly over the past decade.

"We used to think we were only a transit nation," he explained. "Then we became a consumer nation. Now we are discovering that we are also a production nation."

He noted that authorities have uncovered dozens of smaller laboratories over the years, with meth production sites identified in multiple states.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

According to the UNODC, increasing local drug consumption and the expansion of illicit drug markets have coincided with the growth of synthetic-drug production in West Africa.

Adedeji described the impact of drug laboratories as "devastating and destructive."

He said substance abuse is contributing to school dropouts, family breakdowns, mental health challenges and rising criminal activity. Many families struggle to seek help because of stigma and fear of public judgment.

"We see families suffering every day," he said. "Many people are unable to speak openly about addiction because of concerns about reputation and discrimination."

The UNODC says young people are increasingly affected by drug-use disorders, with 34% of people receiving treatment for such disorders in Africa under the age of 25.

In addition to traditional drugs such as cannabis, users are increasingly turning to synthetic substances, including methamphetamine, often known on the streets as "ice." There is also growing misuse of prescription medications and reports of experimentation with dangerous inhalants and other toxic substances.

Nigeria's National Drug Law Enforcement Agency has intensified enforcement efforts in recent years, leading to a series of high-profile drug seizures and arrests.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

NGOs have increasingly stepped in to fill the gap through awareness campaigns, particularly on university campuses where drug use is becoming more visible.

Chukwumah said there is still no coordinated, top-down policy approach and that much of the awareness work is being carried out by civil society groups.

Adedeji argued that policies based solely on punishment often drive users underground, making it harder for authorities and healthcare providers to identify and support people struggling with addiction.

UNODC and civil society groups advocate a broader response that extends beyond law enforcement.

Among the proposals being discussed are stronger national policies on substance abuse, expanded rehabilitation services, workplace drug prevention programs and increased regional cooperation across West Africa.

This article has been adapted from an episode of DW's AfricaLink podcast.

#152. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

China Daily said that the agreement was part of “the rising global use of the Chinese renminbi.” It would reportedly enable China and Brazil to conduct trade and financial transactions using local currencies instead of the dollar.

#149. Trump claims US will end military aid to Israel by end of this year

US president makes surprise election day announcement, saying country does not ‘need’ $4bn annual payment

Donald Trump has said that the US will end military aid to Israel at the end of this year in a surprise election day announcement that appeared to be targeted at voters sceptical of US support for Israel and the war in Gaza.

In a phone interview with Fox News as voting began on Tuesday morning, the US president said the US would cut nearly $4bn (£2.96bn) in military aid to Israel because “they don’t need it”.

“We were giving them $4bn … But it gets cut off at the end of the year,” he said.

Trump also indicated that he had decided to end the aid – which he claimed would be replaced by arms sales to Israel instead – because it had become a popular criticism of US foreign policy led by outspoken candidates including the Michigan Democrat Abdul El-Sayed.

“Why should we suffer the slings from lunatics like El-Sayed when they don’t need it?” Trump said. “It’s a bad talking point.”

During the interview, Trump claimed that the abrupt ending of aid was proposed by Israel’s prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu. “We give them $4bn of things and money, and they don’t need it,” he said. “And I was approached by Bibi Netanyahu, and we’ve been talking about it for a couple of years because it’s a lot of money.”

Netanyahu had previously suggested tapering off US military aid to Israel over the next decade, but Trump’s announcement on Tuesday would significantly accelerate that timeline.

Israeli officials appeared to be taken by surprise by the announcement, and leading members of the opposition said it showed the damage that Netanyahu had done to the US-Israel relationship. The announcement comes at a crucial moment after Netanyahu’s party lost its majority in the Knesset in last week’s elections and the opposition is trying to cobble together a coalition to replace him.

“The fact that the security establishment, along with Israeli citizens, is learning about a change in the strategic relationship framework through the media highlights just how much Benjamin Netanyahu’s conduct poses a genuine danger,” said Gadi Eisenkot, a centre-right rival to Netanyahu who is seeking to replace him as prime minister. “We can no longer rely on him and must replace him.”

“A responsible prime minister would have fought to increase American aid, not dismantle it,” wrote Yair Golan, the head of the Democrats opposition party. “Netanyahu is deepening the security and economic crisis, putting Israel’s future at risk.”

Israel received most of the approximately $3.8bn in 2025 as foreign military financing grants that allow the country to buy US military hardware including F-35 fighter jets. Another $500m was given for joint missile defence programmes.

Get the most important US headlines and highlights emailed direct to you every morning

Trump’s announcement on Tuesday appeared timed to affect the outcome of the midterm elections, in which US foreign policy and support for Israel have played an unusually important role in key races, including the Michigan senatorial race where El-Sayed is running against Republican Mike Rogers. El-Sayed would become the country’s first Muslim senator if he is elected.

A Quinnipiac University poll released in September showed that 48% of Americans believed that the US was too supportive of Israel, and other recent polls have shown a historic shift away from US support for Israel and Netanyahu’s government.

Anger over the humanitarian crisis in Gaza has helped usher in a wave of progressive Democratic candidates including El-Sayed, and Trump’s vice-president, JD Vance, has openly accused Israel of trying to sabotage US negotiations with Iran. In a June interview with the New York Times, Vance said of Israel’s foreign policy: “You can’t just kill your way out of solving every single national security problem that you have.”

During his Fox News interview, Trump spoke warmly about Netanyahu and also boasted about his own popularity in Israel. “If people ever got tired of me in the US, I’ll move to Israel and become prime minister,” he said.
