#123. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

Meanwhile, Ukraine’s president Volodymyr Zelenskyy has landed in Spain, where he is expected to take part in talks with key leaders from Spain and Latin America, as well as the UN secretary-general and the European Council president, according to Spain’s El País newspaper.

The paper reported that Zelenskyy will take part in “several high-level meetings” on the sidelines of the Ibero-American summit in Madrid, hosted by Spain’s embattled prime minister Pedro Sánchez.

Argetina’s controversial president Javier Milei is believed to be among the guests. Portugal will be represented by PM Luís Montenegro.

Just like in Berlin, Zelenskyy’s trip was kept secret until his plane touched down for security reasons, El País said.

In other news, France’s national blood service has said hackers accessed control systems and boosted temperatures at three cold stores stocking vital blood products, in an apparent escalation of recent cyber-attacks on fridges and freezers across Europe.

The French blood service (EFS) said temperature and defrosting thresholds had been raised to “very high levels” in the attack, adding that its alarm systems had allowed technicians to intervene before the products were compromised.

The EFS is France’s public blood transfusion operator, collecting, testing, preparing and distributing products such as blood, plasma and platelets to more than 1,900 hospitals around the country. It supports more than a million patients a year.

The attack, which EFS said had occurred overnight on Monday, came as the temperatures of refrigerators and freezers in supermarkets in almost a dozen European countries soared as high as 50C (122F), causing millions of euros of food damage.

#120. 36 years later: Hungary opens Communist-era secret service files

Decades after the end of Soviet rule, Hungary is releasing all its secret service files. The disclosure is sparking fresh debate about informants, guilt and responsibility.

On November 4, 1956, tens of thousands of Soviet soldiers and tanks swept into Hungary, brutally suppressing the country's anti-Communist uprising. Decades of dictatorship followed, relying on Hungary's brutal secret police to keep the system in place.

Events on Nov. 4 this year represent something of a reckoning with this dark chapter of Hungarian history.

Prime Minister Peter Magyar's government is ordering the full release of the country's secret police "agent files," which contain the names of Hungarian state security informants, some of whom cooperated out of their own volition even as others passed on information unwittingly.

Magyar and his Tisza party had previously promised to make the "agent files" public if elected. Their release is an historic event, as it comes after over three decades of heated debate over how to handle such sensitive files. The decision to publish is also sure to spark further debate on Communist-era guilt and responsibility.

Hungarians have been calling for the release of the files for years. After the fall of the Berlin Wall in 1989 and the end of Soviet rule, civil rights activists and smaller Hungarian political parties said the files should be released. Demands for their publication were expressed repeatedly over following decades.

Yet Hungary's first post-Communist prime minister Jozsef Antall, a conservative, rejected the idea as did Viktor Orban's long-ruling Fidesz party. The latter blocked efforts to open the files, voting more than 30 times against such plans since 2010, according to Hungarian news platform Telex.

A partial release of state security files did occur in the 1990s, when the Hungarian parliament passed the so-called "agents law" in 1994. This required members of the government, members of parliament and certain public servants to undergo background checks to determine whether they had ever worked for the former state security services.

From 1997, Hungarian citizens have been able to view their own files and in 2003, permissions were expanded. But they have never been able to find out the names of informants.

The law relevant to the files contained provisions that were open to broad interpretation, which allowed government agencies such as the Ministry of Interior to prevent the transfer of files from the secret police to the Historical Archives of the Hungarian State Security, or ABTL, if they believed it would compromise  national security.

Such a selective release of files not only prevented Hungarian society from learning the truth about its state security service, it also made it possible for files to be exploited in political power struggles, for example, by leaking compromising information about politicians to the public.

One such case involved former Hungarian Prime Minister Peter Medgyessy, who was exposed by the Fidesz-affiliated daily Magyar Nemzet in 2002 for having worked as a Communist-era secret agent.

Magyar's government is putting an end to this. From Nov. 4, databases containing all the names of, and key information about, informants will be made available online.

Additional databases will be released as well. They include one of the most extensive databases on individuals recruited to work for the Hungarian state security agency. Confidentiality provisions set out in the old "agent law" have also been scaled back, meaning significantly more files will likely be accessible in the future.

The upcoming release of data on up to 60,000 out of an estimated 150,000 secret police informants could have major political consequences. Even so, ABTL director Bendeguz Gergo Cseh recently cautioned that, "you can't believe everything the state security agencies and their files claim." Many documents were incomplete and not fully reliable and many files had been destroyed, he said.

Others are also skeptical about the release. Historian Janos M. Rainer, an expert on Hungary's dictatorship, told Telex that the publication would have made sense right after the fall of the regime. Releasing the files after all these years is more of a symbolic gesture, he argued.

Meanwhile historian Krisztian Ungvary, who has researched the state security files extensively, welcomes the upcoming publication. It will allow historians to pursue new research and society to engage in fresh debates, Ungvary told DW. He believes Hungary will benefit from such reflection, as "societal lies inevitably lead to a strained relationship with politics and the price we paid for that was 16 years of Fidesz rule."

For Magyar, making this information public carries great significance. The idea of coming to terms with the past, especially the years of the Orban regime, is one of his favorite topics.

When the Hungarian parliament amended the law about the files in mid-September, Magyar wrote on Facebook that, "after a 36-year delay, the Hungarian parliament today decided, at the initiative of Tisza, to make the 'agent files' public. Fidesz did not support the motion. I wonder why?"

It was an allusion to the fact that many prominent figures in Fidesz' ranks and in Orban's inner circle were members of the Hungarian Communist party or secret police.

The most well-known of the latter is likely Jozsef Horvath, a former full-time employee of the department of Hungarian state security that was responsible for suppressing the domestic opposition.

Under Orban, Horvath worked as an intelligence officer and security advisor, and in 2024, he became the research director of Orban's Sovereignty Protection Office. This was an agency that exposed supposed enemies of the Orban regime, branding them " foreign agents ."

This article was originally published in German.

#117. Winners, losers, the EU: Five takeaways from the US midterms

Donald Trump's Republicans suffered a crushing defeat in Tuesday's midterm elections, with the Democrats wresting control of Congress. What does this mean for US politics and the world?

The Democrats not only made significant gains in Tuesday's midterm elections but also took control of both chambers of Congress. Here are five insights that can be drawn from this moment.

The storming of the Capitol, attempts to change the rules of the electoral process, a spate of presidential executive orders to bypass Congress and many more examples sparked concerns that the US' vaunted democracy was being transformed into an authoritarian system under Donald Trump. But the country's democratic institutions have proven more resilient than many had feared.

Many voters were driven by their opposition to Trump's desire to rule with unfettered power. Instead they want to see country's institutional safeguards strengthened.

A  post-election survey by news agency Associated Press found that about 30% of voters thought the state of democracy was the country's biggest problem. About 70% felt that Congress was not doing enough to provide checks and balances on the president.

This midterm result sends a clear signal that voters want stronger parliamentary oversight. President Trump could of course continue trying to govern through executive orders, bypassing Congress. But Congress sets the budget.

This means the Democrats, who now control Congress, can deny the president the funds he needs for his political initiatives. They can also set up investigative committees, which puts the Trump government under considerable pressure to justify its actions.

For a long time, the Democratic Party lacked compelling leadership, a unified approach and a clear answer as to how it intends to win votes. Tuesday's election proves the party is still a political force to be reckoned with.

Its gains in many Republican strongholds are nothing short of sensational. The party was even able to score victories in Texas and South Carolina. In Kansas, the Democrats won a Senate seat for the first time since 1932.

The midterms have also put the spotlight on a new generation of Democrats: Younger politicians, some of them noticeably more progressive, who could chart a fresh course for the party.

Take, for instance, the Democratic Socialists of America, or DSA, an organization which is considerably more left-wing than the Democratic Party itself. At least five candidates endorsed by the DSA have been elected to the House of Representatives.

The DSA advocate for strong welfare policies that focus on affordable housing, health care and workers' rights. They also want to see greater checks on the power of large corporations.

"The argument was always that these folks can't win on a national stage," Brandon Bohrn, an expert on trans-Atlantic relations at the Bertelsmann Foundation, told DW. Now some of them have defied the odds and actually won, potentially sparking an internal debate about what the Democrats stand for and how they plan to win voters in the 2028 presidential election, Bohrn said.

Donald Trump has already made it clear that he does not hold himself responsible for this midterms defeat, taking to Truth Social to deflect any blame.

Yet not everyone in the Republican party is likely to agree. Critics of Trump who had been sidelined may feel emboldened to speak up again. The question as to whether the Republicans should abandon Trumpism will be almost impossible to ignore.

By law, Donald Trump cannot run for a third term in the 2028 presidential race so Republicans will have to think about who can. But who will take the lead? And more importantly: On what policy platform?

Tuesday's election defeat will likely accelerate this debate, making a power struggle over the Republicans' political future inevitable.

The war against Iran and resulting economic hardships have cost the Republicans many votes. The cost of living, in particular, was a key issue for many American voters.

The consequences of the Iran war have primarily driven up energy prices. This is likely to significantly increase domestic pressure on Trump to reach a lasting agreement with Iran.

Trump does seem keen to cut foreign policy costs, especially in areas where it has been politically controversial for his party. Shortly before the midterms, he announced that he no longer intends to provide billions in military aid to Israel. This was in relation to a deal that was meant to expire in two years anyway.

Ian Lesser, an expert on US foreign and security policy at the German Marshall Fund, believes that the midterm elections could also impact trans-Atlantic relations. "This actually makes a big difference for Europe in a number of different important ways," Lesser told DW.

The Democrats could try to reinvigorate dialogue between Europe and the US, Lesser suggests. After all, with control of Congress, the Democrats have more say on Trump's NATO policy, can influence budget decisions and military funding, and can complicate planned cuts to US support. Europe and the US may even cooperate on climate and environmental issues.

"Simply restoring a degree of trust and predictability, however we get to that, makes a big difference," Lesser said.

Not all differences will be resolved overnight though.

"Tariffs are a little tricky because there are clearly vested interests in the US in keeping some of this in place," Lesser concluded. "But in terms of new and very aggressive tariff measures, I think Congress would absolutely push back against that, on both sides of the aisle, actually."

This article was originally published in German.

#115. Tinto Brass, director of Caligula and maestro of Euro erotica, dies aged 93

The cult Italian film-maker began as an assistant to Roberto Rossellini but moved into sex films, believing ‘eroticism to be a fundamental key to human expression’

Italian film director Tinto Brass, who was once hailed as the “Antonioni of the 70s” but came to be better known for bringing Italian erotic cinema to a wider audience with films such as Caligula and Salon Kitty, has died aged 93.

His wife Caterina Varzi and children Bonifacio and Beatrice Brass announced the news of the director’s death to Italian press agency Ansa on Wednesday.

Born in Milan and raised in Venice, Brass first started working in cinema after moving to Paris, where he was an editor and archivist at the Cinémathèque. Upon returning to Italy, he worked as an editor and assistant director to Roberto Rossellini.

His experimental and anarchic approach to film quickly earned him a reputation, and in 1964 he was commissioned by Umberto Eco to create two short films experimenting with visual language for the Triennale di Milano. Warner Bros reportedly approached him to direct A Clockwork Orange, though scheduling conflicts meant the film was offered to Stanley Kubrick instead.

In the 1970s, Brass moved on to erotic cinema. Though crass and filled with nudity, his first steps in the genre were also politically minded explorations of the relationships between sex, power and money.

“I made so many erotic films because I believed eroticism to be a fundamental key to human expression and, above all, the key to portraying women,” he told La Repubblica newspaper. “That was what interested me most.”

His 1976 “Nazisploitation” film Salon Kitty was based on the true story of a Berlin brothel co-opted by Nazis to spy on foreign allies and fellow officers. It caught the attention of producer and Penthouse founder Bob Guccione, who hired Brass in 1979 to direct Caligula, which was an attempt to make a star-studded, mainstream pornographic film, with a plot based on the story of ancient Rome’s most feared ruler.

Starring Malcolm McDowell in the title role, alongside Helen Mirren, Peter O’Toole and John Gielgud, the film was based on a screenplay by Gore Vidal, which Brass rewrote to include orgies, decorative phalluses, and plenty of female nudity.

Vidal disavowed the film, as did Brass after Guccione shot additional unsimulated hardcore sex scenes and spliced them into the film without the director’s permission. It was released in the UK and US in 1980, with Brass only credited for “principal photography”. A recut version, without Guccione’s inserts, was released in 2023, although Brass was not involved and did not approve of it.

In recent years Brass lived in Isola Farnese, on the outskirts of Rome. He is survived by his wife, Varzi, whom he married in 2017 and who curated his archive. In an interview on the occasion of his 90th birthday, he expressed a wish for his ashes to be scattered in the Venice lagoon.

→ Baca artikel asli

#114. Bavarian politician Hubert Aiwanger found dead in Berlin hotel

Aiwanger was Bavaria's Economy Minister and the chairman of the right-wing populist Free Voters party. The circumstances surrounding his death are still unclear.

Bavarian politician Hubert Aiwanger was found dead in a hotel in Berlin on Wednesday, the German capital's public prosecutor's office said.

Aiwanger was Bavaria's deputy premier, economy minister and the chairman of the right-wing populist Free Voters party. He was 55 years old.

The circumstances surrounding Aiwanger's death are still unclear. Police and prosecutors in the German capital have launched an investigation.

On Wednesday evening, investigators said the goal is to "determine the specific cause of death and the circumstances surrounding it, as well as to rule out any possible foul play."

Aiwanger had been feeling unwell on Wednesday, German tabloid Bild reported.

He had been scheduled to deliver a speech at the opening of his party's Berlin office, but canceled his appearance due to health reasons, German news agency dpa reported.

Aiwanger's body was found on Wednesday evening at the "Neuer Fritz" Hotel on Friedrichstrasse in Berlin-Mitte, according to Bild.

Bavarian state premier, the Christian Social Union's Markus Söder, has said he is "deeply shocked" by the news of his deputy's death.

"The sudden death of Hubert Aiwanger is an absolute tragedy," Söder said in a post on X.

"Our thoughts are now first and foremost with his family and his children. We mourn with them and pray for them."

Born in Lower Bavaria in southern Germany, Aiwanger was elected to the Bavarian State Parliament in 2008 with the Free Voters and became their parliamentary group leader.

In 2018, his party formed a coalition with the center-right CSU, the Bavarian sister party of the ruling Christian Democratic Union (CDU). Aiwanger held the role of the state's Minister of Economic Affairs and deputy state premier.

Aiwanger was the face of the Free Voters in Bavaria and across Germany, and was known for his combative approach.

He was unable to replicate his political success at the state level, however, as the Free Voters never won enough seats to enter the Bundestag, Germany's federal parliament.

In the weeks before state elections in 2023, Aiwanger became embroiled in a national scandal when the Süddeutsche Zeitung reported that he had written an antisemitic leaflet during high school in the 1980s, which included references to Nazi-era atrocities such as Auschwitz.

He is survived by two children. He was not married but lived with Tanja Schweiger, a district administrator for the Free Voters.

#113. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#112. Trump Pushes Venezuela for Agreement Allowing U.S. Troops to Stay

#111. Democrats take House and Senate from Republicans in US midterm rout

Democrats will control both chambers of Congress after flipping seats in Republican strongholds across the US in an election seen as a repudiation of President Donald Trump.

By late Tuesday night, it was clear that Democrats had succeeded in turning the 2026 US midterm elections into the so-called "blue wave" that had been predicted by pollsters.

But by Wednesday morning, the scale of the victory had turned into a "blue tsunami," surprising even the most optimistic political pundits.

In January, Democrats are set to have a commanding majority of at least 237 to 139 in the House and a 54 to 44 advantage in the Senate.

Democratic candidates flipped seven highly competitive Senate seats, including in what have for decades been reliably Republican districts in "red" states.

Texas voted in James Talarico as its first Democratic Senator since 1988, while electing a Democratic governor, Gina Hinojosa, for the first time in over 30 years.

The heartland state of Kansas, where farmers have been hit by high diesel prices and tariffs, will send a Democrat, Adam Hamilton, to the US Senate for the first time in over 90 years.

Other surprise wins in Republican strongholds included Iowa, South Carolina, and Ohio.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

According to data analyzed by the New York Times, the 2026 Democrats came in at a 13 point margin of victory over the Republicans for the popular vote in the House, the largest such margin since 1976 seen in the wake of the Watergate scandal that shattered the presidency of Republican Richard Nixon two years prior.

Democrats also saw large gains in almost every voter group compared with the 2024 presidential election that put Trump in the White House, indicating the breadth of the political swing away from Republicans.

The full scale of the Democrat majority in both chambers may not be known until later Wednesday or Thursday as some races are still too close to call, or take longer to count ballots.

The midterms were seen as a referendum on President Donald Trump, who had historically low approval ratings of around 38% ahead of election day.

Trump had campaigned relentlessly in the weeks leading up to the election, in what often turned into meandering speeches to dwindling crowds.

Democrats were able to capitalize on Trump's unpopular policies like the war in Iran and ensuing record-high fuel costs, which compounded a growing affordability crisis while the president was seen as enriching himself.

On Wednesday, Democratic House leader Hakeem Jeffries said his party was focusing "relentlessly on affordability and on accountability."

"The days of the House of Representatives functioning like a wholly-owned subsidiary of the Trump administration are over," said Jeffries, who could become the first Black speaker of the House in January.

Democrats say they are ready to launch a 100-day agenda to lower costs for fuel, groceries and healthcare, while conducting vast oversight and investigations into Trump and his administration.

When asked about the option of impeaching Trump or cabinet members for breaking laws while in office, Jeffries said Democrats were not focusing on that for now.

The margin of victory in the Senate is still not a two-thirds supermajority, with which Democrats alone could vote to convict in cases of impeachment, or override presidential vetoes.

Even as a lame duck president for the remaining two years of his term, Trump still has the power of the executive at his disposal, which comes with broad sway, especially in foreign policy.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Despite for months claiming that Democrats would cheat to win the election, Trump did not dispute the Republican blowout. Instead, he claimed that Republicans would have won had his name been on the ballot.

Nevertheless, Trump said he would be willing to work with Democrats, while at the same time promised to use veto power to block legislation.

In remarks on Wednesday outside of his ballroom construction site at the White House, Trump appeared to admit that the economic impacts of the Iran war had cost the Republican Party.

"I think it's largely true because it drove the gasoline up a little bit short term," Trump said.

Earlier Wednesday, Vice President JD Vance was more direct, saying the Iran war was the "reason we lost."

However, Trump spent the majority of the time in front of reporters on Wednesday talking about his ballroom project.

"It's going to be not only one of the biggest, but it'll be the most beautiful ballroom I think anywhere in the world," he said.

#110. Serbia detains police officers over alleged use of force against protesters

Interior ministry claims suspects tortured and intimidated protesters detained in garage of government compound

Serbian authorities detained nine active police officers and one former officer on Wednesday for ⁠allegedly using excessive force against detainees during anti-corruption protests last year in Belgrade, the country’s interior ministry ⁠said.

Anti-corruption protests swept ⁠across Serbia, ​a candidate for European Union membership, in 2025 and 2026 after 16 people died when a concrete canopy ⁠on a renovated railway station in the city of Novi Sad collapsed on 1 November 2024.

The protests ultimately ⁠resulted in the ouster of the populist Serbian Progressive Party (SNS), which is ​loyal to former president Aleksandar Vučić, in ‌an 25 October ‌snap parliamentary election by the leaderless Student List.

The ministry said in a ‌statement that the suspects, who served in an elite unit, tortured and intimidated several people detained in the garage of the government compound in downtown Belgrade on 14-15 August 2025.

“By the use of force and threats, (they) inflicted great pain and severe suffering on a larger ‌number of persons, intending to obtain information from them and intimidating and unlawfully punishing them,” the statement said.

The ministry provided ​only the initials of the officers and former officer who were detained. The news marked the first big arrests in connection with violence against the protesters who took to the streets over the past two years.

Police ⁠used teargas, crowd-control vehicles and stun grenades to disperse ​crowds during the ​protests.

Dozens of students and activists ​who took part in the protests accused police of ​excessive use of ‌force that ​resulted in injuries ​and psychological trauma.

Serbian students, activists, opposition politicians and rights watchdogs have accused Vučić and the SNS of political violence, pressure against media, ties to organised crime and rampant corruption. Vučić and his allies deny the accusations.

#109. The young politicians with African roots making history in the US midterms

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Watch: Abdul El-Sayed thanks supporters for backing 'unlikeliest of candidates'

It was an unlikely greeting to hear at a Democratic Party victory rally after the US midterm elections.

Abdul El-Sayed, 42, born to Egyptian immigrant parents, playfully addressed Vice-President JD Vance, who had campaigned against him but will now swear him into office in January as Michigan's new Senator.

The US-born Democrat spent much of his campaign fending off attacks from conservative media and President Donald Trump, who accused him of supporting the Muslim Brotherhood. El-Sayed rejected the allegations.

"My name is Abdul Rahman Mohammed El-Sayed," he said in an Egyptian Arabic accent to the cheering crowd at his victory rally. His Arabic Muslim name was a target for many of his opponents. He is set to become the first Muslim senator.

El-Sayed ran a social media-first campaign, with TikTok edits showing him speaking directly to young voters, tapping into Gen Z humour, and sporting friendship bracelets.

The former public health administrator's campaign centred on a progressive agenda, including Medicare for All, affordable housing and curbing corporate influence in politics. He has also been a vocal critic of US military aid to Israel.

But El-Sayed was not alone. From an Ethiopian-born Gen Z congresswoman to candidates with Nigerian heritage, several other politicians with African roots won in this year's midterms.

The elections, held halfway through a president's four-year term, decide who represents Americans in Congress, as well as in thousands of state and local offices.

Born in Addis Ababa, Melat Kiros' family moved to Denver when she was a baby

At 29, Melat Kiros became the first Gen Z woman elected to the US Congress.

Born in Ethiopia, she arrived in the US as a baby and grew up in Denver. She had never held elected office before defeating a 15-term incumbent in Colorado's 1st District in the Democrat primary before winning a landslide in Tuesday's election.

Kiros, like El-Sayed, campaigned on issues that have become central to young progressive voters: the cost of living, universal childcare and Medicare for All.

Endorsed by the Democratic Socialists of America, she has also called for abolishing Immigration and Customs Enforcement and ending US military aid to Israel.

Her outspoken criticism of Israel's war in Gaza has attracted controversy. Kiros has said her criticism is directed at the Israeli state, not Jewish people.

She was fired from a top New York law firm in 2023 after she refused to take down an open letter to dozens of law firms who had urged law school deans to crack down on antisemitism on campuses as students engaged in pro-Palestinian protests.

Kiros condemned antisemitism, but warned the law firms, including the one she worked for, against conflating "such bigotry with the geo-political question of Israel's legitimacy".

She said she started thinking more seriously about politics in general after being fired.

Adrian Boafo's parents moved from Ghana to the US

Adrian Boafo, meanwhile, a second-generation Ghanaian-American, will represent Maryland's 5th Congressional District after succeeding veteran Congressman Steny Hoyer, who retired after a political career spanning more than four decades.

The 32-year-old was born in Virginia and raised in Prince George's County, Maryland. His parents immigrated to the US from Ghana.

Boafo's route to Congress began in local politics. He served on the Bowie City Council before becoming a Maryland state delegate in 2023. He also worked for Hoyer before eventually winning the race to replace him.

His campaign focused on the cost of living, childcare and protecting federal workers, a major issue in a district close to Washington DC and home to thousands of government employees.

In his victory statement, Boafo said representing his constituents had "never felt more urgent", pointing to federal job cuts, rising costs and the Trump administration's immigration crackdown.

Among those returning to Congress is Somali-born Ilhan Omar, 44, who won a fifth term despite an ongoing federal investigation.

Federal agents searched her Washington home in October as part of an investigation into her immigration paperwork. Omar has denied wrongdoing and has not been charged with a crime.

First elected to Congress in 2018, Omar became the first Somali-American and one of the first Muslim women to serve in the chamber. She has also been a frequent target of President Trump, who has repeatedly criticised her and other Somali immigrants.

Trump has previously used derogatory language to describe some African countries, including Somalia. In October, his Yemeni-born adviser Amer Ghalib said the president would refrain from using such language.

Others made their breakthroughs in state and local politics.

Junior Ezeonu was born in Nigeria and moved to the US as a young child. By 27, he had already become Grand Prairie's youngest city council member before winning a seat in the Texas State House.

Then there is Carol Kazeem, 34, a Pennsylvania state legislator of Nigerian heritage who has retained her seat at District 159. At least four candidates with Nigerian heritage won in the 3 November midterms.

For some young Africans watching from across the Atlantic, these victories have offered a glimpse of what a new generation of voters could achieve at home.

On social media, users have shared the election results as a source of inspiration, particularly in Nigeria and Kenya, where major elections are due in 2027.

#107. Albanese government warned it is ‘sleepwalking into another robodebt scenario’ over use of algorithms and AI

Centre for Public Integrity report calls for audit and public register of all automated decision-making

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The federal government is “sleepwalking its way into another robodebt” scandal amid a lack of transparency and legislation around the use of algorithms and AI in decisions, the chief executive of the independent thinktank the Centre for Public Integrity said.

Dr Catherine Williams launched the centre’s “Computer Says No” report on Thursday, which examines the use of automated decision-making (ADM) tools by the government and calls for urgent reform.

The centre is concerned Australians may not know whether ADM has been used in decisions affecting them.

Among eight recommendations, the report calls for a mandatory audit and public register of all ADM use, and an independent oversight body with powers to monitor, audit and investigate the government over it.

Williams said the report was developed amid the centre’s increasing concern about the use of an algorithm as part of the government’s integrated assessment tool (IAT), which is used to assess the level of home support older Australians are entitled to.

The IAT has been found to be routinely under-assessing people’s needs and entitlements, with aged care assessors banned by the government from overriding incorrect algorithmic decisions.

“We have been watching the evidence emerge in relation to the aged care algorithm tool, and becoming increasingly concerned about what is clearly the lack of an appropriate and robust framework to govern use of ADM by the government,” Williams said.

“It feels as though we are sleepwalking into another robodebt scenario, and that’s why we’ve decided to put this work forward now, in the hope also that it can help inform any framework that the government does propose in relation to ADM.”

The attorney general’s department has been developing an ADM framework, with public consultation ending in January 2025.

The attorney general, Michelle Rowland, did not respond to questions about when a draft framework might be complete and made public but said the government “is committed to ensuring the safe and responsible use of ADM to deliver timely and efficient services for the Australian community”.

The framework will implement recommendations from the royal commission into robodebt, she said.

A report from the commonwealth ombudsman, published in October, found transparency recommendations from the robodebt royal commission had been ignored by the government in developing and rolling out the IAT.

Rowland did not respond to questions about whether any ADM framework would be retrospective but said it “will be designed to improve accuracy and consistency of administrative decisions, embed robust safeguards, including transparency obligations, and ensure that ADM is used lawfully, responsibly, and with appropriate human oversight”.

The framework will also lift productivity and help deliver faster, more reliable services, she said.

In September the independent MP Kate Chaney introduced a private member’s bill to establish the first legislated, mandatory framework for ADM use by the government.

She said the attorney general department’s consultation process for an ADM framework “finished almost two years ago”.

“I have met with the attorney general about it and they say ‘we’re working on it’. I think it’s really important that we keep the pressure up so that it doesn’t get dropped because it’s too hard.”

Chaney said government departments were making decisions about ADM use with little consistency or transparency.

“The government wants to find cost savings in the public service and automating decision-making can be a way of achieving those savings,” she said.

“But I think trust in the government is low. There does need to be transparency about when decisions are automated, and when it’s not appropriate for those to be automated, for people to be able to get a clear understanding of why the decision was made and to be really clear about what the pathways are to review that decision.

“This is what people expect of government.”

#106. Tears, celebrations and selfies: US midterms in pictures

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. Hugs, cheers and smiles broke out as the Democrats picked up a string of victories in Republican strongholds, delivering them the Senate and the House.

The win gives them control of Congress for the rest of Donald Trump's presidency.

Meanwhile, it was a challenging night for Republican supporters, after a succession of losses in reliably Republican states.

Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms defeated Republican Rick Jackson, a billionaire businessman endorsed by President Trump.

Bottoms becomes the first black woman elected to be governor of Georgia.

The state has not had a Democratic governor in more than 20 years.

Republican Senator Jon Husted shed a tear whilst surrounded by his family after losing his Ohio seat to Democrat Sherrod Brown.

Another Democrat victory was seen in Georgia where Senator Jon Ossoff was re-elected, giving him a second term.

Speculation surrounds Ossoff, considered a potential leading contender to run for the Democrats in the next presidential election in 2028.

James Talarico, the new US senator in Texas, and Gina Hinojosa, the state's new governor, toasted their successes.

In Portland, Maine, supporters reacted to the news that Democratic Senate candidate Troy Jackson won his seat. He beat Republican veteran Susan Collins who has been in the Senate for nearly 30 years.

Susan Collins was pictured looking at election results coming in on a laptop.

The mood was different for Republican supporters at a midterm election night watch party in Des Moines, Iowa. Republican Ashley Hinson lost her Senate seat to Democrat Josh Turek.

Democrat Chris Pappas made history as the first openly gay man elected to the Senate, beating his rival Republican Senator John Sununu.

Pappas celebrated his New Hampshire win on stage with his husband.

Attendees paused for thought after a watch party for Maine Republican Senate candidate Susan Collins, following her defeat by Democrat Troy Jackson.

In North Carolina, the Democrats secured the night's first flip of a Senate seat.

The state's former Democratic governor, Roy Cooper, won against Republican candidate Michael Whatley.

A supporter raised a jubilant fist to celebrate the North Carolina win.

In Michigan, Democrat Abdul El-Sayed will become the first Muslim US senator. He told voters: "You took a chance on the unlikeliest of candidates."

The mood was subdued amongst Republican supporters, after a string of defeats in reliably Republican strongholds.

#105. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#103. Endangered giant burrowing frog returns to wild after tadpoles rescued from ashes of black summer fires

Tadpoles collected from puddles in East Gippsland were transported to Melbourne zoo, where they were raised to metamorphosis and successfully bred in captivity for the first time

After the 2019-20 black summer bushfires, scientists in eastern Victoria stumbled across something they had been after for years: puddles of tadpoles from the southern giant burrowing frog, one of the state’s most cryptic amphibians.

The fires had torn through extensive areas in East Gippsland, sparking concerns for the survival of the frog, which is considered critically endangered in Victoria.

About 160 tadpoles were collected and transported to Melbourne zoo, where they were raised through to metamorphosis into frogs and successfully bred in captivity for the first time last year.

In a homecoming more than half a decade in the making, 53 of the original tadpoles – now male frogs about five years old – have been returned to their native habitat in a remote area of the state’s east.

The elusive frogs grow to roughly the size of a cricket ball in adulthood, and are believed to have long lifespans. Matt Clancy, a herpetologist at Snowline Ecology and an expert in the species, said that like other large frog species such as green tree frogs, giant burrowing frogs “could quite easily get over 20 years old”.

“Being such a large frog, you think they’d be easy to find, but they spend a lot of time buried underground in … sandy soils and forested environments,” he said.

“They will hunker down and not move at all until the next rains come and they emerge.”

The frogs surface to breed in ephemeral creeks and ponds only when conditions are right. Tadpoles can take three months to two years to metamorphose, usually emerging as juvenile frogs in spring or summer, Clancy said.

The 53 frogs were released immediately after big thunderstorms over the past several weeks at sites that had received 80 to 100mm of rain, he said.

Some of the released adults were not suitable for a captive breeding program run by Zoos Victoria. Ryan Moes, a specialist amphibian keeper at Zoos Victoria, said the team conducted genetic testing on all the tadpoles originally collected.

“Within a breeding program, you don’t want to have all siblings breeding together,” he said. “The best thing to do was just to raise those frogs up and release them back out … into the wild once they were healthy frogs.”

Each frog was tagged before release, and song meters have been installed at release sites to detect the frogs’ calls.

Moes said the breeding program, which may involve future tadpole collections, ultimately aimed to ensure “those wild populations stay healthy and robust, and that the frog doesn’t go extinct”.

Giant burrowing frogs were split into two distinct subspecies – northern and southern – in 2021. Populations have been severely affected by habitat destruction and extreme climate events.

More frequent and severe bushfires and droughts due to climate change, as well as less rainfall, were ongoing existential threats, Moes said.

There are only five known breeding sites of southern giant burrowing frog in Victoria.

#102. Anthony Zurcher: Democrats push deep into Trump heartland on historic night

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Watch: The key moments from the US midterm elections

Democrats have torn up Donald Trump's political map on a night that will send shockwaves through American politics for years to come.

With a string of major Senate victories in states that have been reliably Republican for decades - and in Kansas's case, nearly a century - a blue tidal wave crashed across the nation, sweeping Republicans out of power.

Halfway through President Donald Trump's second term, the Democrats now have decisive control of the House of Representatives and have wrested a majority back in the Senate.

They will be able to frustrate Trump's agenda, and launch investigations into his administration. A historic third impeachment attempt could also be on the cards.

Few imagined such an outcome was possible in the months after Trump returned to the White House with unified Republican control of Congress in January 2025.

As the public mood turned against him and his party, Democrats had hopes of retaking the House.

But they contemplated a Senate map that appeared unfriendly, populated by states Trump had won comfortably just two years ago.

Instead, Democrats rewrote the political equations with victories in Texas, Maine, Kansas, Ohio, Iowa and North and South Carolina.

In comments to the BBC's US partner CBS, Trump pledged to strike deals with congressional Democrats, adding that he didn't want to have to spend his final two years in office rejecting their legislation.

He somewhat undermined that sentiment later, posting a picture to Truth Social of his trademark red baseball hat with the word "veto" emblazoned on it.

The president went on to note that his name wasn't on the ballot this year, but if it had been Republicans would have won a decisive victory.

Trump had helped elevate many of those Republican candidates, however, and had urged voters to pretend he was up for re-election.

REACTION: Trump accepts Democrats did a 'good job' in election

THE BIG MOMENTS: Ecstatic scenes that greeted key results

In the Senate, Democrats flipped at least six seats, comfortably securing a majority.

Adam Hamilton won in Kansas - a state that last elected a Democrat to the Senate almost 100 years ago during the Great Depression.

James Talarico beat Republican Ken Paxton in Texas' Senate race, ending a 38-year Democratic losing streak in statewide elections there.

In South Carolina, Annie Andrews beat Darline Graham, who had been appointed to the seat after the death of her brother, Senator Lindsey Graham. Few analysts had that seat in play even a few months ago.

Five-term Republican Susan Collins conceded Maine's Senate election to Democrat Troy Jackson.

Paralympian Josh Turek flipped the Iowa Senate seat held by retiring Republican Senator Joni Ernst. Democrats also wrested from Republicans a Senate seat in Ohio, with Sherrod Brown's comeback victory over Jon Husted.

Meanwhile, Democrat Abdul El-Sayed has beaten Republican Mike Rogers in Michigan's bitterly fought Senate race.

Nor was that the end of the electoral carnage for Republicans.

Ruby-red Texas witnessed another seismic result in the governor's race.

Democratic challenger Gina Hinojosa ousted the incumbent Republican Greg Abbott, who was earlier this year seen as the prohibitive favourite.

Democrats also took governors' races in Iowa, Ohio and Georgia, which made history by electing the first black woman to such a post.

Meanwhile, the party has won a House majority for the first time since 2022.

They are projected to win 245 House seats and the Republicans 166 seats.

Democrats celebrate in Maine where the party won a crucial Senate race

A look at the key races offers plenty of reasons why.

Democrats flipped House seats in Texas, North Carolina, Pennsylvania, Iowa, Kentucky and Colorado, among others. And they were poised to make gains in Virginia and Indiana.

Black Democrats in the southern states turned out in record number, helping to carry their party to victory. And Hispanic voters, whose support had helped secure Trump's presidential victory in 2024, swung decisively back to the Democrats.

Democrats won all three House races in the overwhelmingly Hispanic congressional districts along the Texas-Mexico border.

And Hispanic voters helped Democrats hold House seats in Florida that the state's Republicans had sought to flip by redrawing the congressional district lines.

In fact, the congressional redistricting battles that had dominated headlines for months last year ended up being a bust for the Republicans.

In Texas, they had hoped their line-drawing would net five new seats for their party. But on Tuesday they may end up losing one seat.

If there is a silver lining for conservatives, Florida as a whole still appears to tilt to the right.

Republican Senator Ashley Moody narrowly won re-election, as did congressman Byron Donalds, who was elected the Sunshine State's first black governor.

And embattled Republicans were able to hold governorships in Vermont and New Hampshire. But the bright spots were few and far between.

Just two years ago, Trump won the presidency with 48% of the vote. He claimed an electoral mandate.

He then went about governing like it, pushing sometimes unpopular policies on trade and immigration, and launching an Iran war that is opposed by most of the American public.

On Tuesday, voters gave him the kind of stinging rebuke that will not be easily forgotten.

#98. Everything you need to know from the US midterm elections

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Watch: Key moments from the US midterm election results

Democrats have won both the US Senate and House of Representatives, taking control of Congress for the rest of Donald Trump's presidency.

Projected results have provided the party with a number of things to celebrate - and rival Republicans some reasons to commiserate.

Here is everything you need to know about some of the big Democratic wins and some historic firsts.

Democrats have tallied up victories in states that were comfortably won by Trump two years ago in the presidential election.

With James Talarico winning a seat in the US Senate seat and Gina Hinojosa being elected state governor, Democrats scored two statewide victories in Texas - ending a string of defeats they have endured for almost two generations.

James Talarico, the new US senator in Texas, and Gina Hinojosa, the state's new governor, toast their successes

Talarico's supporters went beserk as the projected results arrived at an election-night party, wrote the BBC's Gary O'Donoghue. Attendees hugged, kissed and drank to a reality they dared not believe would come true.

Hinojosa became the first Latina to be elected governor of the state.

Among the other states where history was made by the Democrats were Kansas, where Adam Hamilton took a closely-fought Senate seat - giving the state its first Democratic senator for nearly a century.

LIVE UPDATES: All the latest developments

BOWEN: Trump's loss limits powers at home, but it will likely be different abroad

RESULTS: How America voted in maps and charts

EXPLAINER: Five reasons why Trump did so badly

ZURCHER: Midterm carnage weakens Trump but he still wields power for now

REACTION: Angry Republicans blame Trump for midterms 'disaster'

NEWSLETTER: Sign up for Anthony Zurcher's analysis in your inbox

Senior party figures who cheered the results included Hakeem Jeffries, the top Democrat in the House of Representatives, who is now poised to take over as the Speaker of the lower chamber of Congress.

"Today begins the great American comeback," he told a crowd of supporters.

If Jeffries' colleagues choose to keep him as the leader of their party's delegation in House, he will be the next Speaker, taking over from Republican Mike Johnson and becoming the first black person to fill that role.

Trump spoke throughout the evening to a number of US media outlets, striking a conciliatory tone and conceding that the Democrats did a "good job".

He said he would accept the results and that he was looking forward to working with the opposition party - albeit he would still have the power, as president, to veto legislation that Democrats advance through Congress.

Trump distanced himself from some of the Republican candidates who contested the election, saying: "In some cases they didn't like the candidates, and in some cases those candidates were not my friend."

He acknowledged that the US-Israel war on Iran, which has impacted consumer prices in the US, had affected the vote. Trump argued that high fuel prices meant voters could not see how well his economy was doing "as much as they should have", adding: "The gasoline was caused by Iran."

Listen to Americast: Democrats make historic shift in US politics

Abdul El-Sayed has become the first Muslim person elected to the US Senate - after claiming victory in Michigan in what was one of a handful of closely fought contests. His win means the seat stays in Democratic hands.

He defeated Republican opponent Mike Rogers, who received the surprise backing of El-Sayed's Democratic rival in the primary contest, Haley Stevens, who changed her endorsement at the eleventh hour.

The race was one of the bitterest of the midterms. Trump invoked 9/11 and even called El-Sayed a "jihadist" without citing any evidence, prompting a backlash and accusations of Islamophobia.

El-Sayed triumphed in one of the bitterest contests of the midterms

El-Sayed's supporters went wild when he took the stage to celebrate his claimed victory, wrote the BBC's Nada Tawfik. He said: "Up until right about now, you might have noticed I always say 'inshallah,' or 'God willing,' whenever the outcome of this race was mentioned."

He went on to say: "But today, tonight, we can turn that 'inshallah' into 'alhamdulillah'. For the right-wing ops, that just means 'praise be to God.'"

The defeat of Susan Collins, who has served as the Republican senator for Maine for three decades, was one indicator of the scale of the Republican losses.

She was felled by Democratic candidate Troy Jackson, a logger who sought to point to his background to persuade blue-collar Mainers to vote for him.

In a statement reported by US media, Collins conceded defeat and said it had been the "honour of my life" to serve the state for 30 years.

The BBC's Helena Humphrey described a sombre atmosphere at Collins's watch party in the city of Maine - where the song Closing Time was played.

There was a sombre atmosphere at the watch party for Republican Susan Collins

Collins entered the race as one of the most vulnerable incumbent Republicans seeking re-election, and found that her tactic to distance herself from Trump did not work.

Ashley Hinson suffered the same fate in Iowa, losing that closely-fought Senate seat to Josh Turek. Hinson had slightly distanced herself from the president, urging him to end the war with Iran and breaking with him on easing beef tariffs, while at the same time defending Trump's broader tariff policies as important.

Among the other firsts of the night were in Florida, where Republican Byron Donalds has become the state's first black governor, following a hearty endorsement from Trump.

Donalds, who will take over the job from Ron DeSantis, said his state was the "envy of the nation", and that he would work to make the state more affordable.

The result was one of the few high points of an otherwise difficult evening for the Republicans.

A win for Bryon Donalds in Florida was one of the few high points for Republicans

In the neighbouring state of Georgia, Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms, the former mayor of Atlanta, won the governor's race, becoming the first black governor and first woman to be elected to the state's highest office.

The BBC's Carl Nasman described ecstatic scenes as the race was projected blue. Bottoms defeated Republican Rick Jackson, a billionaire businessman and healthcare executive. The state has also not had a Democratic governor for more than 20 years.

Elsewhere, Democrat Chris Pappas became the first openly gay man elected to the Senate. He celebrated on stage alongside his husband in New Hampshire.

Pappas defeated former Republican Senator John Sununu in the race, whom he thanked on Tuesday, saying: "We all owe John Sununu a big debt of gratitude for his public service for the people of New Hampshire."

Watch: "I am ready to fight" - Senator-elect Chris Pappas celebrates New Hampshire win

In Colorado, Melat Kiros became the first woman elected to Congress from Gen Z - meaning those who were born between 1997 and 2012.

#95. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#92. How America voted in maps and charts

Americans are electing members of Congress, which is made up of the House of Representatives and the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election. In the Senate, only 35 of 100 seats are being contested because senators serve six-year terms. This results tracker shows both the races being decided now and the 65 seats that are not up for election this year - 34 held by Democrats and 31 by Republicans.

Watch: Key moments from the US midterm election results

It's been an extraordinary night for the Democrats in the US midterms.

They have taken control of the Senate and the House of Representatives too.

The results in the Senate are beyond what the Democrats would have expected. North Carolina was a likely pick-up but the previously deep red state of South Carolina was a huge shock and Kansas had not returned a Democratic senator since 1932.

Texas, Iowa, Ohio and Maine also turned blue and the Democrats held Michigan. There are two seats remaining, Alaska and Mississippi, both are on a knife edge.

The shift from Republican to Democrat happened across the country. Some of the biggest swings were in Texas where the Democrats took the Senate seat and the governor.

The BBC's partner CBS News projected the Democrats would win the House just after 03:00 GMT on Wednesday (22:00 EST Tuesday).

Now it's a question of how big that win will be as counting continues.

Two significant seats are Florida 14 and Florida 25 which the Democrats have held. This is a blow to the Republicans' attempt to redraw the boundaries in their favour.

The redistricting saw both districts move from having a majority of 2024 Kamala Harris voters to having a majority of people who voted for Donald Trump.

Florida 22, which was also redrawn to favour the Republicans, saw a victory for the Democrats too.

A huge poll of voters suggests women turned out in numbers and most voted Democrat. Men on the other hand were evenly split between the two main parties.

Hispanic voters look to be one of the key groups in the elections in Florida. The poll suggests this group backed the Democratic Senate candidate Angie Nixon by 55% to 44% for the Republican Ashley Moody.

This is a turnaround from the estimated 55% of Hispanic voters who backed Donald Trump in 2024.

The poll also asked what issues voters were most concerned about. It suggests that across the country the economy was the biggest issue, followed by the state of democracy.

When voters were asked if Donald Trump's policies impacted economic conditions, the poll suggests that two-thirds believe they had made things worse.
